| D163 | **Superseded by D196**, which returns the pin to `3.3.0` after finding this row's premise environment-specific, and **reaffirmed by D227** on the separate ground that a code-review remediation cannot amend the frozen plan it is reviewed against; the advisories below remain correct and are escalated as a tracked item. **Supersedes D100 and D125.** Raise `python-jose[cryptography]` from `3.3.0` to **`3.5.0`** in `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, and move the security-relevance note in `backend/tests/test_dependency_closure.py` with it. | (a) Keep `3.3.0` and record both CVEs as unreachable from this suite — what D100 decided. (b) Pin `3.4.0`, the first release carrying both fixes. (c) Keep `3.3.0` in the manifest and install `3.3.0` into `.venv-backend`, so D101's gate agrees at the vulnerable version. (d) Leave the manifest alone and add a constraints file that overrides it. | D100's premise is measured false: the warmed environment supplies **3.5.0**, not the `3.3.0` that row asserted, so D101's gate was already failing — `python-jose is pinned to 3.3.0 in backend/requirements-dev.txt but 3.5.0 is installed` — and the backend suite was red before any change here. (a) is therefore not available at all without also downgrading the environment, which is (c): deliberately installing a distribution affected by CVE-2024-33663 (algorithm and key confusion, an OpenSSH ECDSA key accepted where an HMAC secret is expected) and CVE-2024-33664 (a compressed-JWE decompression bomb) in order to satisfy a gate whose purpose is to keep the declared and installed graphs identical. (b) carries the fixes but is neither what is installed nor the line's current release, so it would leave the gate red for the same reason. `3.5.0` supports Python 3.9, so the `ci.yml` runtime ceiling is intact, and the suite already ran green against it — 411 passed — with `backend/tests/unit/test_core_security.py` constraining `jwt.decode` to `algorithms=["HS256"]` with an explicit key, so no assertion in the suite depends on the jose patch level. | Diverges from AAP §0.6.1, which declares `3.3.0`, and is escalated as such in the resolution report rather than presented as compliant: a version the AAP fixed is raised without the AAP being amended. Neither CVE is reachable from any test here — the suite never decrypts a JWE and never loads an OpenSSH ECDSA key — so the change protects future production use of this dependency rather than a current test, which is why the review classified it as supply chain rather than as a live exploit. Any future rebuild must resolve `3.5.0` exactly or D101's gate fails the run, naming both versions; that is the intended signal and the reason the pin is not expressed as a range. |
| D164 | **Extends D102, and is extended in turn by D421**, which names the second half of the `Host`-handling pair — GHSA-jp82-jpqv-5vv3 / CVE-2026-54282, an unvalidated request path poisoning `request.url.hostname` — and the full seven-advisory set the pin sits inside. **Extends D102.** Name the *second* starlette advisory — GHSA-86qp-5c8j-p5mr / CVE-2026-48710, `Host`-header handling, affecting every release through 1.0.0 — in the manifest marker as **reachable**, and cover it with a twenty-case `Host` census in `backend/tests/integration/test_route_surface.py` that pins the reflection, the absence of any refusal, the contrast against `TestClient`'s trusted default host, and the absence of `TrustedHostMiddleware` from `app.user_middleware`. Do **not** install the mitigation. | (a) Install `TrustedHostMiddleware` in `app/main.py` with an allow-list. (b) Upgrade starlette, which means upgrading fastapi. (c) Assert the reflection is *absent*, i.e. write the test the mitigated stack would pass, and leave it failing as a known gap. (d) Record the advisory in the manifest and add no test, since the exposure is a dependency's rather than the suite's. | The exposure is real and was measured rather than inferred: `POST /tweets/` with `Host: attacker.example` answers `307` with `location: http://attacker.example/tweets`, and eight further spellings are reflected verbatim — with a port, with userinfo, with an extra path segment (`attacker.example/evil` → `http://attacker.example/evil/tweets`), with a scheme, empty (`http:///tweets`), containing a space (percent-encoded), a bracketed IPv6 literal, and a comma-joined pair. Not one is refused, so on a public unauthenticated path the redirect is an open-redirect primitive. (a) and (b) are both production changes outside the two touches AAP §0.11.3 authorizes — (a) adds middleware to `app/main.py` and would also change the CORS-only `user_middleware` census three other tests rest on; (b) breaks the `fastapi==0.95.2` pin that `TestClient` under `httpx==0.27.2` is calibrated against, which is D102's standing reason. (c) is forbidden outright by the requirement to assert current behaviour, and a permanently red test is not a gate. (d) leaves the suite unable to notice if the behaviour ever changes. So the reachable half of the advisory is documented on the pin, pinned by tests, and left open — the honest disposition, and the one the review asked for. | The suite now asserts a *vulnerable* behaviour, which reads as endorsement to someone who skims it. Mitigated in three places: each test's docstring says it asserts the behaviour and claims no mitigation, the manifest marker says the mitigation needs an authorized change, and `test_application_installs_no_host_validating_middleware` asserts the middleware set by equality — so the day a validator is installed, that test and the census both fail and force this row to be revisited rather than silently passing. The residual exposure itself is unchanged by anything here and is escalated in the resolution report as still open. Negative validation performed: repointing `HOSTILE_HOST_LOCATION` at `http://testserver/tweets` and adding `TrustedHostMiddleware` to the expected middleware set failed exactly three cases; both perturbations were reverted. |
| D165 | Delete the `package-lock.json` rule from `.gitignore` and replace it with a comment saying no such rule may exist, rather than narrowing it to one path or leaving it in place. | (a) Narrow it to `frontend/package-lock.json`. (b) Keep it and add a `!e2e/package-lock.json` negation. (c) Keep it, on the grounds that no lockfile is committed anyway so the rule is inert. | The rule was inert only in the sense that nothing had tried: it is unanchored, so it suppressed a lockfile at every depth, and `npm ci` exits rather than installing when no lockfile is present — so the rule made the repository's frozen-install path unreachable by construction, for `frontend` and `e2e` alike. It was also outside what AAP §0.5.4 specifies for this file, which enumerates test artifacts only, and a lockfile is not a test artifact. (a) and (b) keep a suppression whose only effect is to hide the one file that would fix a supply-chain gap. The generated artifacts stay ignored under their own rules, which is unchanged. | Removing the rule does not by itself produce a lockfile, so the supply-chain gap D166 addresses is closed by pins rather than by hashes; that residual is D166's, not this row's. A future `npm install` run now leaves an untracked `package-lock.json` visible in `git status` where it was previously invisible — which is the intended signal, and `frontend/src/test-utils/dependency-closure.test.ts` fails if the rule ever returns. |
| D166 | **[Superseded by D171 — the seventeen pins are restored to the baseline ranges, because AAP §0.5.3 does not admit the trade this row made.]** **Supersedes the no-lockfile half of D93.** Close the supply-chain finding by **exact-pinning every direct dependency** of `frontend/package.json` — both the `dependencies` block and the pre-existing `devDependencies`, seventeen entries that carried caret ranges — to the versions already installed and already exercised, rather than by committing a lockfile. | (a) Commit `frontend/package-lock.json` and `e2e/package-lock.json` and switch CI to `npm ci`, which is the review's primary resolution and the stronger mechanism. (b) Pin the `devDependencies` only, staying literally inside AAP §0.8.1's "devDependencies and test scripts only", and record `axios` as an accepted risk. (c) Add an `overrides` block instead of editing the declarations. (d) Change nothing and carry the whole finding as a suggested next task, as D93 did. | (a) is excluded on two independent grounds, and the review anticipates the outcome — "If a lockfile remains forbidden, exact-pin all direct packages". First, AAP §0.6.2 and §0.10.5-C7 place `frontend/package-lock.json` out of scope in as many words, and a frozen AAP exclusion outranks a finding's suggested remedy. Second, half of (a) is not even reachable from this checkpoint: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` belongs to a later index and is still pristine, so `npm ci` could not be adopted, and a committed lockfile generated here — under npm 10 on Node 22, against a matrix declaring Node 16 — could not be validated by the run that produced it. (b) leaves the finding open at exactly the package the review names as the concrete exploit: `axios ^1.4.0` admitted the compromised `1.14.1`, whose postinstall delivered a remote-access trojan, and `^1.4.0` still admits it. (c) hides the real specifier behind a second mechanism, so the declaration a reader sees stays wrong. Every pin equals the version `npm ls --depth=0` already reports, so no dependency's resolved version changes and nothing about the application's behaviour changes — the manifest is made to describe the tree the suite was actually validated against. The same shape is already in the tree and already accepted: `e2e/package.json` exact-pins all three of its direct dependencies, and D109 exact-pinned `react-router-dom` for a security reason. | Diverges from AAP §0.8.1 and §0.5.3, which scope this file to devDependencies and test scripts and say "nothing else in the file changes" — the seven `dependencies` entries are edited, and that is escalated in the resolution report rather than presented as compliant. The residual the pins do not close is the **transitive** graph: exact direct pins constrain the top level only, so a clean install still resolves transitive dependencies freely and no integrity hash is verified anywhere. That is weaker than a lockfile, the review says so, and it is carried as a suggested next task rather than described as equivalent. Pins also stop tracking upstream patches, so a security fix now needs a manifest edit — deliberate, since an unattended upgrade is the exposure being closed, and D167's gate makes any drift between manifest and tree fail a run. |
| D167 | **[Superseded by D172 — the gate now asserts a two-set partition rather than one exact-pin rule.]** Enforce D166 with a **Jest gate**, `frontend/src/test-utils/dependency-closure.test.ts`, that asserts every direct dependency of both npm manifests is an exact version, that every `frontend` pin equals the version in `frontend/node_modules`, and that `.gitignore` carries no active `package-lock.json` rule. | (a) Trust the manifest, since a reviewer would notice a caret. (b) `npm ls --depth=0` in CI. (c) `npm audit` or a Dependabot policy. (d) A pre-commit hook. | It is the frontend counterpart of D101, adopted for the same measured reason: the backend manifest and its environment had silently disagreed, `pip check` reported nothing, and only a test caught it. `npm ls` reports an unsatisfied *range*, so it would have said nothing at all about the caret ranges this finding is about — a tree resolved from `^1.4.0` to a compromised `1.14.1` satisfies `npm ls` perfectly. (b) is also unavailable inside this checkpoint, since `ci.yml` is a later index. (c) answers a different question, needs the network and gates nothing locally. (d) can be bypassed and does not run in CI. A test fails in the same run, with the same output, as the suite it protects. | The gate reads three files off disk from inside a jsdom suite, which is unusual for a test and is documented as such in the module docstring. It deliberately asserts nothing about the transitive tree — there is no integrity-hashed reference to assert against, and inventing one would be theatre — and nothing about `e2e/node_modules`, which belongs to the Playwright runner; only that manifest's shape is checked. It also couples the frontend suite to `e2e/package.json` existing: if that file is ever removed the gate fails loudly rather than silently skipping, which is the intended direction. Negative validation performed: reintroducing `"axios": "^1.19.0"`, mis-declaring `chart.js` as `4.4.0`, and re-adding the `package-lock.json` ignore failed exactly four cases; all three perturbations were reverted. |
| D168 | Assert a traversal-bearing tweet id at **both** observation points in `frontend/src/services/api.test.ts` — keep the mocked-`axios` case that pins the string `api.ts` interpolated, and add a real-adapter case that pins what the network receives: pathname `/undefined/tweets/7`, bound route parameter `7`, and a response body carrying `tweet_id` `7`. | (a) Keep only the pre-adapter assertion, as reviewed. (b) Replace it with the network assertion. (c) Encode the id in the test before passing it, so the segment survives. (d) Fix `api.ts` to `encodeURIComponent` the id. | The pre-adapter assertion is true and worth keeping, but on its own it *misleads*: with `axios` mocked no URL parser runs, so a reader concludes `tweet 1/../7` reaches the backend as the final path segment. It does not — under the URL Standard the `..` segment removes the preceding one, so the request lands on `/undefined/tweets/7`, `7` is what a route pattern binds, and the caller is handed the record for an id it never asked for, with nothing on either side signalling the substitution. Both facts matter and neither implies the other, so (a) and (b) each drop half the behaviour. (c) would be the test repairing the input on production's behalf, which is exactly the concealment the review warned against, and (d) is a production change: `api.ts` is outside the two authorized touches, and the requirement is to assert current behaviour rather than to fix it. | The suite now documents a path-confusion behaviour without fixing it; the fix belongs to whoever is authorized to change `api.ts`, and it is escalated in the resolution report rather than left implicit in a green test. The network case depends on the default `*/tweets/:tweetId` handler and on `pathParams` being recorded, so it is coupled to the request log in `src/test-utils/handlers.ts` — deliberately, since that log is the only place the bound parameter is observable without a live backend. Negative validation performed: repointing the pathname oracle at `/undefined/tweets/tweet%201/../7` and the parameter oracle at the raw id failed the case, reporting the normalised path as received; both perturbations were reverted. |

## §19 Code-review remediation — the client/mock/backend route seam and the manifest change boundary

Scope: the three findings raised against the seam between the frontend HTTP mocks, the service suites that
assert through them, and the routes the backend actually declares — one critical (the mock answered a
*routed* outcome for a path the application does not route), one major (the collection response model
collapsed fastapi's validation stage into its execution stage, and reported one failing parameter where
fastapi reports all of them) — together with the major AAP-compliance finding that `frontend/package.json`
had been changed outside the surface the frozen plan authorizes.

Every outcome named below was measured in this repository against the assembled application through
`TestClient`, once with `app.db.firestore.get_db` left alone and once with it overridden, and against the
frontend through msw 1.3.5 under Jest 29 + jsdom on Node 22.23.1. `docs/testing/TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §A
carries the resulting table and §D row X16 the divergence it turns on.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D169 | **Supersedes the pattern half of D103/D136 and the layer-2 half of D1.** Register every msw pattern under a **fully spelled-out base path prefix** — origin, then `/undefined` or nothing, then the route path — with no wildcard segment anywhere, and split layer 2 into one factory per base URL: `unsetBaseBackendHandlers()` for the paths the application emits today and `configuredBaseBackendHandlers()` for the backend's own paths. The five `currentBehavior*` / `dependencyOverridden*` factories are removed. | (a) Keep the `*/…` prefix and only correct the statuses it answers. (b) Keep the wildcard for the isolation layer and use exact paths for layer 2 alone. (c) Keep one layer-2 set and give it a `base` option instead of splitting it. (d) Normalise the prefix away inside the resolver and keep the pattern exact. | The wildcard was not merely cosmetic: it **absorbed** the `/undefined` segment, so a request to a path the backend does not route was matched by a handler named for the route it does not reach, and then answered `500` — the status that path would produce only if the prefix were not there. That is a false integration claim of exactly the kind the two layers exist to prevent, and the review found it as one. (a) leaves the mechanism that produced it in place: the same handler would still answer a request to `/api/v1/tweets` or any other mis-set base. (b) keeps a silent success available to the layer whose whole purpose is to be recognisable as a fixture, and leaves the isolation layer following a client onto a path it was never measured against. (c) hides the discriminator that decides the answer inside an argument, where a reader of the call site cannot see it; the base is the first thing that decides the outcome, so it belongs in the name. (d) is the same concealment one level down. Naming the prefix also converts a mis-set base URL from a silent `200` into a ledgered unhandled request, which is what the security finding asked for. | Each layer now answers exactly one base, so a suite that configures the base URL and forgets to install the matching set gets an unhandled-request failure rather than a fixture — intended, and the message names the origin, the URL and the remedy. Six exported factories became two, so any future caller of the old names fails to compile rather than silently changing meaning; only the three service suites used them, and all three are updated in the same change. The isolation layer no longer answers a configured base at all, which is deliberate: a suite wanting a nominal success there registers its own handler for that exact URL, as `setup-jest.test.ts` already does. |
| D170 | **Supersedes D8 and D137.** Model `GET /tweets` as the three stages fastapi applies, in that order — routing, then query coercion, then endpoint execution — and return **every** parameter that fails coercion, in the order the endpoint declares them, rather than the first. The `422` is therefore returned under both dependency dispositions, and the `dependencyOverridden` option changes only what a request that *passes* coercion receives. | (a) Keep D137's split, where the 422 lived only in the dependency-overridden factory. (b) Keep reporting the first failing parameter, since no production caller sends more than one malformed value. (c) Report the failures in query-string order. (d) Assert the multi-failure body in the backend suite only and leave the mock single-failure. | D137's split was measured against an overridden dependency only, and that is what hid the ordering: `GET /tweets?page=5&limit=undefined` answers `422` with the **real** `get_db` too, because `solve_dependencies` coerces the declared parameters before the endpoint body runs, so tying the 422 to the override misattributed a framework behaviour to a test fixture. (b) is a claim about callers, not about the route, and the mock is a model of the route; it also made the body's shape wrong in a way no caller could reveal, which is how it survived review. (c) is measurably false: with `limit` sent first and `skip` second, fastapi still reports `skip` then `limit`, because the order is the declaration order on `routes/tweets.py` line 12 — asserted in both suites. (d) leaves the frontend mock able to disagree with the backend it claims to model, which is the finding. | The coercion rule and the ordering are a reimplementation of pydantic v1's behaviour in TypeScript, and could drift from it. Bounded three ways: it governs a mock only; `backend/tests/integration/test_http_tweets.py` asserts the same single- and multi-parameter bodies against the real route, including the order-independence; and `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.test.ts` asserts the mock's own output, so a drift fails a test rather than propagating. Negative validation performed: truncating the failing-parameter list to its first entry failed exactly the declaration-order case, and the perturbation was reverted. |
| D171 | **Supersedes D166.** Restore all seventeen `frontend/package.json` declarations D166 pinned — the seven `dependencies` entries and the ten pre-existing `devDependencies` — to the caret ranges the repository declared at the frozen baseline, keeping exact pins only for the eleven test devDependencies AAP §0.5.3 authorizes (`jest` and `ts-jest` among them, which that section pins by name). | (a) Keep D166's pins and carry the AAP divergence as escalated, which is what D166 chose. (b) Pin only the `devDependencies` and restore the seven runtime ones. (c) Move the pins into an `overrides` block so the declarations read as the baseline while resolution stays pinned. (d) Commit a lockfile instead, which is the mechanism both rows really want. | D166 weighed a supply-chain exposure against a scope boundary and chose the exposure; the review's finding is that the boundary is not the kind that trades. AAP §0.5.3 says of this file, in as many words, that nothing other than test devDependencies and test scripts changes, and §0.10 makes the frozen plan the authority a finding's suggested remedy cannot outrank. `axios` in particular is part of the very seam under review, so pinning it here also changed the transport the reviewed suites run on. (b) is the same violation at a smaller size — the ten pre-existing development declarations are equally outside the surface. (c) is worse than either: the effective version would no longer be the one the file appears to declare. (d) remains excluded by AAP §0.6.2 and §0.10.5-C7 and is unreachable from this checkpoint anyway, since `ci.yml` is a later index and could not adopt `npm ci`. Restoring costs nothing operationally: every installed version already satisfies its restored range, `npm ls --depth=0` exits 0 with no invalid entry, and no reinstall is needed. | The exposure D166 named is reopened at the top level: `axios ^1.4.0` again admits the compromised `1.14.1`, and the same is true of the other sixteen ranges. That is a real residual and it is escalated as such rather than presented as closed — the closure needs either a committed lockfile or an amended plan, both of which sit with a human. D172's gate narrows the window it leaves: a declaration that drifts from the baseline now fails a test, so the ranges cannot be quietly re-pinned or quietly widened, and the eleven suite-owned pins remain exact and asserted against the installed tree. |
| D172 | **Supersedes D167.** Re-scope the npm gate in `frontend/src/test-utils/dependency-closure.test.ts` from "every direct dependency is an exact pin equal to the installed version" to a **two-set partition**: the eleven suite-owned entries must be exact, equal to the version the plan names and installed at exactly it; the seventeen entries outside the authorized surface must be byte-identical to the frozen baseline specifier and installed at a version that specifier admits. Three partition cases assert the union is exhaustive, disjoint and expressible in the two specifier forms the comparator handles. | (a) Delete the gate, since its premise changed with D171. (b) Keep the exact-pin gate and exempt the seventeen with a skip list. (c) Assert the seventeen only for existence, dropping the range check. (d) Import `semver` to evaluate the ranges properly. | The gate's purpose survives D171 — it exists so the declared and installed graphs cannot silently disagree — but its rule no longer fits half the file, and a test whose rule contradicts the manifest it guards fails for the wrong reason. (a) discards the half that still holds, the eleven pins this work owns. (b) is a skip list, which records nothing and drifts silently as entries are added. (c) drops the only remaining property the baseline set can carry: that the resolved release is one the declaration admits. (d) would add a dependency the manifests do not declare — `semver` is present only transitively through Jest — to evaluate two specifier forms a fifteen-line comparator handles exactly, with a partition case asserting no third form can appear. Making the baseline specifiers a **frozen table in the test** also turns the AAP change boundary itself into an assertion: the declarations this work may not touch now fail a test if anything touches them. | The comparator implements caret semantics for the two forms present rather than the whole of the npm range grammar; a future declaration in another form fails the partition case instead of being mis-evaluated, which is the intended direction. The baseline table duplicates seventeen strings that also live in the manifest, so adding a legitimate runtime dependency means updating both — deliberate friction, since that file is outside the authorized surface. Two residuals are named in the module docstring and not papered over: the transitive graph is still unasserted, and the baseline set admits a newer in-range release than the one the suite last ran against. Negative validation performed three ways: re-pinning `axios` to `1.19.0`, loosening `msw` to `^1.3.5`, and adding an ungoverned devDependency each failed exactly one case, and all three perturbations were reverted. |
| D173 | Add `frontend/src/test-utils/configured-base.ts` — one exported `importWithConfiguredBase(load)` that sets `REACT_APP_API_BASE_URL`, discards the module registry and then runs the caller's `import()` — and assert the seam under **both** base URLs rather than one. | (a) Assign the variable inside each test, without re-importing. (b) Repeat the assign-reset-import sequence inline in each suite that needs it. (c) Add a second `jest.config.js` project with the variable set globally. (d) Assert only the unset base, since that is what the application does today. | (a) cannot work and would look as though it did: `services/api.ts` line 5 reads the variable once at module scope, so a test that assigns it afterwards still observes the literal `undefined` base and would quietly assert the wrong disposition. (b) is the copy-pasted setup AAP §0.7.4 forbids, and the sequence has an order that matters — variable before reset, reset before import — which is exactly the kind of thing that drifts between three copies. (c) makes a whole-run global out of a per-test question and would split the suite in two for four assertions. (d) is the reason the reviewed shape had no honest place to put a route-level outcome: with only the unset base available, the `500` and `422` the routes really produce were unassertable, and the previous seam solved that by pretending the emitted path reached the route. Two bases, each asserted under its own handler set, is what makes both facts sayable without either standing in for the other. | The returned module is a fresh instance, so a `jest.spyOn` on the suite's own `axios` import does not affect it — stated in the module docstring, and the configured-base cases use msw rather than spies for that reason. The helper depends on Jest's module registry, so it is coupled to Jest rather than to the application; the alternative was coupling every suite to the same mechanism by hand. Cleanup is not this module's: the `afterEach` in `setup-jest.ts` already deletes the variable, and that hook stays its single owner. |
| D174 | **Refined by D201**, which adds the third oracle this row left missing — the unoverridden `GET /tweets` `500`, asserted server-side. Give the seam two direct oracles it did not have: `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.test.ts`, which asserts what each layer-2 factory answers, and a `/undefined` census in `backend/tests/integration/test_route_surface.py`, which asserts the same statuses and bodies against the real application. | (a) Leave the factories asserted only through the three service suites, as before. (b) Add the frontend contract suite only. (c) Add the backend census only. (d) Assert the mock against a recorded fixture of real responses instead of against literals. | A handler that answers the wrong status is invisible by construction: every suite built on it asserts the value it was handed, so the mock and the suites agree and the run is green — which is precisely how the reviewed defect reached a review boundary with 267 tests passing. Breaking that circularity needs an oracle the mock cannot influence, and the only one available is the application itself: (b) alone leaves the frontend asserting its own literals, and (c) alone leaves nothing failing when the mock drifts from them. Together, the backend census fixes the values and the frontend suite holds the mock to them, so a divergence fails a test on one side or the other. (d) would need a captured-response fixture, which is a third artifact to keep honest and one no test could regenerate offline. | Two suites now encode the same four statuses and bodies, so a genuine change in the application's behaviour fails cases in both — intended, and each names the other in a `@see`. The backend census asserts a `404` that is a *routing* failure while the same route file contains an unreachable handler `404`; both suites' docstrings say which is which, because conflating them is the one misreading these rows exist to prevent. |

---

## §20 Toolchain selections, test-style choices and boundaries the earlier sections left implicit

Scope: the decisions that shaped the suite but had not yet been written down — three version selections
forced by the documented runtime ceilings, four choices about test style that were made by following the
existing idiom rather than by adding a tool, the boundary drawn around the CI workflow and the repository
ignore rules, and the meta-decision about how this document itself is extended.

The runtime ceilings referred to below are the ones the repository documents, not the ones a given machine
happens to have: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` line 15 pins `python-version: [3.9]` and line 16 pins
`node-version: [16.x]`. `README.md`'s "Python 3.8+" is a lower bound and does not raise a ceiling.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D180 | Pin `msw` at **1.3.5** and write the interception layer against the 1.x `rest.*` and `setupServer` API, rather than adopting the 2.x line. | (a) `msw@2.x` with its `http.*` API. (b) A hand-rolled `axios` adapter or `jest.mock('axios')`. (c) `nock`, which intercepts at the Node http layer. | msw 2.0.0 and later declare `node>=18`, above the documented Node ceiling; the 1.x releases declare `node>=14`. Within jsdom the component and service suites need the *XHR* axios actually issues to be intercepted, which 1.x does, and which (c) does not see at all. (b) was refused for a stronger reason than convenience: mocking `axios` or `api.ts` deletes the behaviour several oracles exist to assert — the literal `undefined/` base URL, the query-string assembly and the response unwrapping (D152). | msw 1.x is a superseded major line, so no upstream fix reaches this suite. |
| D181 | **[The version boundary is corrected by D198; the choice of 1.44.1 stands.]** Select `@playwright/test` **1.44.1** as the E2E runner, on the ground that it is the most recent release still declaring `node>=16`. | (a) 1.45.3 or later, the current line. (b) 1.40.1 or 1.43.1, which also declare `node>=16`. (c) Cypress or WebdriverIO instead. | 1.40.1, 1.43.1 and 1.44.1 all declare `node>=16` while 1.45.3 and later declare `node>=18`, so 1.44.1 is the newest runner that satisfies the documented ceiling; (b) would forgo fixes for no gain. (c) would introduce a second, unfamiliar runner where Playwright's request interception is exactly what the fixture-driven specs need. This row records why the version was *chosen*; D111 records why it was *kept* once CVE-2025-59288 was raised against its browser downloader. | No upstream Playwright fix can reach this suite without a Node bump. |
| D182 | Pin `jest-junit` at **16.0.0** rather than 17.0.0. | (a) 17.0.0, the current major. (b) No JUnit reporter, relying on Jest's default output. (c) A different XML reporter such as `jest-sonar-reporter`. | 17.0.0 declares `node>=20`; 16.0.0 declares `node>=10.12.0` and sits inside the documented ceiling. (b) would leave the frontend as the only layer with no machine-readable result artifact, which is what the result-correlation obligation in §8 and §16 turns on. | A superseded major line, pinned by a runtime ceiling rather than by preference. |
| D183 | Mock with the standard library's `unittest.mock`, adding no `pytest-mock`. | (a) `pytest-mock` and its `mocker` fixture. (b) Both, choosing per suite. | The legacy suite already used `patch` and `MagicMock`, and the directive is to continue the established idiom rather than replace it. `mocker` wraps the same machinery, so it would add a dependency for no capability this suite lacks, and the patch-boundary convention in D149 is written in terms of `patch` used as a context manager and a decorator. | A patch started outside a `with` block must be stopped by hand, because there is no fixture teardown doing it. |
| D184 | Assert with plain `assert` and `pytest.raises`, adding no assertion library. | (a) `assertpy`, `hamcrest` or similar. (b) Keep `unittest.TestCase`'s `assertEqual` and `assertIn` family. | pytest rewrites plain asserts and reports both operands on failure, which is the diagnostic benefit an assertion library is bought for. (b) would also re-introduce class grouping, whose alphabetical method order was one of the determinism hazards this suite exists to remove — in the legacy suite `test_delete_tweet` ran before `test_get_tweet` and deleted the record it read. | Multi-condition assertions have to be split by hand to stay legible, since there is no fluent chain. |
| D185 | Add `__init__.py` under `backend/tests/`, `tests/unit/` and `tests/integration/` only, leave `backend/app/` a PEP 420 namespace tree, and give the new `routes` package no marker either. | (a) Mark `app/` as regular packages too. (b) Mark nothing and let pytest infer module identity from rootdir. (c) Add `routes/__init__.py` re-exporting the four routers. | The test tree needs unambiguous module identity because same-named modules sit in sibling directories; without markers, two `conftest`-adjacent trees collide. `app/` already imports correctly as a namespace tree, so marking it would be a production change with no testing benefit — and (c) is what D50 declined, since no other package in this repository carries a marker. | A future `app/` module whose name shadows a third-party distribution will resolve unpredictably, and no marker under `tests/` prevents that. |
| D186 | Treat the two dependency-install steps of `ci.yml` as **prerequisites of the test steps** and change them, while changing no other non-test step. | (a) Leave them alone, so the workflow keeps aborting before any test runs. (b) Add new install steps and leave the broken ones in place. (c) Rewrite the workflow. | The requirements direct installing `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, which is impossible without editing the step that installs Python dependencies: today it runs `pip install -r requirements.txt` against a file that does not exist anywhere in the repository (line 34) and the job dies there, before a single test. The Node step runs `npm ci` at a root that has neither a manifest nor a lockfile. A test step that cannot execute is not a test step, so its install prerequisite is in scope with it. | The install steps sit outside the literal phrase "test steps", so this is a documented reading of that boundary rather than a self-evident one. |
| D187 | **[Refined by D199 — those three steps are still byte-identical, but the test and reporting steps that follow them no longer depend on their conclusion, so the workflow being red no longer means the suite did not run.]** Leave `flake8 .`, `mypy .` and `npm run lint` exactly as they are, and scope the claim that CI is green to the backend test step, the frontend test step and the new `e2e` job. | (a) Make them pass: declare the linters and a type-checker, add configuration, and correct the sources. (b) Delete the three steps. (c) Add `continue-on-error: true` to them. | They are not test steps and they are broken independently of testing — no linter or type checker is declared anywhere in the repository, and the sources do not typecheck — so (a) needs both new configuration and production edits far outside the two authorized touches. (b) discards a signal the project should keep and (c) hides it. | The workflow's overall conclusion stays red until lint and type-check are addressed separately. |
| D188 | Place the Rule 4 executive deck in a new top-level `blitzy-deck/` directory. | (a) `docs/testing/`, beside this log. (b) `documentation/`, with the design corpus. (c) Omit the deck. | Rule 4 (Executive Presentation) names `blitzy-deck/references/blitzy-reveal-theme.css` as the canonical theme path, which fixes the directory rather than leaving it to taste. `documentation/` is reference-only and off-limits for writes, and a new top-level directory touches no protected path and no production runtime. See C4. | A top-level directory that exists to satisfy a rule rather than to serve the product. |
| D189 | Add a `.gitignore` covering generated test artifacts, build output and local environments only, attempting no broader repository hygiene. | (a) Keep the status quo of no `.gitignore` at all. (b) A comprehensive language-wide template. (c) Also ignore lockfiles. | The repository had no `.gitignore` anywhere, so merely probing imports left untracked `__pycache__` directories behind, and the three suites add coverage, report and dependency trees on top. (b) would ignore paths nobody has examined, which is how a source file goes missing. (c) is refused outright, and the file carries a comment where the rule would otherwise sit — see D159 and D165. | Untracked exposure outside test artifacts is unchanged from before. |
| D190 | Extend this log by **appending** sections and rows, preserving every shipped row verbatim, rather than reorganising it into a tidier taxonomy. | (a) Rewrite the document into a single planned outline. (b) Start a second decision log beside it. (c) Renumber the rows into contiguous ranges. | Twenty-eight files outside this log cite these row ids and section numbers, and forty-two link to it: `backend/tests/conftest.py` alone cites D25, D105, D106 and D121, `frontend/jest.config.js` cites D30-D37 and D146, `e2e/playwright.config.ts` cites D111, D112, D114, D123, D128 and D129, and `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md`, `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`, `e2e/README.md`, `frontend/TESTING.md`, both `.npmrc` files and seven backend test modules cite more. Rewriting or renumbering turns every one of them into a dangling reference and destroys the traceability web Rule 1 (Explainability) exists to create. (b) would split the single source of truth in two, which is the one thing that rule forbids by name. | Section order reflects the order decisions were taken, not a taxonomy, so a topic search needs the Document coverage index rather than a linear read. |
| D191 | State **eight** required `Settings` fields and a measured **28** `# HUMAN ASSISTANCE NEEDED` occurrences, where the plan's prose says nine of each. | (a) Repeat the plan's figures unchanged. (b) Give no counts at all. (c) Note the discrepancy without resolving it. | Both were re-measured against the tree. Exactly eight fields are declared without a default — `SECRET_KEY`, the four Twitter credentials, `OPENAI_API_KEY`, `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT` and `BIGQUERY_DATASET`, at `app/core/config.py` lines 7, 9-13, 15 and 19 — and the placeholder comment appears 28 times across 22 modules, 15 of them in 10 backend modules and 13 in 12 frontend ones, with one more in each of `ci.yml`, `cd.yml`, `docker-compose.yml` and `nginx.conf`. A document the whole change set treats as authoritative cannot repeat a number its own repository contradicts. | This log and the plan's prose disagree on two counts, which a reader comparing them will notice. |
| D192 | **Superseded by D208**, which performs all three steps once a browser became available, and by **D218**, **D234** and **D239**, which record the E2E browser run independently; D239 adds that both browser steps ran on a newer Node than the CI matrix declares. Record the E2E browser run and the Rule 4 deck render as **outstanding acceptance steps**, and rest no claim in this document on browser evidence. | (a) Report them as verified. (b) Drop the E2E layer and the deck rather than ship unproven artifacts. (c) Assert the harness works because its configuration is asserted. | Neither was performed when this programme was planned, so reporting them would put an unfalsifiable claim in the artifact every other file defers to. The harness is instead verified by means that leave evidence: its route table and module graph are covered by the suites named in §4 and §10, and its configuration is asserted directly rather than assumed. | The E2E layer's in-browser behaviour is unproven until that run happens. |
| D193 | Treat the coverage and JUnit artifacts the three runners already emit as this programme's **metrics surface**, and add no production metrics endpoint. | (a) Add a `/metrics` endpoint and a Prometheus client to `app/main.py`. (b) Add an OpenTelemetry exporter and span middleware. (c) Report to Codecov only, emitting no local artifact. | (a) and (b) are each an unauthorized production change *and* the implementation of a missing product feature, which the requirements forbid twice over. The artifacts are what the two Codecov steps in the workflow already consume, so extending them reuses instrumentation the project has instead of inventing a parallel one — which is what Rule 2 (Observability) asks for in its check-reuse-document clause. See C1 for the scoping argument and §8 for the correlation identifiers. | The metrics describe the test system, not the running application. |
| D194 | Record the production instrumentation gaps in §23 and leave every one of them unfixed. | (a) Implement structured logging, tracing and health checks in production. (b) Fix only the health check, as the smallest of them. (c) Leave them out of this log entirely. | Each would be production code outside the two authorized touches, and the `/health` route that `docker-compose.yml` and `cd.yml` both aim at does not exist — so "fixing" the health check means implementing an endpoint, which is a missing product feature by definition. Recording them turns a silent gap into a tracked item that Rule 3's onboarding documents surface as suggested next tasks. | A green test suite can be mistaken for operational readiness. |

---

## §24 Code-review remediation — the governance checkpoint: the coverage gate, the CI invocation, egress ownership, the deck and the acceptance evidence

Twenty-one findings were raised against this delivery at the governance checkpoint. Most were corrected as
facts and need no row here. The seventeen below are the choices a competent engineer could have made
differently, and three of them are **divergences from a literal reading of the frozen plan**, which Rule 1
makes a defect if left unexplained: D197 and D198 depart from the two commands AAP §0.5.4 spells out for the
`e2e` job, and D196 reverses a pin this log had already raised.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D195 | Add `backend/.coveragerc` carrying **only** `[report] precision = 2`, and assert both the kept and the removed behaviour in `backend/tests/test_coverage_gate.py`. | (a) Pass `--cov-precision=2` on every invocation. (b) Raise the threshold to 91 so the rounding band sits above the real bar. (c) Put `fail_under = 90` in `.coveragerc` as well. (d) Leave it: the measured total is 93.33%, comfortably clear. | `pytest-cov` compares `round(total, precision) < fail_under` and takes `precision` from the coverage config, defaulting to `0` — so `--cov-fail-under=90` exited **zero** on any total from 89.50 up while printing `FAIL`, which was reproduced directly (`--cov-fail-under=92 --cov-precision=0` on a 91.79% run: exit 0, output `FAIL`). A config file fixes it once for every invocation, including a developer's, where (a) fixes it only where someone remembers the flag. (b) moves the bar the proposal fixed at 90. (c) would gate the plan's own plain measurement command, because `pytest-cov` reads `fail_under` from the config when no flag is given. (d) leaves a gate that does not gate. | Two decimals still admit `[89.995, 90)`, which rounds to `90.00`. The band is asserted rather than hidden, and it at least keeps the exit status and the printed message from disagreeing; closing it entirely needs a precision the tooling does not offer. `.coveragerc` also declares no `[run] source`, so anyone adding one there would silently override the `--cov=` scoping the gate depends on. |
| D196 | **Supersedes D163.** Return `python-jose[cryptography]` to **`3.3.0`**, the version AAP §0.6.1 fixes, and install it. | (a) Keep `3.5.0`, which is what D163 decided. (b) Pin `3.4.0`, the first release carrying both fixes. (c) Keep the manifest at `3.3.0` and leave `3.5.0` installed, so the manifest and the tree disagree. (d) Add a constraints file overriding the manifest. | D163's reason was environmental, not architectural: the *warmed* platform environment supplied `3.5.0`, so D101's manifest-equals-installed gate was red unless the manifest moved. That premise does not hold in a clone that builds its own environment — this one did, and `3.3.0` installs cleanly on CPython 3.9.13 against `cryptography` 50.0.0, with `jose.jwt.encode` working. The plan is frozen and the review found the deviation, so aligning to it and escalating the exposure explicitly beats keeping a silent deviation. (b) is still not the pinned version. (c) breaks D101 by construction. (d) hides the pin from the one file that is supposed to state it. | `3.3.0` predates the fixes for CVE-2024-33663 (algorithm confusion with an ECDSA public key) and CVE-2024-33664 (JWE decryption resource consumption). Neither is reachable from this suite — no test decodes a token whose header it did not itself encode, and none decodes a compressed JWE — but the pin ships in the manifest a human will reuse. The exposure is named in `backend/requirements-dev.txt` beside the pin and carried into the suggested next tasks; raising it is a human decision, not a test-suite one. |
| D197 | **Diverges from AAP §0.5.4.** Run the E2E job as `npm test` with `working-directory: e2e`, rather than the plan's `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from the repository root. | (a) Use the plan's command verbatim. (b) Add a root `package.json` so a root invocation has a manifest. (c) Call `node e2e/node_modules/@playwright/test/cli.js` explicitly from the root. | The repository has no root manifest and no root `node_modules`, so the plan's command reaches `npx`, which resolves `playwright` **from the registry** when no local binary is found — an unpinned runner fetched at build time, which is exactly what D111 and D129 exist to prevent. `working-directory: e2e` makes `npm test` resolve `e2e/node_modules/.bin/playwright`, the pinned 1.44.1, with the config sitting beside it; the job's intent — install, provide a browser, run Playwright, upload the report — is unchanged. (b) adds a manifest for no other purpose. (c) works but hard-codes an internal path that a Playwright upgrade may move. | The workflow no longer matches the plan's text character for character, so a reader comparing the two will find a difference; this row is that difference's explanation. Verified locally: `npm test` from `e2e/` exits 0 at 11 passed / 1 skipped. **Never exercised on a hosted runner** — no workflow run exists, which §7.5 of the dashboard states rather than implies. |
| D198 | **Diverges from AAP §0.5.4.** Remove `playwright install --with-deps chromium` from the workflow and from `README.md`; instead resolve `${CHROME_BIN:-/usr/bin/google-chrome}`, fail closed on `test -x`, export it as `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`, and keep `npm run browsers:verify` as the download-free check. | (a) Keep the plan's install step. (b) Keep it but add `PLAYWRIGHT_SKIP_BROWSER_DOWNLOAD`. (c) Cache the browser between runs and install only on a cache miss. (d) Vendor the Chromium archive. | The install command is precisely the path CVE-2025-59288 concerns — the downloader does not verify the TLS chain of the host it fetches from — and D111 and D129 already removed every other route to it, so leaving it in the workflow and the onboarding document contradicted the policy the rest of the tree enforces. The remedy costs nothing here: `ubuntu-latest` ships Google Chrome, and the installed `playwright@1.44.1` package declares **no `scripts` key at all**, so `npm install` in `e2e` runs no postinstall that could fetch — making (b) unnecessary. `test -x` turns a missing browser into a named failure rather than a silent download. (c) still downloads on a miss. (d) puts ~130 MB in git. | The job now depends on the runner image providing Chrome at that path, which is true of `ubuntu-latest` today but is an image property rather than a declared dependency; if it changes the job fails closed with the path it expected, which is the intended failure mode. A developer on a fresh machine has no in-repo command that obtains a browser and must provision from a verified artifact first — the same residual D129 recorded, now stated in `README.md` too. |
| D199 | Key the backend egress guard's ownership registry on the endpoint tuple `(family, socket type, normalized protocol, normalized address, port)`, refcount it, and release it on `close`, on `detach` and on garbage collection. | (a) Keep the bare-integer port set. (b) Keep the port set but clear it between tests. (c) Drop the loopback allowance entirely and mock every socket. (d) Key on `(address, port)` only. | The port-only set discarded everything that makes an endpoint an endpoint: a TCP listener on 5000 authorized a **UDP** datagram to 5000, a bind on `127.0.0.1` authorized `127.0.0.2`, an entry was never removed so a closed port stayed authorized for the rest of the session, and every `AF_UNIX` socket was admitted unconditionally regardless of path. Each of those is a hole in the one guarantee the suite's no-network claim rests on. (b) fixes lifetime and nothing else. (c) breaks `TestClient` and `socketpair`, which the suite needs. (d) still lets a datagram ride a stream listener. | The guard is now more intricate, and intricacy in a fail-closed mechanism is itself a risk — answered with `backend/tests/unit/test_egress_guard.py`, 26 cases under an autouse leak guard that fails if either registry structure is left dirty, and with two performed-and-reverted negative validations: reinstating port-only authorization failed 7 cases, and removing the release patches failed 2 and errored 12. One deliberate widening remains: an owned bind to the *unspecified* address authorizes that family's loopback addresses on that exact port only. |
| D200 | Cover `app.db.firestore.get_db` directly, with a `firestore_client_constructor` fixture that patches the subject-bound `Client` name while leaving `get_db` itself in place, and broaden the module's isolation-fixture gate from one required fixture name to a set of admissible ones. | (a) Leave `get_db` uncovered and record it as a ceiling. (b) Patch `google.cloud.firestore.Client` at the library instead. (c) Call `get_db` with the autouse credential neutralizer alone. (d) Weaken the module's isolation gate instead of extending it. | `get_db` is a public function of a gated package, it is reachable, and it is safely isolatable — so calling it a ceiling would have been a coverage claim about the tests rather than about the code, and it left `app/db/firestore.py` at 88.89% with lines 8-10 the only gap. Patching the subject-bound name is the same boundary discipline the rest of the suite uses. (b) patches a shared library object other tests observe. (c) reaches a real constructor. (d) would let a future test in that module request no isolation at all. | The gate now accepts either of two fixtures, so it is a weaker structural check than "exactly this one name" — mitigated by the set being closed and named in one constant. Measured effect: `app/db/firestore.py` 88.89% → **100.00%** and the gated total 91.79% → **93.33%**. |
| D201 | **Refines D174.** Add the server-side oracle for the unoverridden `GET /tweets` `500`: leave `Depends(get_db)` in place, patch only the subject-bound `default` and `Client` names so the dependency resolves to `MagicMock(spec=google.cloud.firestore.Client)`, and assert the status, the body and the content type the frontend handler answers. | (a) Leave the frontend handler's `500` asserted only against itself, as D174 left it. (b) Override the dependency with a stand-in that lacks `query`. (c) Record the `500` as unverifiable server-side. (d) Assert the mock against a recorded fixture of a real response. | D174 gave the seam two oracles but the unoverridden `500` was not among them: the frontend handler answered a literal that no backend assertion produced, which is the exact failure mode D174 was written to remove. `spec=` reproduces the real defect — `hasattr(firestore.Client, 'query')` is `False` — so the `AttributeError` is the production one rather than a staged one, and the test additionally proves the dependency actually resolved (empty override map, `Client` constructed with `project=Settings().PROJECT_ID`). (b) tests the override, not the application. (c) accepts an unfalsifiable literal. (d) needs a live backend to record against. | The oracle depends on `spec=` continuing to reflect the installed `google-cloud-firestore`; a future release that adds a `query` method would change the production behaviour and this test would fail, which is the correct signal rather than a false one. Negatively validated: dropping `spec=` failed exactly 4 cases, including two that then raised on `Tweet.id` — one step *later* than the real failure. |
| D202 | Model pydantic v1's `int` coercion in the msw layer as `/^[+-]?\p{Nd}+(?:_\p{Nd}+)*$/u` over the trimmed value, and state the one residual difference rather than claiming byte parity. | (a) Keep the ASCII `/^[+-]?\d+$/`. (b) Narrow the documentation to "ASCII integers only" and leave the regex. (c) Reimplement CPython's integer parser in TypeScript. | The handler documented itself as backend-faithful while rejecting values the backend accepts. Measured on CPython 3.9.13 with pydantic 1.10.13, an `int` field accepts `'1_0'`, `'١٠'`, `'１０'`, `'۱۰'`, `'༡༠'`, `'+٠١'` and mixed-script `'١1'`, and rejects `'_10'`, `'10_'`, `'1__0'`, `'0x10'` and the superscripts `'⁰'`/`'²'` (category `No`, not `Nd`) — a grammar of optional whitespace, one optional sign, and `Nd` digits with single underscores strictly between digits, which the pattern expresses exactly. (a) leaves the divergence. (b) makes the mock honest by lowering it, when the accurate version is one regex. (c) is disproportionate. | One difference survives and is documented at the call site: CPython's integer parser strips `\x1c`-`\x1f` and `\x85`, and JavaScript's `trim()` strips `\uFEFF`; neither character survives URL encoding, so the difference is unreachable through the seam but is stated rather than papered over. Nine backend cases and nine mock cases assert the accepted forms in lockstep; restoring the ASCII pattern failed exactly those nine and left the seven rejections green. |
| D203 | Apply an exact-pin grammar to **every** active line of `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, and assert that the parsed-pin count equals the active-line count. | (a) Keep parsing only lines that contain `==`. (b) Add a minimum-count assertion. (c) Validate the manifest with `packaging.requirements` instead of a regex. | The old parser skipped any line it did not recognise, so loosening a pin to `>=` removed it from the assertion set silently and the only guard was "at least one pin exists" — a supply-chain check that a supply-chain change could switch off. Counting active lines makes an unparsed line a failure. Tightening the version character class also caught a real hole: the previous pattern accepted `pytest===8.4.2` as version `=8.4.2`. (b) still admits one loosened pin among many. (c) pulls a dependency into the guard that is meant to have none. | The grammar is now strict enough that a legitimately unusual but valid requirement line — an environment marker, an extras-plus-URL form — would fail the suite rather than be ignored. That is the intended direction, and the failure message names the line. Negatively validated: rewriting one `==` as `>=` failed exactly two cases, one naming the line and one reporting `assert 24 == 26`. |
| D204 | **Continues D144-D156.** Reduce the comments in `backend/tests/conftest.py`, `e2e/playwright.config.ts`, `frontend/src/pages/Dashboard.test.tsx`, `frontend/src/components/Analytics.test.tsx`, `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.ts` and `backend/tests/integration/test_http_tweets.py` to contracts, invariants and mechanics plus a log pointer, and correct three conftest comments that contradicted the code. | (a) Leave them: they are accurate and useful. (b) Delete the comments outright. (c) Move the argumentation into the log and leave a summary of it in the comment. | Rule 1 makes this log the single source of truth for "why", and these comments carried the argued form — the counterfactual, the rejected alternative, the risk — which is the content the rule places here. The three corrected comments were worse than redundant: they described defaulted settings as *removed* when the prologue pins them, described an arbitrary loopback allowance as intended, and said the `verify_token` shim returns `None` when it raises `MissingProductionSymbolError`. (b) removes the contract a reader needs at the call site. (c) is the duplication the rule forbids. | A reader at the call site now has to follow a pointer for the reasoning, which is a real cost and the one the rule accepts. Every rationale removed exists here; nothing was deleted without a home. |
| D205 | **[The word counts in the Risks column are the counts at the time of this decision, not of the delivered file — later checkpoints added disclosure that exceeded the cap. D308 records the delivered measurement.]** Rewrite all eight content slides of the executive deck to at most 40 body words, measured on the **broadest** reading — everything except the `h1`/`h2` headings, so eyebrows, table cells and Mermaid node labels all count. | (a) Count only paragraphs and list items, which would have passed six of the eight unchanged. (b) Move the detail into `<aside class="notes">` speaker notes. (c) Split the eight slides into sixteen and stay under the 18-section ceiling. | The measurement scope was reverse-engineered from the review's own figures and reproduced exactly: adding each slide's eyebrow to a count that includes table cells and diagram labels reproduces 68, 97 and 152 on the three slides where no diagram confounds it. Meeting the cap on the strictest reading means it is met on every looser one. (b) fails the same measurement — notes are text in the file — and hides detail from the printed artifact. (c) doubles the deck to satisfy a word cap, which is padding. | Compression is real, so the deck now carries less detail: the two-decimal coverage band, the shim requirement and the empty origin default each survive as a table row of four to seven words rather than as a sentence. Every one of them is stated in full here and in the dashboard, and the deck names those documents. Final counts on the review's scope: 28, 32, 32, 37, 29, 37, 35, 35 — smallest margin 3 words. |
| D206 | Correct the deck's three overstated claims and put the residual risks on the face of it. | (a) Soften the wording without changing what is claimed. (b) Remove the claims and say nothing about enforcement. (c) Keep them and add a caveat slide. | Three claims were not true as written: the authorized touches do not make the application start — it still raises `ImportError: cannot import name 'TwitterService'`, and what they enable is the suite *loading* it under shims; "five layers run on every push" describes a workflow no run has exercised; and "the build fails below the bar" omits the band the gate rounds into. An executive artifact that overstates enforcement is worse than one that omits it, because it is what a reader will quote. (b) drops the programme's main result. (c) leaves the overstatement on its own slide and the correction on another. | The deck is now less flattering, and the residual-risk rows — empty allowed origins, the deferred security patch, the test-only shims, the unexercised pipeline, the rounding band — are visible to a non-technical reader who may weigh them differently than intended. That is the trade this row accepts. |
| D207 | Pin all four jsDelivr subresources with `sha384` integrity plus `crossorigin` and `referrerpolicy`, add a `default-src 'none'` CSP, and include `connect-src https://cdn.jsdelivr.net`. | (a) SRI only, no CSP. (b) CSP with `script-src` hashes instead of `'unsafe-inline'`. (c) Omit `connect-src` and accept two console errors. (d) Vendor the three libraries into the repository. | The deck executes ~3 MB of third-party JavaScript from a CDN with nothing binding it to the bytes reviewed; SRI is the control for that and the CSP is what stops an injected reference to any *other* origin. `connect-src` was added only after measuring the alternative: without it, `default-src 'none'` blocks the `.map` source maps that reveal.js and lucide reference in a trailing `sourceMappingURL` comment, producing two console errors whenever developer tools are attached, and the origin it names is the one `script-src` already trusts. (b) would be stronger, but an inline-script hash breaks on any whitespace or line-ending change to a file that must stay editable. (d) contradicts Rule 4's single self-contained file. | `script-src` and `style-src` both keep `'unsafe-inline'`, because Rule 4 requires one self-contained file and both reveal.js and Mermaid inject style elements at runtime — so the CSP restricts *destinations* rather than preventing inline execution. The four hashes must be recomputed if any pin moves; each is recorded beside its byte count in the file so a mismatch is diagnosable. The Google Fonts stylesheet carries no integrity because that endpoint serves user-agent-dependent CSS. |
| D208 | **Supersedes D192.** Perform all three acceptance steps and reconcile every artifact to the evidence. | (a) Leave them outstanding, which is what D192 recorded. (b) Perform the E2E run only. (c) Report them verified on the strength of the configuration being asserted. | A browser was available at this checkpoint, so D192's premise no longer held, and four artifacts disagreed with each other about the state — the E2E onboarding document reported a completed run while the dashboard, this log and the deck all said outstanding. Contradiction between governance artifacts is worse than an honest "not done", because a reader cannot tell which one is stale. All three were run and left artifacts: E2E **11 passed / 1 reasoned skip / 0 failed** on Chrome 151 with a JUnit record; the deck rendered with 5 of 5 diagrams, 22 of 22 icons, zero console messages and 9 of 9 requests at 200; and **30 of 30** negative validations turned their covering suite red and restored byte-exact. | Two things remain unproven and are stated as such in §7.5 of the dashboard rather than folded into the pass: failure-path artifact retention was never exercised, because the run had no failure, so `e2e/test-results/` holds only `.last-run.json`; and the CI workflow has never run on a hosted runner. |
| D209 | Restate the traceability completeness proof on the change-set arithmetic — §F rows, distinct §F paths, change-set entries and logical operations as four separate figures — instead of on a row count described as a file count. | (a) Correct the number and keep the single-figure claim. (b) Split §F so one row means exactly one file. (c) Drop the numeric claim and assert completeness in prose. | "All 89 files in scope" was a row count wearing a file count's clothes, and the two cannot be equal here by construction: some rows name three or four paths, and six rows name *sections of the same file*. Figures that close against each other are checkable — 92 rows, 98 change-set entries, 99 logical operations, 99 distinct change-set paths, and 95 of those named in §F, with the difference of 4 being the three deleted legacy modules that §E accounts for plus the deleted extension-less `backend/app/api/routes` file that §I.4 and F16 account for. Entries and paths are separately stated because the rename makes them differ. (b) would split `conftest.py` into six rows and lose the per-concern grouping that makes §F readable. (c) removes the arithmetic that makes the claim a proof. | The figures must be re-derived whenever the change set moves, and a future contributor who adds a file without an §F row breaks the identity rather than the prose — which is the intended failure mode, but it does make the section higher-maintenance than a sentence would be. The reverse direction deliberately does **not** close: §F cells also name the production subjects a row covers, and 34 of those are unchanged by design, so only the change-set → §F direction is asserted. |
| D210 | Source every per-layer row of the dashboard from an explicit grouping predicate over `testcase`/`testsuite` identity, and add the two layers a two-row-per-stack version had silently dropped. | (a) Emit a separate JUnit artifact per layer by splitting the pytest invocation and adding Jest `projects`. (b) Keep one artifact per stack and leave the per-layer rows to a reader's judgement. (c) Reduce the panel to one row per stack. | Four of the five per-layer rows cited one aggregate artifact each, and the backend's `<testsuites>` root carries no attributes at all — only `name` — so the instruction to "read from the root element" could not be followed. Both runners already emit an exact grouping key: pytest's dotted `classname` and jest-junit's `{filepath}` suite name. Naming the predicate makes the panel reproducible without touching a runner, which the minimal-change constraint favours over (a). Grouping also exposed 63 backend and 147 frontend tests that belonged to no named layer, so the panel now carries a suite-level and a suite-infrastructure row and a completeness check that the rows sum to the artifact total. (c) throws away the resolution the panel exists for. | The grouped "summed time" is not wall-clock and diverges from it in both directions — smaller on the backend, because per-test time excludes collection and session fixtures; larger on the frontend, because Jest overlaps suite files across workers. The panel labels which is which and forbids presenting one as the other, but a reader who ignores the label will draw a wrong conclusion. |
| D211 | Leave `blitzy-deck/references/blitzy-reveal-theme.css` in the tree at the canonical path Rule 4 names, unlinked, with the deck self-contained via its inline `<style>`. | (a) Link it and remove the inline style block. (b) Delete it as dead weight. (c) Link it *and* keep the inline block. | Rule 4 requires both a single self-contained HTML file and that canonical theme path, and those pull against each other. Keeping the file satisfies the path while the inline block satisfies self-containment, which is the only arrangement that meets both. (a) makes the deck depend on a sibling file and stop being self-contained. (b) removes a path the rule names. (c) loads the same declarations twice. | The file is not exercised at runtime, so it can drift from the inline block without anything failing. It is recorded here so the drift is a known property rather than a discovery, and the deck's own comment states that the styles it uses are the inline ones. |

---

## §21 Conflict resolutions — where a rule and an instruction pulled in opposite directions

Seven conflicts arose between the four user-specified rules and the requirements' own constraints. Each is
recorded with the resolution and, in the "Why this choice" column, the argument for why that resolution is
the defensible one — because a conflict silently resolved is indistinguishable from a requirement missed.

The general principle applied throughout: where a specific, explicit instruction from the requirements
meets a general obligation from a rule, the specific instruction bounds *what may be changed* and the rule
is satisfied within that boundary rather than abandoned. Every one of the seven is resolved that way.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| C1 | **Rule 2 (Observability) versus the minimal-change clause and the two-touch allow-list.** Resolved by applying Rule 2 to the *test system* — the deliverable actually being built — rather than to the production application. | (a) Instrument production: a metrics endpoint, tracing and correlation-id middleware in `app/main.py`. (b) Declare Rule 2 inapplicable to a testing change set. (c) A partial reading: add health checks only. | (a) is simultaneously an unauthorized production change and the implementation of missing product features, both forbidden outright; the user's boundaries are specific and enumerated while Rule 2 is general, so the specific instruction governs the scope and the rule is met inside it. (b) would abandon a rule that can be honoured honestly. Rule 2's check-reuse-document clause is satisfied in full: **reused** are the two Codecov upload steps and their `backend` and `frontend` flags; **added** are the JUnit reporters on all three suites, the extra coverage reporters, the correlation identifiers of §8 and the collectability gate of D72. Production gaps are routed to §23 by D194, and the metrics surface is defined by D193. | Production remains unobservable, and this log is the only record of that fact. |
| C2 | **Rule 1's prohibition on rationale in code comments versus the requirements' mandated `# TESTING:` markers.** Resolved by splitting marker from rationale: the comments name only the blocker, and every alternative and risk lives here. | (a) Expand each marker into full inline rationale. (b) Drop the markers and rely on this log alone. (c) Carry the rationale in both places. | Rule 1 (Explainability) forbids this log's *content* migrating into comments; it does not forbid a marker existing. The requirements want a greppable trace in the diff, and terse markers give exactly that — `# TESTING: undeclared setting` on each of the four new fields, `# TESTING: param order` on each reordered signature, and `# TESTING: app.main route-module import` in each router-only module. Because nothing is duplicated, the two cannot drift apart, which is the failure mode (c) would introduce. | A reader of the diff alone sees the blocker but not the reasoning, and must follow the marker back to this log. |
| C3 | **Rule 3's instruction to update existing onboarding docs versus the requirements' instruction that the stale `README.md` identity be noted and not fixed.** Resolved by making every `README.md` change strictly additive and leaving the inaccurate identity text byte-for-byte intact. | (a) Correct the product identity while editing the file anyway. (b) Leave `README.md` untouched entirely. (c) Replace the README with an accurate one. | Rule 3 (Onboarding & Continued Development) asks that gaps be filled without replacing what is already there, and the absent testing onboarding is precisely such a gap; the requirements separately direct that the stale identity be recorded rather than repaired, and §23 records it. Both instructions are satisfiable at once because one is about what is *missing* and the other about what is *wrong*. | The README still describes an unrelated product above its new Testing section. |
| C4 | **Rule 4 forcing an executive deck into a change set whose write allow-list names no deck.** Resolved by treating the deck as a non-production documentation artifact in a new top-level `blitzy-deck/` directory. | (a) Omit the deck and accept a rule violation. (b) Place it inside one of the protected directories. (c) Place it in `docs/testing/` with this log. | The allow-list exists to protect production runtime behaviour and the excluded infrastructure directories, and a static HTML deck touches neither, so honouring Rule 4 (Executive Presentation) costs the protected surface nothing. The location is not a free choice either: the rule names `blitzy-deck/references/` as canonical. The artifact is flagged as in scope *only* because a rule requires it, so no reader mistakes it for a requirement of the work. See D188. | A top-level directory that exists for a rule rather than for the product. |
| C5 | **Rule 1's bidirectional traceability matrix versus the requirements' separately mandated disposition table.** Resolved as complementary rather than conflicting: one artifact serves both, with the disposition table as a projected view of the matrix. | (a) Two documents, a matrix and a standalone disposition table. (b) Put the disposition table in this log instead. | Both ask for the same mapping at different granularities, so a single bidirectional artifact satisfies both at the 100%-coverage standard Rule 1 sets, and §E of `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` carries all 25 legacy functions with their disposition in both directions. Two documents would duplicate one mapping, and the predictable failure is that one is updated and the other is not. Keeping it out of this log preserves the division of labour where this file holds *why* and the matrix holds *what maps to what*. | Readers looking for a disposition table have to know it is §E of the matrix. |
| C6 | **Rule 4's twelve-slide floor versus a code base of roughly a thousand lines.** Resolved by scoping the deck to the testing programme rather than to the product. | (a) Pad to the floor with filler slides. (b) Ship fewer slides than the rule requires. (c) Present the product's capabilities instead of the work performed. | Rule 4 itself directs that the presentation be scoped to the work performed, and the work here is the test programme — five layers, the collection-failure remediation, the two authorized touches, the coverage gates, the documented ceilings, the risk register and onboarding. That is enough substance to fill the target without padding, which is why this row exists: so nobody reads the slide floor as licence for filler. (c) would describe a product this change set did not build. | Leadership sees the testing programme, not the product's overall state. |
| C7 | **[Refined by D199 and D200 — the resolution stands, but "green" is now scoped differently: the test steps and the `e2e` job run and report on every push regardless of the lint conclusion, so the workflow's red conclusion no longer implies an unrun suite.]** **The expectation of a green CI matrix versus the prohibition on touching non-test steps**, given that `flake8 .`, `mypy .` and `npm run lint` are broken independently of testing. Resolved by changing the test steps and their install prerequisites, leaving lint and type-check alone, and scoping "green" to the two test steps and the new `e2e` job. | (a) Repair the lint and type-check steps so the whole workflow passes. (b) Remove them. (c) Report the workflow as failing with no explanation. | The requirements explicitly direct installing `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, which makes the install steps prerequisites of a test step rather than independent non-test steps (D186). The lint and type-check steps fail for reasons that predate testing — no linter or type checker is declared anywhere and the sources do not typecheck — so (a) needs new configuration plus production edits far outside the two touches (D187). The same reasoning keeps `frontend/package-lock.json` outside the authorized surface, so `npm ci` becomes `npm install`. | Installs are not byte-reproducible without a committed lockfile. |

---

## §22 The two authorized production touches, in full

Exactly two production changes were authorized, and both were required before the application object could
be constructed at all. This section carries their **complete** rationale, because the markers in the code
carry none: by C2 a `# TESTING:` comment names only the blocker it unblocks. Nothing below should be copied
back into those comments. The decision rows are D50, D51, D52 and D53; this is the argument behind them.

### Touch #1 — `backend/app/api/routes` becomes a package

`backend/app/api/routes` was a single extension-less **file** of 35 lines. Line 3 of `app/main.py` executes
`from app.api.routes import tweets, users, analytics, config`, which cannot resolve against a file, so
importing `app.main` failed with `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'app.api.routes'` and *no*
application-level test was possible — not a failing one, not a skipped one, none. The file is therefore
replaced by a package of four modules named for the four symbols that import statement asks for.

A byte-for-byte copy of the original body into `tweets.py` was impossible, because the original does not
compile. Two of its three endpoint signatures place a **defaulted parameter before a required one** — at
line 17, `def get_tweet(db: Session = Depends(get_db), tweet_id: str)`, and again at line 24,
`def generate_response(db: Session = Depends(get_db), tweet_id: str)`. CPython rejects this at parse time;
the exact wording of the message has changed across releases, so the defect is described here by its shape
rather than pinned to one string. The resolution is to reorder each signature to put the required parameter
first, giving `def get_tweet(tweet_id: str, db: Session = Depends(get_db))`, marked `# TESTING: param
order` at the two lines it applies to.

That reorder is **behaviour-preserving**, and the reason is specific rather than hopeful: FastAPI binds
dependencies and path parameters **by name**, not by position. It inspects the signature, matches
`tweet_id` to the `{tweet_id}` path segment and resolves `db` through `Depends(get_db)`, so parameter order
affects neither the runtime binding nor the generated OpenAPI schema. `get_tweets`, whose parameters were
already legal, is untouched.

`users.py`, `analytics.py` and `config.py` contain a `router = APIRouter()` and nothing else. **No endpoint
