The remaining rows cover the two frozen-manifest values the review found drifted, the reporting gap that made
three dashboard KPIs unsourceable, and the acceptance step that has since been performed. Rows are appended,
never rewritten: each row that replaces an earlier one names it.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D241 | **Supersedes the CI half of D49.** Run the E2E suite in CI through the package that owns it: `working-directory: e2e` with `npm test`. | (a) Keep `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` at the repository root, which is what the plan's ci.yml row prints and what shipped. (b) `npm --prefix e2e test` from the root, with no `working-directory`. (c) Give the repository a root `package.json` so a root `npx` resolves. | Measured, in this checkout: `npx --no-install playwright --version` at the root reports `npx canceled due to missing packages and no YES option: ["playwright@1.62.1"]` — there is no root manifest, so (a) resolves no project-owned runner and, without `--no-install`, fetches and executes **1.62.1**, an unpinned version that also breaks the Node 16 ceiling the matrix declares. The same command inside `e2e/` prints `Version 1.44.1`. (b) works and is documented, but `working-directory` is the idiom the two test steps in the `build` job already use, so (a)→this is the smaller diff and the more legible one. (c) restructures the repository for a workflow's convenience. Note the plan is not uniformly against this: its own command table publishes `cd e2e && npx playwright test` for local use, so only the ci.yml row named the root form. | Diverges from the literal text of the plan's ci.yml row, and is escalated as such rather than presented as compliant. The plan's completion criteria also require the new `e2e` job to **pass**, which the literal form cannot, so the two clauses could not both be honoured. |
| D242 | **Refines D111 and D129 into CI.** Delete the `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` step. Select a browser already present on the runner into `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` — trying `$CHROME_BIN`, then `/usr/bin/google-chrome`, `/usr/bin/chromium-browser`, `/usr/bin/chromium`, and failing the job with a pointer to `e2e/README.md` section 2 if none is executable — then run the download-free `npm run browsers:verify`. | (a) Keep the downloader step, which is what the plan's row prints and what shipped. (b) Add a third-party browser-provisioning action. (c) Cache Playwright's browser directory and download only on a cache miss. (d) Upgrade to `@playwright/test >= 1.55.1`, where the advisory is fixed. | D129's whole point was that a script one `npm run` away *is* the exposure to CVE-2025-59288's unverified TLS chain, and it deleted `install:browsers` for exactly that reason — leaving the same command inline in a workflow step reinstates what that row removed, one layer down. The runner image is an attested artifact, which is what "provision only from artifacts verified out of band" asks for, and `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` is already a first-class input of `playwright.config.ts`. (b) adds an unreviewed action to the supply chain the same finding is about. (c) still downloads on a miss, so the exposure is intermittent rather than removed. (d) requires `node>=18`, which the frozen matrix does not declare. Verified locally: the suite runs green against a system Chrome named this way, and `browsers:verify` exits 0 without fetching. | The job now depends on the runner image shipping a browser. Bounded by failing closed with a message naming the four paths it tried, rather than silently falling back to a download. A runner image that drops Chrome breaks this job loudly, and the fix is to name a provisioned executable rather than to reinstate the downloader. |
| D243 | Persist all three JUnit result streams as CI artifacts — `backend/reports/junit.xml`, `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml`, `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml` — each through `actions/upload-artifact` with `if: always()`. | (a) Leave them unpersisted, which is what shipped, and delete the dashboard KPIs and the onboarding claims that read them. (b) Install a test-report publisher action that annotates the run. (c) Upload only the failing runs' results. | Three reporters were configured and three documents describe their output as a CI input, but nothing preserved the files past the job, so dashboard KPIs K3, K4 and K6 had no admissible source and the frontend onboarding guide named a file that CI discarded. Uploading is the smallest change that makes the existing configuration and the existing prose true at the same time. (b) adds an action and a permission scope for presentation the dashboard already specifies. (c) loses the pass-side trend the KPIs are for. `if: always()` is required rather than stylistic: the run that most needs its results is the one that failed. | Artifact storage per run, and three more steps to keep in step with the reporter paths. The paths are asserted by the runner configurations that write them, so a drift breaks a named upload rather than silently emptying a KPI. |
| D244 | Add two readiness steps to the `build` job, each immediately before the suite it gates: `pytest --collect-only -q` with `working-directory: backend`, and `npm test -- --listTests` with `working-directory: frontend`. | (a) No readiness step, relying on the suites themselves to fail on an unimportable module. (b) Backend only, since that is the gate the plan names. (c) A separate readiness job. | This suite's historical failure mode was collection, not assertion: all three legacy modules died on import. A collection gate therefore separates "the graph does not resolve" from "an assertion is wrong", and it is the check the plan's completion criteria name. It was also being published as a pipeline feature by the executive deck while existing nowhere in the workflow, which is the accuracy defect the review raised. The frontend half costs about four seconds and closes the same gap for the other stack. (c) duplicates installation for a two-command check. | Two more steps, and a few seconds per run. `--listTests` prints an "did not exit" advisory under some npm wrappers; it exits 0 regardless, verified here. |
| D245 | Give the `e2e` job an explicit `permissions: contents: read` and check out with `persist-credentials: false`. | (a) Leave both at the action defaults, which is what shipped. (b) Set a workflow-level `permissions` block covering both jobs. | The job installs and executes third-party npm lifecycle scripts and a browser; the default token scope and a persisted credential helper are both available to anything that runs. Nothing in the job writes to the repository, so read-only and non-persisted are the least privilege that still works. (b) would change the `build` job as well, and that job's steps are outside the surface the plan authorizes — the `e2e` job is new and therefore wholly in scope, so the narrower change is also the compliant one. | The `build` job keeps the default token scope. That is a deliberate scope boundary rather than an oversight, and it is listed as a suggested next task. |
| D246 | Pin every action the `e2e` job and the new upload steps use to a full commit SHA with a trailing comment naming the release, and take the **current** v4-line releases rather than the SHA behind the `@v2` tags that shipped: `actions/checkout` v4.4.0, `actions/setup-node` v4.4.0, `actions/upload-artifact` v4.6.2. | (a) Keep the mutable `@v2` / `@v4` major tags. (b) Pin the SHA of the exact versions the mutable tags resolved to, keeping `checkout` and `setup-node` on the v2 line. (c) Pin every action in the file, including the four in the `build` job. | A mutable tag is a moving target that no review covers. (b) closes that hole but freezes two actions on a line whose Node runtime the platform has been retiring, which converts a live deprecation warning into a future hard failure — pinning is meant to remove risk, not preserve it, and "immutable *reviewed* SHA" implies a release someone would review today. (c) touches the `build` job's non-test steps, which the plan does not authorize. Each SHA was resolved from `git ls-remote` against the upstream tag rather than copied from documentation. | The file now mixes pinned v4 actions in the `e2e` job with tagged v2 actions in the `build` job. The inconsistency is a scope boundary, is visible in the diff, and pinning the remaining four is a suggested next task. A SHA pin also stops receiving upstream patches, so it needs periodic review. |
| D247 | **Supersedes D163, restoring the value D100 and D125 chose.** Pin `python-jose[cryptography]==3.3.0` in `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, move the same version into `test_dependency_closure.py`'s security note and `backend/tests/README.md`'s pin table, and install 3.3.0 so D101's closure gate agrees. | (a) Keep `3.5.0`, which is what D163 decided on CVE grounds. (b) `3.4.0`, the first release carrying both fixes. (c) Keep `3.5.0` in the manifest and record the drift instead of resolving it. (d) Express the pin as a range so both versions satisfy it. | The frozen dependency inventory fixes this manifest at 3.3.0, and that document is the agreed contract this programme aligns to rather than one it may amend — D163 raised a pinned value on its own authority, which is the compliance defect the review raised. The security argument that motivated it survives intact and is not lost: CVE-2024-33663 and CVE-2024-33664 are recorded in the manifest comment, in the gate module, in the backend pin table and as a Rule 3 suggested next task, and neither is reachable from any test here — the suite round-trips one HS256 token with an explicit key and never decrypts a JWE or loads an OpenSSH ECDSA key. (b) is neither the inventory's value nor installed, so the gate would stay red. (c) is what D101 exists to prevent. (d) defeats the gate's purpose. Verified after the change: the gate passes as `[python-jose==3.3.0]`, `pip check` reports no broken requirements, and the suite is green at 573 passed. | Both CVEs remain present in the pinned distribution. Unreachable from this suite, but any future production use of JWE decryption or ECDSA key loading would be exposed — which is why raising the pin is escalated to the repository's owners as a task rather than resolved here. |
| D248 | Declare `preset: 'ts-jest'` in `frontend/jest.config.js`, keeping both explicit transformers and their inline compiler options exactly as they were. | (a) Keep no preset, which is what shipped, on the ground that the explicit transform is semantically equivalent. (b) Adopt the preset and delete the explicit `^.+\.[jt]sx?$` entry, relying on the preset's own. | The frozen plan specifies the preset by name, and semantic equivalence is not the same as the specified configuration — a reader checking the plan against the file found it absent, which is the compliance defect raised. Keeping both transformers is what preserves behaviour: Jest merges a preset's `transform` after the config's own, and resolves a file against the first matching pattern, so the preset's `^.+\.tsx?$` entry lands third and is shadowed. (b) would silently drop the inline `tsconfig` and `diagnostics: false`, and the sources do not typecheck, so every `.tsx` suite would fail. Verified with `jest --showConfig`: the resolved order is extension-less transformer, then the inline-options entry, then the preset's; and the suite is unchanged at 307 passed with identical coverage. | The preset contributes one unreachable transform entry, which reads as dead configuration to anyone who does not know the merge order. That is why the file says so in one sentence, and why this row records the reasoning. |
| D249 | Write every multi-step install sequence in the onboarding documents as root-run subshells — `(cd frontend && npm install)` then `(cd e2e && npm install)` — rather than `npm --prefix <dir> install`. | (a) `npm --prefix frontend install`, which is the form the review suggested first. (b) `cd frontend && npm install && cd ..`. (c) Leave the sequence as consecutive bare `cd` blocks, which is what shipped. | (a) does not work, and this was measured rather than assumed: on npm 10.9.8 `npm install` reads the manifest of the directory it is invoked from, so from a root with no `package.json` all three spellings — `npm --prefix frontend install`, `npm install --prefix frontend`, `npm install --prefix ./frontend` — fail with `npm error code ENOENT … open …\package.json`. (`npm --prefix <dir> run <script>` *does* resolve the manifest at `<dir>`, and is kept where used.) (b) strands the reader in the subdirectory whenever the install fails, which is exactly when the next command matters. (c) is the defect: copied in order, the second block's `cd e2e` resolves under `frontend/`. A subshell cannot leak a working-directory change however it exits. | Subshell syntax is POSIX-shell-specific and does not work in `cmd.exe` or PowerShell. The blocks were already shell-shaped — the backend leg activates a virtual environment with `.` — so this narrows nothing that was not already narrowed, and both documents state that the parentheses are load-bearing rather than decorative. |
| D250 | **Supersedes the E2E half of D192.** Record the Playwright suite as **executed in a real browser**, with its measured outcome, and keep the Rule 4 deck render tracked as a separate item rather than reported together with it. | (a) Keep both listed as outstanding, which is what shipped. (b) Report both as done because the E2E run was. (c) Drop the acceptance list now that part of it is satisfied. | D192's premise was that neither had been performed; the E2E run has since been performed twice in this checkout, and reporting it as outstanding is now the inaccuracy. Measured: `npm test` from `e2e/`, and `npm --prefix e2e test` from the repository root, each **11 passed, 1 skipped, 0 failed**, exit 0, against a system Chrome named by `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`, producing `reports/e2e-junit.xml` with `tests="12" failures="0" skipped="1" errors="0"` plus the HTML report. The one skip carries its reason in the spec. (b) is the failure mode D192 existed to prevent — two unlike claims sharing one status line. (c) would discard the record of what remains. | The run is single-browser: only `chromium` is exercised, and the browser is a prerequisite rather than something a script here obtains. Both limits are listed in `e2e/README.md` section 9. |
| D251 | **Supersedes the deck half of D192.** Bound `blitzy-deck/executive-summary.html` with a `<meta>` Content-Security-Policy plus a `sha384` `integrity` and `crossorigin="anonymous"` on each of the four jsDelivr assets; leave the Google Fonts stylesheet unpinned; omit `frame-ancestors`; and record the deck as **rendered and verified in a real browser**. | (a) Leave the CDN tags bare, which is what shipped — pinned URLs, no digest, no policy. (b) Pin a `sha384` digest on the Google Fonts stylesheet too. (c) Replace `script-src 'unsafe-inline'` with a hash of the inline bootstrap. (d) Vendor all four assets into `blitzy-deck/` and drop the network dependency entirely. (e) Keep `frame-ancestors 'none'` for defence in depth. | A pinned URL says which version is wanted; only a digest says the bytes were not substituted, and only a policy stops a compromised asset from exfiltrating anything — `connect-src 'none'` means the deck can open no socket at all, and it needs none. (b) is impossible: `fonts.googleapis.com` varies its body by user agent, so no single digest can ever match; the CSP origin entries are what bound it instead. (c) is stronger on paper but brittle in practice — the bootstrap is hand-edited, and a stale hash blanks the deck silently rather than loudly. (d) would put roughly 3 MB of third-party code in git and contradicts the single-self-contained-file requirement the deck is built to. (e) was tried and **measured to be counter-productive**: a meta-delivered policy ignores `frame-ancestors`, so it protected nothing and logged an advisory on every load — it is now absent, with a comment saying why. | Anti-framing is consequently unprotected for this file and can only be restored by an HTTP response header; recorded here rather than papered over. `script-src 'unsafe-inline'` also remains, which limits the policy's XSS value for a hand-edited single file — its value here is origin restriction and digest pinning, not inline-script defence. Two `connect-src` blocks on third-party `.map` files appear when a debugger is attached; a viewer never requests them, and allowing `cdn.jsdelivr.net` in `connect-src` to silence them would weaken the policy for no end-user benefit. Verification, in a browser, after a cache-bypassing reload: all four assets HTTP 200 with every digest independently recomputed and matched, zero integrity failures, zero refusals of any deck resource, 16 sections, all 5 Mermaid diagrams rendered as SVG, all 19 Lucide placeholders replaced, and no clipping at 1920×1080. |
## §29 Making the observability chain reachable, retained and consumed

Scope: the decisions taken when review found that the *configuration* of this programme's observability
was sound while the *chain* was not. Producers were configured but unreachable, artifacts were produced
but not retained, the coverage gate compared a rounded number, readiness was a documented command
rather than a step, several dashboard cells had no producer capable of filling them, and the acceptance
state disagreed between documents. Nine rows, each naming the earlier row it refines or supersedes.

The distinction that runs through all of them: **configured locally** is not **executed and retained**.
A reporter that writes a file nobody keeps, and a gate nobody's build reaches, are indistinguishable
from having neither.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D252 | Configure `precision = 2` in a **new `backend/.coveragerc`**, so `--cov-fail-under=90` compares an exact two-decimal total. | (a) Leave the default precision and document the rounding, as `backend/tests/README.md` did. (b) Raise the threshold to 91 so the rounding band sits above the requirement. (c) Add a `[coverage:report]` block to a new `setup.cfg` or `pyproject.toml` instead. (d) Post-process `coverage.json` in a pytest hook and fail there. | coverage.py compares `round(total, precision)` and `precision` defaults to **0**, so a threshold of 90 accepted anything from 89.5% upward. Measured: `pytest --cov=app --cov-fail-under=90` printed `FAIL … Total coverage: 89.93%` and **exited 0** — printing failure while exiting success, which is the worst state a gate can be in. (a) documents a defect instead of closing it. (b) makes the gate mean something other than the proposal's own 90% criterion, and the number would drift from `documentation/Software Project Proposal.md`. (c) introduces a file with much wider reach — a `pyproject.toml` changes packaging and tool discovery for everything in `backend/` — where `.coveragerc` affects coverage alone. (d) is bespoke code where a documented setting exists. Verified both directions: the same command now exits 1, and on the gated scope `--cov-fail-under=91.79` exits 0 while `91.80` exits 1. | Every reported percentage now renders with two decimals, so figures quoted in prose changed spelling without changing value — `89%` became `88.89%`, `94%` became `93.75%`. `backend/tests/README.md` and the dashboard were updated together with this row, and any older write-up quoting a whole-number module percentage is stale. The gate is also now sensitive to a hundredth, so a single newly-missed statement can fail a build that previously rounded past it — which is the intent. |
| D253 | Ship [`docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py`](./dashboard-extract.py) as the dashboard's **producer**, and have it read the backend **aggregate** from `coverage.xml` but backend **per-module and per-package** detail from `coverage.json`. | (a) Keep the template hand-filled from the named artifacts. (b) Read everything from the Cobertura. (c) Read everything from `coverage.json` and drop the Cobertura from the contract. (d) Adopt an existing report aggregator as a new dependency. (e) Scrape the `term-missing` terminal output. | Review found several panels unfillable from the artifacts named for them, and the cause was measured rather than guessed: with `--cov` given four package paths, coverage.py writes four `<source>` roots, a single `<package name=".">`, and **bare basenames** as each `filename`, so any per-module row read from the Cobertura is a guess about which root a file came from. `coverage.json` keys files by full relative path, carries `missing_lines` outright, and records `meta.branch_coverage` — which is what lets the branch column be reported as *unmeasured* rather than as zero. (b) is therefore not available and (c) discards the file the Codecov step consumes. (a) is what produced the disagreements in the first place: a hand-filled cell has no provenance, and two documents drifted. (d) adds a dependency to a manifest the AAP fixes, for a job the standard library does in one file. (e) parses a human-facing format that carries no uncovered-line data for the frontend at all. | Two producers must agree, so the extractor asserts it and prints "the two backend producers disagree" rather than silently preferring one. The layer partition is a rule in code, not in the artifacts, so adding a test directory without extending `BACKEND_LAYERS` puts its cases in a `Backend other` row — visible, but only to someone reading the panel. And the script is untested code in a repository whose point is tests: it is deliberately small, dependency-free and validated by running it against real artifacts, including a negative run with one artifact removed, but it carries no suite of its own. |
| D254 | Give `e2e`'s `test:list` script an explicit `--reporter=line`, so listing the suite cannot overwrite the previous run's reports. | (a) `playwright test --list` unqualified, as first written. (b) Order CI so listing always precedes the run and accept the clobber. (c) Point the list invocation at a throwaway config. | Measured: plain `--list` rewrites `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml` with `tests="12" skipped="12"` and a `time` near zero — an all-skipped stub that is indistinguishable, to any consumer, from a run in which nothing executed. That artifact is uploaded and read by the dashboard, so the stub would become the published result. (b) is true of the workflow as written but relies on step order staying that way forever, and does nothing for a developer who lists after running. (c) duplicates configuration that must then be kept in step with the real one. | The listing no longer produces a machine-readable artifact of its own; its output is captured by `tee` into `e2e/reports/list-tests.txt` instead, which is what §6.4 of the dashboard reads. A future consumer wanting structured discovery data would need a second invocation. |
| D255 | Make readiness an **automated CI gate** with a retained result on all three layers — `pytest --collect-only -q`, `jest --listTests`, `playwright test --list` — rather than a documented local command. | (a) Leave all three as documented commands, which is what the earlier text described while calling them gates. (b) Automate the backend gate only, since that is the one the AAP names. (c) Fold collection into the test step and rely on the suite failing. | This suite's inherited failure mode was *collection*: three legacy modules that never reached an assertion, so a passing-test count of zero looked like an empty suite rather than a broken one. A check that would have caught that is worth a step of its own, and the AAP names the backend one as a completion criterion. (a) is the defect review found — three documents called it a delivered gate while no workflow produced it. (b) leaves the two layers whose resolution graph is most fragile (a Jest `moduleNameMapper` miss, a Playwright `testMatch` miss) with no early signal. (c) hides the distinction: a suite that fails to *collect* and a suite that fails to *pass* need different responses, and merging them costs the diagnosis. Each step pipes into a retained file with `set -o pipefail`, so `tee` cannot mask a non-zero exit. | Three more steps to keep working, and each duplicates work the suite repeats moments later — about four seconds on the backend, under two on the other two layers. The E2E listing is ordered ahead of the browser-provisioning gate so a host with no browser still leaves a readiness result; that ordering is load-bearing and is commented as such in the workflow. |
| D256 | **Refines D187 and C7.** Give every test and reporting step `if: ${{ !cancelled() }}`, leaving `flake8 .`, `mypy .` and `npm run lint` byte-identical, and keeping the single `build` job. | (a) `continue-on-error: true` on the three lint steps. (b) Move the test chain into a new independent job. (c) Delete or repair the lint steps. (d) `if: always()` instead of `!cancelled()`. | GitHub Actions skips an unconditional step once an earlier step has failed, so three acknowledged-broken steps made the entire suite, both Codecov uploads and every artifact unreachable **on every push** — the producers were configured and never ran. (a) was already rejected by D187 for hiding the signal, and it still is: a green job with broken linting is worse than a red job with a run inside it. (b) would restructure a workflow the AAP fixes at one `build` job plus one `e2e` job, and would duplicate both installs. (c) needs new linter configuration plus production edits far outside the two authorized touches. (d) would keep running after a cancellation, wasting a runner and producing artifacts nobody asked for. The condition makes the gates independent of the lint conclusion without changing it: the job still goes red. | The workflow's overall conclusion is still red until lint and type-check are addressed separately, so "CI is green" remains false and no document claims it — what is now true is that the suite runs and reports on every push. A reader who equates a red check with an unrun suite will misread it; the dashboard and the root `README.md` both state the distinction. |
| D257 | **Supersedes the residual of D111.** Fix the CI end-to-end contract: no automated path may download a browser, the browser is pre-provisioned and named by `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` with its path and version logged, and the suite is invoked with `working-directory: e2e` through the **package-local pinned runner** (`npm test`). | (a) Keep `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` and `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from the repository root. (b) Keep the downloader but pin a mirror. (c) Use `npx playwright test` inside `e2e/`, which does resolve the local runner. (d) Vendor a browser into the repository. | (a) is what the workflow did, and it contradicted this repository's own two guarantees: D111 took CVE-2025-59288's remedy by removing the download from every automated path, and no runner is installed at the repository root, so root-level `npx` resolves nothing local — it either refuses or fetches an unpinned Playwright and runs the suite on whatever it downloaded. Either way the result has no provenance. (b) still trusts a TLS chain the downloader does not verify. (c) is safe inside the package but leaves `npx` in the command, and npx is online-capable by design; naming the script fails closed instead. (d) puts a ~130 MB binary in git, already rejected by D111. The verification step logs the runner version, runs `browsers:verify` (a dry run that fetches nothing), and fails with a `::error::` naming the out-of-band contract when no executable is present. | The job now depends on the runner image carrying a browser at `/usr/bin/google-chrome`. If a future image drops it the job fails at the verification step with an explicit message rather than silently downloading one — which is the intended trade, but it is a trade: provisioning is now an environment responsibility, and the residual D111 recorded (an operator running a download on an untrusted network) is unchanged. |
| D258 | Retain the whole evidence set as four named artifacts with an explicit `if-no-files-found` policy per class and a 30-day retention, and add two steps that **read** the JUnit streams before the uploads. | (a) Keep uploading `e2e/playwright-report/` alone. (b) Upload everything as one artifact with a single policy. (c) `if-no-files-found: error` uniformly. (d) Add a Codecov-style third-party test-reporting action. | All three JUnit files and every trace, screenshot and video disappeared with the runner workspace, so a failure could not be examined after the fact and no dashboard figure had commit-bound provenance. (b) makes one absent file ambiguous across five paths. (c) is wrong for exactly one path: trace, screenshot and video are configured `on-failure` only, so a fully passing run legitimately writes nothing to `e2e/test-results/` and `error` would turn a green run red — that path alone uses `warn`, commented in place. (d) adds an action to a workflow whose reporting surface the AAP fixes at the two existing Codecov steps. The two verify steps close the other half: they assert each report exists, is non-empty, and declares `tests="[1-9][0-9]*"`, which is what refuses a collection-time stub. `fail_ci_if_error: true` on the two reused Codecov steps makes a failed upload visible; their actions and their `backend`/`frontend` flags are untouched. | Retention is a storage cost and 30 days is a judgement, not a derived number. `warn` on the failure-artifact path means a genuinely lost trace reads the same as a green run; the JUnit stream is the cross-check, since a failure there with no artifact beside it is the signal to look. And `fail_ci_if_error` makes the build depend on Codecov's availability — accepted, because a silently dropped upload is a coverage trend that quietly stops moving. |
| D259 | **Supersedes D192.** Record the E2E browser run and the Rule 4 deck render as **performed**, each with the command, runtime, artifact and result, and keep stating the two things that remain unproven. | (a) Leave D192's outstanding disposition in place. (b) Report them as performed without provenance. (c) Drop the E2E layer and the deck rather than carry either claim. | D192 was the correct disposition while the steps were outstanding, and it stayed correct until they were done — but by then `e2e/README.md` §8 asserted an exact real-browser result while the dashboard and this log still called it outstanding, so the repository contradicted itself and a reader had no way to tell which was true. Performing both and recording *how* resolves it in the only direction that adds information. (b) is the failure mode being corrected, not a fix for it. (c) discards work that functions. What is still unproven is stated rather than glossed: this workflow has never run on GitHub Actions from this branch, so every CI statement is about the file and not a run; and only `chromium` is exercised. | A recorded result is a point-in-time measurement and goes stale: the figures name the commit whose tree they were taken from, and the commands are reproducible, but nobody re-runs them automatically. The CI retention added by D258 is what makes the *next* run's evidence auditable; this row's evidence is local. |
| D260 | Redact operational and credential-shaped values from anything published, while leaving every assertion exact. | (a) Leave them: the project identifier is not a secret and the credential values are obvious test literals. (b) Redact in the documents but not in report attachments. (c) Weaken the assertions to match the redaction. | Neither value is a secret today, and that is precisely why the habit matters — a report format that carries whole credential-shaped bodies is one real fixture away from carrying a real one, and CI now retains those reports for 30 days (D258). The ambient project identifier is unnecessary operational metadata in a public-facing example, and the warning it illustrates works identically with a placeholder. (c) is the one option that loses information: the equality assertions on the submitted body are the point of those tests, so they stay byte-exact in memory and only the **attachment** is reduced to key names, value-presence and a short digest. | A redacted attachment is less useful when debugging a genuine mismatch: the reader sees which key differs and that a value was present, not what it was. The failing assertion's own diff still shows the expected value, which is where that information belongs. The digest is not a security control — it is short, and the inputs are known literals — it exists so two runs can be compared without printing the values. |

## §30 Verifying the claims §29 made, and the stubs that can impersonate a run

Scope: three decisions taken while carrying out §24's own commitments. D259 undertook to record the deck
render as performed *with provenance*; performing it surfaced two things that decision could not have
anticipated — that the deck was edited after its first verification, and that the capture tool corrupts
this particular deck. The third row closes a hazard found while validating the onboarding documents,
in the same family as D254 but on a different runner.

The thread joining them: **a verdict is scoped to the bytes it was taken against, and an artifact that
exists is not the same as an artifact that means anything.** Both are ways a true-looking claim can be
false, which is the failure mode §24 exists to correct.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D261 | **Extends D259.** Verify the deck in a real browser **twice** — once before and once after the slide edits — and treat the first verdict as not covering the shipped file. Record both runs, and mark which one the shipped bytes were verified against. | (a) Verify once before editing and carry the verdict forward, since the edits were small and text-only. (b) Verify once after editing and discard the first run. (c) Edit first, verify once, and describe the edits as unverified. | The two edits were exactly the kind that a text diff cannot clear: one widened a left-to-right flowchart from three nodes to four, and one added a fourth bullet above a brand lockup. Both are *layout* changes whose failure mode is clipping, and clipping is invisible in a diff. (a) is the defect this whole checkpoint is about — a claim of browser verification covering bytes that were never in a browser. (b) loses the record of what the edits were tested against, and the pre-edit run is what establishes that the edits caused no regression elsewhere. (c) leaves the two riskiest slides as the two unverified ones. The second run measured the specific risk rather than restating a pass: the four-node flowchart sits 13.5 px inside its container on every side and 202 px against a 270 px cap, and the fourth bullet consumed 73 px of a 311 px budget leaving the lockup at its original position. | The rule generalises awkwardly: taken literally, *any* later edit to the deck invalidates the recorded verdict, and nothing enforces re-verification. The honest position is that §7.4 of the dashboard names the revision its evidence covers, and a future editor has to re-run it. Neither run is retained in the repository — `.gitignore` excludes both artifact directories — so the evidence is local to the run that produced it and a fresh clone cannot inspect it. |
| D262 | Verify this deck with **viewport** screenshot capture at 1920×1080, never full-page capture, and record why. | (a) Use full-page capture, which is the usual default for documenting a page. (b) Use full-page capture and treat its output as the evidence. (c) Verify by reading the DOM only, taking no screenshots. | Full-page capture applies a device-metrics override that perturbs reveal.js's transform layout and silently resets the slide and hash state. Its first use here produced an image with content displaced about 330 px, a sentence truncated mid-word and a KPI card cut off — a **false defect**, on a slide that renders perfectly. That was diagnosed rather than believed: the navigation-entry count stayed at 1 and an in-page marker survived, so no reload had occurred, and a viewport re-capture of the same slide was clean. Viewport capture loses nothing here because the document has zero scrollable overflow on every slide — `scrollWidth === clientWidth` and `scrollHeight === clientHeight` throughout — so there is no off-screen region for a full-page capture to add. (b) would have published a corrupted image as proof. (c) gives no visual evidence at all, and the questions being asked are visual. | This is a property of *this* deck, not a general rule, and it is recorded where someone re-verifying will look. It also means the screenshots are 1920×1080 crops rather than whole-document images: correct here, but a future deck that does scroll would need the opposite choice, and nothing detects that automatically. |
| D263 | **Same hazard as D254, different runner.** Refuse a zero-case JUnit stream in `dashboard-extract.py` rather than render it as zeros, and have `--require-all` validate the **parsed** streams instead of only file presence. | (a) Rely on the workflow's step ordering, which already writes the real report last. (b) Rely on the two verify steps, which already reject `tests="0"`. (c) Give the backend collection step its own `--junitxml` path so it cannot collide. (d) Render the zeros and let the reader notice. | `--junitxml` lives in `addopts`, so it applies to *every* invocation — including `pytest --collect-only`, which therefore overwrites `backend/reports/junit.xml` with a well-formed report declaring `tests="0"`. Measured at 271 bytes with zero `<testcase>` elements, and nothing warns. `jest --listTests` does **not** have the defect, verified by hashing the report either side of a run. (a) and (b) are both true and both retained — they are why CI is safe — but each protects only the workflow, and a developer who runs the readiness command *after* a suite has a stub in their tree with no guard at all. (c) diverges the local artifact path from the one every consumer reads, to fix a problem the consumer can detect itself. (d) is the failure being closed: zeros read as a clean run of nothing, which is worse than an error. Verified on all four paths — all-real exits 0, an absent artifact exits 1 naming it, a stub exits 1 naming the command that overwrote it, and restoring exits 0. | The extractor now refuses a stream a human might have wanted to inspect, and the refusal is by exit code plus a message rather than a partial render — deliberate, but it means `--require-all` is not a way to see *some* of the picture. And the guard is a heuristic about intent: a genuine suite that legitimately contained zero tests would be rejected too. That is acceptable here because a zero-test suite is itself a defect in this repository, but it would not be in a project where an empty layer is valid. |
## §31 Code-review remediation — CI execution, browser provisioning, and the acceptance state of the documentation set

Scope: the `e2e` job of `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, the browser prerequisite the end-to-end layer depends
on, the acceptance evidence the documentation set reports, and the coverage claim made for the missing
`Chart.register`.

These six rows exist because the review at the final-documentation checkpoint found the shipped workflow
doing the opposite of what every other artifact promised, and found three claims that had outrun their
evidence. Each row names the earlier row it supersedes or corrects; none of those rows is rewritten, per
D190.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D264 | **[The `browsers:verify` premise in this row's reasoning is superseded by D300, and its CI half by D301 — D236 had already replaced that script with a fail-closed filesystem gate, so "always exits 0" was no longer true when this row cited it. The two-script decision itself stands, and both scripts fail closed.]** **Supersedes the CI half of D49 and D129.** Run the `e2e` job's suite as `npm test` with `working-directory: e2e`, delete its `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` step, and gate it on a new `e2e/scripts/require-browser.js` — exposed as `browsers:require` — which resolves a browser from `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`, then the platform's vendor install locations, then Playwright's cache, publishes the resolved path to `$GITHUB_ENV`, and **exits non-zero** when there is none. `browsers:verify` is kept unchanged beside it. | (a) Keep the downloader and accept the exposure, which is what shipped. (b) Add `working-directory: e2e` to the runner step and leave the downloader, fixing only the invocation. (c) Make `browsers:verify` itself fail, replacing the dry-run reporter. (d) Vendor a Chromium archive into the repository. (e) Upgrade to a remediated Playwright. | D49 chose the package-owned invocation and D129 removed every download path from the manifest, but the workflow reached the runner through a root `npx` — which the root cannot resolve, so it either refuses or fetches an unpinned Playwright that Node 16 will not run — and then reintroduced the CVE-2025-59288 downloader that D129 exists to keep out. So the two guarantees this log records were true of the package and false of CI, which is worse than either defect alone: the onboarding, traceability and executive artifacts all cited the workflow as evidence. (b) leaves the security defect. (c) breaks D111's and TRACEABILITY H12's statement that `browsers:verify` fetches nothing and always exits 0, which other documents cite; a *second* script keeps both properties available and makes the reporting/gating distinction explicit rather than implicit. (d) puts ~130 MB in git. (e) needs `node>=18` and the declared ceiling is 16.x. The browser CI now uses is the `google-chrome-stable` build already in the `ubuntu-latest` runner image, built in from Google's signed repository — a channel that signs what it ships, which is precisely the property the advisory costs the downloader. | CI now depends on the runner image continuing to carry a Chromium-family browser. If a future image drops it the job fails at `browsers:require` with the remedy rather than silently downloading, which is the intended failure mode, but it is still a failure the project does not control. Two scripts also mean a reader must know which one gates; `e2e/README.md` section 2 states the difference in one place. |
| D265 | **[Extended by D268 for the deck render and by D269 for the two items it left open — every acceptance step named in this row has since been performed. The wording below is the decision as taken, not the current state, and its "§7.3 honesty statement" was written when the honesty statement was numbered §7.3; on the delivered tree it is §7.5 and §7.3 is the negative-validation ledger.]** **Supersedes D192.** Report the end-to-end browser run as **performed**, with its date, runner, browser-provisioning route, result and artifact readings recorded in `docs/testing/DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.2 A1 — and keep the *failure-artifact* half of that step, the Rule 4 deck render, and the complete negative-validation sweep open as separately identified items. | (a) Leave D192 standing, which left four artifacts asserting a state that had changed. (b) Report the whole of A1 as complete, since the suite ran. (c) Rewrite D192 in place. | D192 was accurate when written and is not any more: the suite has since run in a real browser, green, and `e2e/README.md` §8 already recorded that outcome — so the log was the only artifact still calling it unperformed, in exactly the document every other file defers to. (b) would be the same error in the other direction: `use.trace`, `use.screenshot` and `use.video` are all failure-conditional, so a green run exercises none of them, and the artifact-on-failure claim remains unobserved. Splitting the step into A1 and A1-b is what lets each half be reported at its own true state. (c) is forbidden by D190. | Four items now carry three different states — performed, configured-but-unobserved, and open — so a reader who skims §7 can still overstate one. The §7.3 honesty statement enumerates what may and may not be claimed, rather than leaving it to inference. |
| D266 | **[Superseded by D308 — the construction failure is now asserted in a real browser by `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts` test 4, so the "unverified" classification below is the decision as originally taken, not the shipped state. This row's reasons for refusing the shims and the production fix still stand.]** **Refines D92.** Classify the missing-`Chart.register` construction failure as a **documented, unverified ceiling** rather than as behaviour the suite asserts, and say so in every artifact that had claimed coverage of it: the E2E skip reason, `frontend/TESTING.md`, `e2e/README.md`, `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §3.3 and §7.3, `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §F and §G, and the executive deck. | (a) Keep the claim, which was false in four documents at once. (b) Add the canvas and `ResizeObserver` shims so a Jest case can reach the registration failure. (c) Make `e2e/harness/stubs/analyticsService.ts` resolve so the browser layer reaches it. (d) Call `Chart.register` in production. | Nothing executing observes it, and the two layers fail short of it for different reasons: under jsdom there is no 2D canvas context and Chart.js reports that *before* any registry lookup, while the browser layer has a context but the harness stub rejects on every path so `renderCharts` is never reached. (b) is refused on the measured evidence in D92 — with both shims Chart.js reaches `"linear" is not a registered scale`, which escapes to React and unmounts the component, trading three assertions for none; AAP §0.6.2 records the same measurement and AAP §0.7.3 directs that the caught-failure path be asserted instead. (c) throws out of the subject's `useEffect` and unmounts the route, which is what `UnrenderableTrendSeriesError` in that stub exists to record. (d) is production code outside the two authorized touches. So the honest move is to name what *is* asserted — the caught transport failure, and the real library entered once with jsdom's context notice caught and the component still mounted — and label the registration failure unverified. | A ceiling that is documented rather than asserted can be mistaken for an oversight by a later reader. It is recorded as a suggested next task in `e2e/README.md` section 9 and root `README.md`, naming `Chart.register` as the one-line production change that closes it. |
| D267 | **Corrects the version boundary in D181.** State that `@playwright/test` **1.45.0** is the first release declaring `node>=18`, not 1.45.3, and that the current line — 1.62.1 — declares `node>=20`. | (a) Leave 1.45.3 in place. (b) Drop version boundaries from the prose and cite only the pin. | Re-measured from package metadata: 1.44.1 declares `node>=16`, 1.45.0 declares `node>=18`, and every release after it does too, so 1.45.3 was a true-but-not-tight statement that understated how narrow the 1.44.1 ceiling is. The distance to the current line matters as well, because the upgrade a remediated downloader would need is two Node majors away, not one. (b) would remove the fact that makes D111's and D264's reasoning checkable. | The "current line" figure ages with every Playwright release. It is dated in `e2e/README.md` where it appears in prose, and the pin it justifies — 1.44.1 — does not move with it. |
| D268 | **Extends D265, and closes the deck half of D192.** Report the Rule 4 deck render as **performed**, on a headless-Chrome reading at a 1920×1080 viewport recorded in `docs/testing/DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.4, and add Subresource Integrity digests plus `crossorigin="anonymous"` to the four pinned `cdn.jsdelivr.net` assets the deck loads. | (a) Report the render on the static checks alone — valid HTML, balanced CSS, sixteen `<section>` elements, diagrams parsed out of band. (b) Add the digests without rendering, trusting them. (c) Render without adding the digests, keeping the two concerns apart. (d) Inline all four assets into the file, removing the CDN dependency and the need for digests. | Rule 4 asks for a browser render specifically, and the static checks cannot answer the questions it is really asking: whether Mermaid's `startOnLoad: false` plus `mermaid.run()`-on-`slidechanged` pipeline actually produces SVG on every slide, whether `lucide.createIcons()` replaces every placeholder, and whether any slide ends up text-only once rendered. Doing both at once was deliberate rather than convenient: a wrong digest makes Chrome **block** the asset, so the render is also the test of the digests, and (b) would have shipped four unproven hashes while (c) would have needed a second render later anyway. The reading is unambiguous — 16 sections, 5 of 5 diagrams as inline SVG with no error box and no raw block left, 19 of 19 icons with zero leftover `<i data-lucide>`, a non-text visual on every slide, all four assets HTTP 200 with digests accepted and `decodedBodySize` matching the bytes hashed out of band, and zero console messages at any level across two loads. (d) would put ~3 MB of third-party code in the repository and break the version-pin-by-URL contract Rule 4 sets. | The digests pin four exact builds, so changing any pinned version now requires regenerating the matching hash or the deck silently loses that asset — the failure is loud in the console but invisible in a diff review. The render evidence also lives in gitignored screenshots rather than in the repository, so it is reproducible rather than archived; §7.4 states the viewport and the walk needed to reproduce it. |
| D269 | **Extends D265 and D268, and closes the two acceptance items D265 left open.** Report the **failure-artifact half of A1** and the **complete negative-validation sweep A3** as performed: an induced-failure run whose readings are recorded in `docs/testing/DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.2 A1-b, and a sixteen-module perturbation sweep recorded per case in §7.3. Drop the "configured-but-unobserved" qualifier from §4 and §7.3 that those two items required. | (a) Leave both open, which is what D265 recorded and what the artifacts deferring to it would keep asserting. (b) Demonstrate the failure artifacts by breaking a shipped spec rather than adding a throwaway one. (c) Run the negative sweep on a sample — one module per layer — rather than one per critical-path module. (d) Commit the perturbations behind a skip so the exercise is archived rather than described. | Both items were open for a stated reason and both reasons are now discharged, so leaving them open would repeat the error D265 was written to correct, in the other direction. (b) risks a shipped spec being left perturbed; a spec that exists only to fail is easier to prove gone than a real one is to prove restored, and this one carried the `blitzy_adhoc_test_` prefix, was never committed, and `npx playwright test --list` returning to 12 tests in 4 files is that proof. (c) would have missed the two cases most worth having: N08, where moving a boundary by a single unit was still caught, and N12, where the perturbation also showed the msw guard answering an un-allow-listed URL with HTTP 599 rather than letting it out. (d) is refused by the AAP's own instruction that the perturbation be described rather than committed, and by the reliability constraint against committed dead tests. All sixteen restored byte-exactly — bytes hashed before, restored after, digests compared, a mismatch aborting the sweep — and `git status` then reported every one of the sixteen paths clean. | The sweep is evidence of a moment, not a standing gate: nothing re-runs it, so a later edit that weakens an oracle will not be caught by it. Repeating it is a scripted exercise rather than a CI step, which is why §7.3 records the method in enough detail to repeat rather than reinvent. |
## §32 Code-review remediation — the final completeness and arithmetic checkpoint

Scope: the thirteen findings raised at the final completeness checkpoint — one critical (the `e2e` CI job
provisioned a browser with the downloader this delivery's own policy forbids and then invoked the runner
from a root that owns none), nine major (eleven file operations beyond the frozen transformation map, an
arithmetic claim the map cannot support, a manifest that had drifted from the frozen dependency table, a
missing Jest preset, an ignore file reaching past its authorized scope, three status artifacts left stale
against evidence that had since been produced, an unperformed deck render, an unrecorded negative-validation
ledger, and onboarding that contradicted the end-to-end guide) and three minor (one wrong count and two
stray blank lines at end of file).

Two conventions govern this section, both following D190. Rows already shipped are preserved **verbatim**
even where this remediation removed the file a row names; where that happened, the row below says so and
supersedes it by id, which is the same mechanism D192 established. The **Document coverage** index at the
top of this log is a navigation table rather than a row, so its file lists are corrected to the paths that
exist after this checkpoint.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D270 | **Supersedes D163 and restores the pin D100 and D125 set.** Return `python-jose[cryptography]` to the frozen plan's **3.3.0**, install 3.3.0 into `.venv-backend` so the manifest and the environment agree at that version, and record the two advisories that motivated the earlier 3.5.0 substitution here instead of in the manifest. | (a) Keep 3.5.0 and argue the security exception at review. (b) Keep 3.5.0 and amend the frozen plan. (c) Add a `constraints.txt` pinning the transitive `cryptography` instead. | The frozen dependency table in the plan is the agreed contract for this manifest, and a delivery cannot silently choose a different one — the substitution was never sanctioned by an exception, which is precisely what the finding says. (b) is not this checkpoint's to make: the plan is frozen, so the amendment is a decision for whoever unfreezes it, and it is recorded as a suggested next task in `backend/tests/README.md` instead. (c) adds a manifest the file's own contract forbids. The exposure is bounded here: CVE-2024-33663 (algorithm and key confusion) and CVE-2024-33664 (compressed-JWE decompression bomb) are both reachable only through JWT **verification** of attacker-supplied tokens, and no implemented route verifies one — `app/api/dependencies.py` fails closed on a `verify_token` symbol that does not exist, and the suite's only JWT work is `create_access_token` encoding under a test key. | The manifest now names a version with two known advisories. Anyone who implements the missing auth route inherits them, so raising the pin belongs in the same change as that route. |
| D271 | Remove `grpcio` and `grpcio-status` as **direct** pins, leaving grpcio to arrive transitively through `google-cloud-firestore`. | (a) Keep both pins. (b) Keep `grpcio` and drop `grpcio-status`. (c) Move both to a comment naming the versions the guard was verified against. | The frozen table declares 23 distributions and neither of these is among them; a direct pin the plan does not authorize is scope expansion whatever its motive. The egress guard that motivated them is unaffected — `grpc` and `grpc.aio` are still importable because Firestore requires grpcio, and the guard's own probes still pass, verified at grpcio 1.80.0 / grpcio-status 1.62.3 after the pins were removed. (c) would put rationale in the manifest, which Rule 1 bars there. | A future resolver could pick a grpcio release the channel-factory guard has not been exercised against; the guard patches by module path, so a rename upstream would silently narrow it. |
| D272 | **Supersedes D101.** Delete `backend/tests/test_dependency_closure.py` and let the manifest be enforced by installing from it rather than by asserting about it. | (a) Keep the file and argue it into scope. (b) Fold its 26 cases into `tests/unit/test_core_config.py`. (c) Keep the file but stop documenting it. | It is one of the eleven paths outside the frozen 85-path map, and the map is what this checkpoint measures. (b) would put environment assertions in a suite whose subject is `Settings`, breaking the one-suite-per-production-module rule the plan sets and leaving the count unchanged in the only place it matters. Nothing production-facing is lost: the file asserted installed-versions-equal-manifest-pins, which `pip install -r backend/requirements-dev.txt` establishes directly, and which `ci.yml` runs on every push. | An environment patched in place after installing can now drift from the manifest without a test noticing. `pip check` does not catch it, as the manifest section of `backend/tests/README.md` still warns. |
| D273 | **Supersedes D140, D158, D172, D174's frontend half and D173.** Delete the five `frontend/src/test-utils/*.test.ts` contract suites, and move `importWithConfiguredBase` out of its own module into `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.ts`, beside the `CONFIGURED_BASE_URL` it applies. | (a) Keep all six files and argue them into scope. (b) Keep the five suites and only fold the loader in. (c) Fold the five suites' cases into the subject suites that use the harness. | Six of the eleven paths outside the frozen map are these, and `frontend/src/test-utils/**` carries no suite in the map — the map allocates exactly one suite per subject module. Nothing production-facing is lost, measured rather than assumed: `collectCoverageFrom` already excluded `src/test-utils/**`, and the whole denominator, every gated scope and every per-file figure are byte-identical before and after (76.27% / 87.5% / 70.14% / 76.15%, `src/schema` 100%, `src/services` 100%, `src/store` 97.43%). (c) would move assertions about a transformer, a reporter template and a manifest into suites whose subject is a slice or a component, which is the coupling the one-suite-per-module rule exists to prevent. The loader is a different case from the five — it is imported by two required suites, so its behaviour had to survive, and `handlers.ts` already owned the constant it sets and was already cited by it in both directions. | The transformer, the reporter templates, the msw handler factories and the setup lifecycle no longer have a suite whose only subject is them: a regression in any of the four now surfaces as a failure in a subject suite, where the message names the symptom rather than the contract. `frontend/TESTING.md` §3 says which suite tells you about which. |
| D274 | Declare `preset: 'ts-jest'` in `frontend/jest.config.js` alongside the explicit two-entry `transform` map. | (a) Leave the preset out, as the file did, on the ground that the explicit map supersedes it. (b) Drop the explicit primary transform entry and let the preset supply it. | The file's own contract requires the key, and a delivery does not get to decide a prescribed key is redundant. It is also not inert: it is the declaration a reader checks first, and Jest's preset merge replaces the preset's `transform` with the one declared here, so the extension-less entry keeps its precedence — verified by re-running the suite (identical results, 18 test files listed, none under `src/test-utils/`) and by `jest --showConfig`, which reports no unknown option and no validation warning. (b) would forfeit the inline `tsconfig` and `diagnostics: false` that the preset does not carry, and the sources do not typecheck. | Two declarations now describe the same transform, and a future editor who deletes the explicit map will silently lose `diagnostics: false`, the inline `tsconfig` and the extension-less entry. |
| D275 | **Supersedes D159 and restores D144.** Delete `frontend/.npmrc` and `e2e/.npmrc`, so a `package-lock.json` an `npm install` writes is visible to `git status` again rather than suppressed at source. | (a) Keep both files. (b) Keep them and add a `package-lock.json` ignore rule instead. (c) Keep them only in `e2e`. | Both are paths outside the frozen map, and neither is needed for the posture they served: D93's decision is to commit no lockfile, which is a decision about what gets committed, not about what npm may write. (b) is what D144 refused and D159 already replaced — an ignore rule is the one mechanism that would hide a supply-chain change from review. | `npm install` in either package now writes a lockfile that shows as untracked, so a contributor must decide about it deliberately — which is the intent, but it is one more thing to notice. |
| D276 | **Supersedes D130.** Delete `e2e/harness-origin.ts` and resolve the host, port and origin in a "Harness origin" section of `e2e/vite.harness.config.ts`, which exports `BASE_PORT`, `HARNESS_HOST`, `HARNESS_PORT` and `HARNESS_ORIGIN`; `e2e/playwright.config.ts` imports all three values from there and resolves none of its own. | (a) Keep the module and argue it into scope. (b) Put the resolution in `playwright.config.ts` and have the Vite config import it from the runner. (c) Duplicate the resolution in both configs. (d) Read the port from an environment variable in each config with no shared code. | It is one of the eleven paths outside the frozen 85-path map, and the map contains exactly two config files for this layer, so the resolution has to live in one of them. The Vite config is the one that binds the socket, and it is also the only choice that keeps `npm run harness` — `node ./node_modules/vite/bin/vite.js --config vite.harness.config.ts`, per D123 — free of `@playwright/test`: under (b) starting the server on its own would load the runner, and Vite bundles its config with esbuild, so the runner would be pulled into the harness path for nothing. (c) and (d) reintroduce exactly the disagreement D130 removed. Verified end to end: `--list` reports 12 tests in 4 files, and the full suite is 11 passed / 1 skipped in real Chrome on port 4194, the value `CLONE_INDEX=21` resolves. | Loading `playwright.config.ts` now evaluates the whole Vite config module — including `require('vite')` and `require('@vitejs/plugin-react')` — in the runner process and in every worker. A future error at that module's top level would fail the runner rather than only the server, and `--list`, which needs no server at all, would fail with it. |
| D277 | **Supersedes D131 as to where the fixture lives; its design is unchanged.** Delete `e2e/tests/harness-fixtures.ts` and declare the extended `test`/`expect`, the automatic `noEgress` fixture, `HARNESS_API_PATHS` and both ledgers in a "Spec fixtures" section of `e2e/playwright.config.ts`; the four specs import `{ expect, HARNESS_ORIGIN, test, unInterceptedApiRequestUrls }` from `'../playwright.config'`. | (a) Keep the module and argue it into scope. (b) Move it into the Vite config beside the origin. (c) Give each spec its own copy of the fixture. (d) Move it into one of the four specs and import across specs. | It is another of the eleven unplanned paths, and the runner config is where the rest of the isolation contract already lives: the browser-level `--host-resolver-rules`, `--proxy-server` and `--proxy-bypass-list` of D114 and D128 are declared there, and the context-wide route this fixture installs is the layer directly above them, so one file now carries the whole contract. It is safely importable from a spec because `testMatch` is the recursive `.spec.ts` glob under `testDir`, and this file sits outside that directory — proven by `--list`, which reports 12 tests in exactly the 4 spec files. (b) would make the dev server depend on the runner, which D276 exists to avoid. (c) is the copy-paste the plan's shared-setup rule forbids. (d) makes one spec's collection a precondition for another's. | A spec author now imports test infrastructure from a file named `playwright.config.ts`, which reads as configuration rather than as fixtures; the section banner and `e2e/README.md` sections 3 and 8 are what signal otherwise. The fixture's module-level ledgers are also now in the same module graph as the config object, so a worker that fails to load the config reports it as a config error rather than a fixture error. |
| D278 | **Diverges from the `ci.yml` file contract, deliberately and on the record.** Replace the `e2e` job's `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` step with a preflight that resolves an already-installed browser — honouring a pre-set `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`, else the first of `google-chrome`, `google-chrome-stable`, `chromium`, `chromium-browser` on `PATH` — fails the job with a diagnostic when none is executable, prints the resolved build's `--version`, runs `npm run browsers:verify`, and exports the path through `$GITHUB_ENV`; and change the run step to `working-directory: e2e` with `npm test`. | (a) Leave the job as the contract prescribes and report the concern only. (b) Delete the browser step and let Playwright's cache supply the browser implicitly. (c) Add `working-directory: e2e` to the run step but keep the downloader. (d) Pin the browser by committing an archive. | The prescribed form cannot work and is the checkpoint's only critical finding: there is no manifest and no runner at the repository root, so `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` resolves nothing local and reaches the registry for an unpinned Playwright — the failure mode `e2e/README.md` section 3 already documents — and the downloader is the exact code path D111 and D129 refused, since `@playwright/test` 1.44.1's downloader does not verify the TLS chain of the host it fetches from (CVE-2025-59288). Leaving it would also make the delivery's own no-downloader policy false in the one place it is executed. (b) is silent: a missing cache would fail deep inside the runner instead of at a named step. (c) fixes half of one finding and leaves the download. (d) is what D111 rejected. Both branches of the preflight were executed under `bash -eo pipefail`: with nothing resolvable it prints both diagnostics and exits 1 without contacting the network, and with a `google-chrome` on `PATH` it prints the build, completes `browsers:verify` with no fetch, and writes `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE=<path>` to the file `$GITHUB_ENV` names. The workflow still parses to exactly two jobs, `build` and `e2e`, with no `needs` between them, and no lint, type-check, matrix, `env:`, caching or action-pin line was touched. | The job now depends on the runner image carrying a browser. On `ubuntu-latest` that holds today — Google Chrome and Chromium are both preinstalled — but an image change or a self-hosted runner without one turns a passing job into a hard failure at the preflight, which is the intended trade and still a change in behaviour. The resolved build is also whatever the image ships rather than the version Playwright pins, so a browser-version regression is possible in a way a pinned download would not allow. |
| D279 | Bring the two onboarding documents into line with the workflow: `README.md`'s end-to-end install leg installs the runner only and states the browser prerequisite separately, its run table uses `npm test` and `npm run report`, `e2e/README.md` section 3 names `working-directory: e2e` with `npm test` as the CI form, and section 9's item recording the `ci.yml` mismatch as a known gap is removed because the gap is closed. | (a) Change `ci.yml` only and leave both documents. (b) Change the documents only and leave `ci.yml`. (c) Point `README.md` at `e2e/README.md` without stating the prerequisite. | Rule 3 asks for one clean-machine path, and the finding is precisely that three documents described three different ones. Leaving item 17 in place after fixing the job would leave the guide asserting a defect that no longer exists, which is the same class of error as the finding itself. (c) would make the root README unusable on its own for the one step that cannot be inferred — that no command here installs a browser. | Two documents now state the browser prerequisite, so a future change to the provisioning policy has to touch both plus `ci.yml`; `e2e/README.md` section 2 remains the single normative statement, and both other places point at it. |
| D280 | **Supersedes D189.** Reduce `.gitignore` to the 18 patterns its own contract authorizes — the 15 required artifact and dependency-tree rules plus `.venv-backend/`, `venv/` and `*.py[cod]` — under five terse grouping labels, and move every other rule to this clone's `.git/info/exclude`, which is local and uncommitted. | (a) Keep the file as shipped and argue the extra rules as hygiene. (b) Keep the build-output and editor rules only, dropping the rest. (c) Delete the extra rules with no local replacement. (d) Keep the narrative header and the lockfile essay while trimming the patterns. | The file's stated scope is test artifacts and dependency trees, and 17 rules sat outside it: `*$py.class`, `.mypy_cache/`, `.ruff_cache/`, `.coverage.*`, `/reports/`, `playwright/.cache/`, `blitzy/screenshots/`, `blitzy/screen_recordings/`, `dist/`, `build/`, `.vite/`, `.venv/`, `env/`, `ENV/`, `.DS_Store`, `Thumbs.db`, `*.swp`. Measured, none of them was ignoring anything that exists: after the narrowing, `git status --porcelain` reports no untracked entry other than this session's own scratch files. (c) would leave a contributor's editor and build noise in `git status`, which is a real cost with no offsetting benefit, and `.git/info/exclude` is git's own mechanism for exactly that. (d) leaves the Rule 1 violation in place — the header narrated the file's purpose and the lockfile block argued a decision, both of which belong here. Both mandatory checks in the file's contract pass: 25 committed producers are **not** ignored, and 21 generated artifacts **are**, each matched by a line in `.gitignore` itself rather than by the local exclude. | The removed rules now protect only this clone. Another contributor's `dist/`, `.mypy_cache/` or `.DS_Store` will show as untracked until they add their own local excludes or a future change extends the committed file with a decision to match. A partial `.coverage.*` file left by an interrupted `pytest -n auto` is also only locally excluded. |
| D281 | **Completes acceptance step A3, which `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` recorded as deferred.** Demonstrate the negative validations as **one perturbation per critical-path module across all 28 of them** — 14 backend and 14 frontend — each applied by a scripted harness that hashes the file, refuses to run unless the target string occurs exactly once, runs only the named test, restores the original bytes in a `finally` block, requires the digest to match, and re-runs the test to green; and record the whole ledger in `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.3 rather than summarising it. | (a) Perturb one threshold on each side of the stack, as the plan's wording literally allows. (b) Add a mutation-testing tool (`mutmut`, Stryker) and report a survivor score. (c) Commit the perturbations behind a feature flag so the demonstration is reproducible. (d) Describe the exercise in the completion summary only, with no ledger in the repository. | A finding was raised precisely because the exercise had not been performed, so a two-perturbation reading of it would answer the letter and miss the point: the claim worth making is that **every** gated module's suite is sensitive to its own module, and only a per-module ledger supports that. (b) is a new dependency for a programme whose dependency set is frozen, and a survivor score answers a different question. (c) violates the plan's explicit instruction that the perturbation must not be committed. (d) leaves the evidence outside the repository, where the next reader cannot check it. Two perturbations that did **not** fail their first-choice test are recorded with the reason rather than quietly re-rolled — the singleton-versus-declared-default distinction in `test_core_config.py`, and the fact that including a route-less router is unobservable through the app object. | The ledger is a point-in-time record: it was produced against this commit and nothing re-runs it, so a later change that weakens an oracle will not invalidate the table automatically. It is also one probe per module rather than a survivor analysis, which §7.3 states in full so the figure is not read as a mutation score. |
| D282 | **Supersedes D192.** Perform both browser acceptance steps and record what was observed: run the Playwright suite in Chrome 151 against the harness, induce one failure to prove the trace, screenshot and video actually appear, and render `blitzy-deck/executive-summary.html` in Chrome at 1920×1080 — then rewrite `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.1, §7.2 and §7.5 to the evidenced position, listing the terms the evidence does and does not cover. | (a) Leave D192 standing and note the runs elsewhere. (b) Report the suite as verified without inducing a failure, since it passes. (c) Delete the honesty statement now that browser evidence exists. (d) Perform the deck render only, as the narrower of the two. | D192's reasoning was that reporting an unperformed step would put an unfalsifiable claim in the artifact every other file defers to; that reasoning is unchanged and is exactly why the reverse is now required — leaving it standing would make the dashboard understate what is known, which is the same defect with the sign flipped. (b) would leave the one clause of A1 that matters untested: a suite that never fails never exercises `retain-on-failure`, and the induced failure is what turned three configured paths into three files on disk (`trace.zip` 34,437 B, `test-failed-1.png` 6,331 B, `video.webm` 14,432 B), after which the spec was restored byte-identical and the suite re-ran green. (c) throws away the part still worth keeping: the run is **chromium only**, the skipped chart spec stays unreachable, and the deck render checks structure rather than wording — §7.5 now states each of those limits instead of a blanket disclaimer. | The evidence is a point-in-time observation on one browser build. Chrome on the runner image will move, and nothing re-runs the deck render, so a later CSS or CDN change could break a diagram without any gate noticing — the deck has no automated check of its own. |
| D283 | Restate `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` so its counting units are explicit rather than implied: keep every surviving row id **stable**, delete the ten §F rows whose only subject was a removed artifact, fold each removed row's still-live obligation into the survivor that now carries it (F23 gains `importWithConfiguredBase`, F31 the spec fixtures, F32 the clone-specific origin), and replace the bare row count with the row-versus-path arithmetic — 79 §F rows naming 78 of the 85 paths directly, plus F16's three brace-written route modules, plus the change set's four deletions dispositioned in §E and §I, which is 85. Rewrite the four §H rows whose evidence lived in a deleted suite to name the surviving mechanism and say plainly where a property is no longer held by a dedicated assertion. | (a) Renumber §F to F1–F79 so the ids are contiguous. (b) Leave the deleted rows in place with a "removed" marker. (c) Leave the count at 89 and add a footnote. (d) Delete the rows and leave the four §H rows citing their deleted suites, since the negative validations they record did happen. | The finding was that a stated total could not be reconciled with the change set, so the fix has to make the reconciliation checkable rather than restate a different number: the arithmetic is now printed with its units, and it was verified programmatically — 79 rows, 78 required paths named in the artifact column, the 7 unnamed being the four deletions and F16's three, zero duplicate ids, zero unresolvable id citations, and every remaining backticked path either present on disk or a URL, a bare specifier or an ignore pattern. (a) is cosmetic and unsafe: about 55 F-id citations live inside this document — §E, the touch note, §I and the tail — and renumbering rewrites correct text to gain contiguity, so the ten gaps are declared in the framing instead. (b) leaves a reader following a row to a file that is not there. (c) is the defect. (d) would publish "N assertions in `<file>`" for files a reader cannot open; the rewritten rows keep the negative-validation history explicitly as history and separate it from what is enforced today. | Ten id gaps are now a permanent feature of §F, so a future reader may read a gap as an omission rather than as a removal; the framing paragraph names all ten and why. And four §H guarantees now rest on structural enforcement rather than a named assertion — the mechanisms are unchanged and every suite still runs through them, but a regression that weakens one without any suite happening to exercise the weakened path would no longer be named by a failing test. Each of the four rows says so in its own words. |

## §33 Reconciled decisions — where a proposed deletion met a live dependency, and the arithmetic that followed

Scope: the eight decisions taken while bringing the whole delivery into one state. Four of them reverse a
proposed deletion, because the artifact the row would remove is named as a live reference by an onboarding
document, by the traceability matrix, or by another module’s `import`; one accepts half of a row and reverses
the other half; one refines a wording decision; one collapses two remediations of the same defect into a single
declaration; and the last is the arithmetic that follows from keeping what was kept. Every reversal is recorded here rather than applied silently, and each names the row it supersedes,
following D190’s convention. The deletions that stand — `e2e/harness-origin.ts`, `frontend/.npmrc`,
`e2e/.npmrc` and `frontend/src/test-utils/configured-base.ts` — are not restated; D275, D276 and D285 are
their record.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D284 | **Supersedes D272.** Retain `backend/tests/test_dependency_closure.py`. | (a) Apply D272 and delete it. (b) Delete it and assert the manifest from `pytest.ini` instead. | The gate is named as a live reference by `README.md`’s command table and by `backend/tests/README.md`’s suite map, and it is the only artifact that fails when the installed interpreter drifts from the pinned manifest — a drift this delivery actually produced, with `python-jose` 3.5.0 installed against a manifest pinning 3.3.0. Deleting it removes the only automatic detector of that condition. (b) puts assertion logic in a configuration file. | A suite that asserts about its own environment rather than about production. Bounded by it testing only the manifest-to-environment relation, and by it being excluded from the coverage denominator. |
