(c) Report all twelve percentages in the KPI cell rather than the worst plus the failing ones. (d) Drop the lcov read and print no uncovered-line columns. | The two halves are one defect with one cause — a figure that reads as measured while nothing measured it. `--require-all` exists so an absent artifact is fatal, and lcov was the sole source of every uncovered-line column while being the one artifact the contract did not name, so the flag could exit 0 with those columns blank. K2 had the mirror problem: Jest declares one `coverageThreshold` group per path, so a run is failed by the *worst group*, and `src/store` at 70% beside two packages at 100% aggregates to 98.57% — a published pass for a run Jest fails. (c) puts twelve numbers in a KPI cell nobody can scan; the cell now names the worst and then names every gate below the threshold, which is the information a reader needs and nothing more. (d) discards the per-file diagnosis the dashboard exists to carry. Usability is checked as well as presence, because an lcov with no `SF:`/`DA:` pair is present and measures nothing. Verified by 26 new cases in `backend/tests/test_dashboard_extract.py`, including the aggregate-hides-a-failure oracle stated in both directions. | The KPI cell no longer shows all four metrics at a glance, so a reader wanting the per-metric spread has to read §6.2. `lcov_is_usable()` also reads the whole artifact a second time, which is a few milliseconds on a file this size and would matter on a much larger tree. |
| D333 | **Closes finding M5. Applied to the `test:load` script D298 declares, which is the same run under the name the delivered manifest uses.** Make the frontend readiness gate `npm run test:load` — `jest --ci --watchAll=false --runInBand --reporters=default -t "__readiness_probe_that_matches_no_test__"` — retain its output at `frontend/reports/load-tests.txt` from its own `ci.yml` step, parse it with `jest_readiness()`, and keep `test:list` as the discovery census it actually is, in its own step retained at `frontend/reports/list-tests.txt`. | (a) Replace `test:list` with the readiness run and keep one script. (b) Make the readiness gate `jest --ci --coverage` — a full run — since that also loads every module. (c) Use `--onlyFailures` or `--findRelatedTests` to load without running. (d) Assert importability from a committed test that requires every test module. | `--listTests` enumerates the files that match discovery and resolves no import, so a module that throws while loading appears in its output and the step passes — which is what the finding says and what `frontend/TESTING.md` already admitted in its own §1. The name-filtered run loads and transforms all 24 modules, executes no test body, and exits non-zero on a load failure: measured at exit 1 with `1 failed, 24 skipped, 1 of 25 total` against a deliberately unresolvable import, where `test:list` exited 0 on the same tree. (a) loses the census, which `--list`-style output is genuinely good for and which the dashboard cross-checks against the result total. (b) is the 100-second `test:ci` run, so readiness would stop being a cheap early signal and a failure would be attributed to the suite rather than to loading. (c) does not load an unrelated module. (d) would need a second module graph maintained by hand. `--reporters=default` is mandatory: without it the readiness run overwrites `reports/jest-junit.xml` with a 370-case all-skipped stream, which is the stub-refusal defect H17 records in another layer. | Two frontend readiness artifacts now exist and a reader has to know which answers which question; `frontend/TESTING.md` §1 and §9 and `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §6.4 each name both. The readiness run also costs about 13 seconds of transform work that `test:ci` then repeats, because the two runs do not share a Jest cache entry for the name filter. |
| D334 | **Closes finding M1, and is the remediation of conflict C2.** Reduce every comment in every changed code and configuration file to descriptive documentation — what the code does, what its contract is, what the production code under test does — plus a pointer to this log; and move every statement of an alternative, of why a choice was made, and of what a choice costs into this log. The terse `# TESTING:` markers on the two authorized production touches are untouched. | (a) Leave the comments and argue that a self-documenting test suite is the better reading of Rule 1. (b) Move only the explicit "(a)/(b) alternatives" passages and keep the "because" clauses. (c) Delete the flagged prose outright without recording anything here. (d) Convert the prose into `@see` pointers with no descriptive replacement. | Rule 1 makes this log the single source of truth for "why" and forbids that content in implementation comments, and the finding measured 41 changed files carrying it, so (a) is a re-argument of a rule rather than a fix. (b) leaves the same class of statement in place under different punctuation — the flagged text in `.coveragerc`, `.gitignore` and `pytest.ini` was almost entirely "X because a Y would Z", not enumerated alternatives. (c) would lose reasoning that nothing else records; every clause removed here is either already carried by an existing row or is carried by a row added in this section. (d) would leave a reader of the code unable to tell what a construct does without opening another document, which is the descriptive documentation the rule explicitly permits. The measurement used to close it ran in two passes over the 91 changed non-Markdown files. Pass one counted comment lines carrying a decision-flavoured marker — `deliberately`, `on purpose`, `the risk`, `the cost`, `alternatives`, `the choice`, `was chosen`, `previously`, `an earlier revision`, `why this`, `papering over` — which fell from 101 across 37 files to **0**. Pass two counted the scope-authorization form that pass one does not match — “which is production code this programme is not authorized to change, the two authorized touches are both in `backend/`” — which stood at seven sites (`frontend/src/components/Dashboard.test.tsx`, `TweetManagement.test.tsx`, `Analytics.test.tsx` and `Configuration.test.tsx`, `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts` and `configuration.spec.ts`, and `backend/tests/integration/test_route_surface.py`) and is now **0**; each site keeps the factual statement of the ceiling and its pointer to `frontend/TESTING.md` and the traceability matrix. The counterfactual `would`-clauses that remain describe runtime behaviour and what an assertion catches, not decisions. | Two costs, both real. A reader of a config file no longer sees why it is shaped that way without following the pointer, and the pointer is a relative path that a future move would break. And the boundary between "descriptive" and "argumentative" is a judgement: a sentence such as "`localhost` resolves to either `127.0.0.1` or `::1` depending on the host's resolver order" states a fact that happens to justify the adjacent literal, and it is kept on the ground that the fact is what a reader needs. A later reviewer drawing the line differently will find borderline cases. |
| D335 | **Closes finding m1.** Reject a candidate browser path carrying CR or LF in `isLaunchable()` before the filesystem is consulted, and publish `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` to `$GITHUB_ENV` in GitHub's delimited multi-line form — `NAME<<ghenv_<32 hex>`, the value, then the delimiter — regenerating the delimiter if it occurs in the value. | (a) Keep the `NAME=value` form and strip or escape CR and LF from the value. (b) Keep `NAME=value` and refuse only when the value carries a newline. (c) Use the delimited form with a fixed delimiter string. (d) Stop publishing to `$GITHUB_ENV` and let every later step resolve the browser itself. | The `NAME=value` form terminates at the first newline, so a value carrying one appends a second assignment to the environment file and every later step of the job inherits it; because `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` is caller-supplied, that is an environment-injection primitive rather than a formatting defect. (a) rests on escaping a form that has no escape syntax. (b) closes the assignment-form injection but still publishes a value the delimited form splits across lines. (c) is injectable by any value containing the fixed delimiter. (d) forfeits what the preflight exists for — one resolution shared by the whole job. Measured: with `GITHUB_ENV` pointed at a scratch file and a real Chrome path, the file receives the three-line delimited block; with `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` set to a real path followed by a newline and `GITHUB_TOKEN=pwn`, the script exits 1 and no `GITHUB_ENV` file is created. | The delimiter is drawn from `crypto.randomBytes`, so a platform CSPRNG failure throws instead of publishing — a hard failure rather than a silent weakening. The CR/LF rejection is absolute: a path legitimately containing a line break, which neither filesystem in use here permits, would be refused rather than quoted. |
| D336 | **Closes the counting half of finding M2.** Re-derive every arithmetic claim the Rule 1 and Rule 2 documents make, in `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py`, instead of maintaining the figures by hand: the `.gitignore` census and its anchored/unanchored split, the manifest pin census and the case arithmetic built on it, the §F row count in two places, the withdrawn ids, id uniqueness, path ownership in both directions, the completeness-table arithmetic, the layer labels and `REQUIRED` artifacts the extractor emits, the deck's `<section>` count, that every document quoting the measured commit quotes the same one, and that the dashboard's retention panel lists every `upload-artifact` the workflow performs. | (a) Correct the seven wrong figures and leave them hand-maintained. (b) Generate the whole of §F from a filesystem walk at build time. (c) Put the check in `docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py`, which already reads these documents' inputs. (d) Add a lint step to `ci.yml` rather than a test. | Every figure the finding named was correct when written and wrong when read, which is the signature of a hand-maintained count rather than of carelessness, so (a) fixes the instances and not the class — and this checkpoint's own code changes invalidated four more of them while it was in progress. (b) would make the census a build product and lose the one thing §F carries that a walk cannot: what each artifact *covers*. (c) would put a documentation assertion in the producer whose own output the documentation describes, and the extractor is already the subject of `test_dashboard_extract.py`. (d) runs outside the suite that gates the branch. The suite form also pays immediately: it found the frontend readiness artifact named by the extractor's `REQUIRED` contract and by `ci.yml` but by no panel of the dashboard, and it found itself unowned by §F. Measured: 19 cases, all passing; five of them failed on first run against the delivered documents. | The module reads the filesystem and never invokes `git`, because the conftest guards refuse a child process while a test runs. So the three change-set figures only `git diff` can produce are checked for internal consistency — entries + 1 = operations = distinct paths, and distinct paths − named paths = the four stated exceptions — rather than re-derived: a coordinated error in all three would pass. The provenance case likewise proves the documents agree on one hash, not that the hash names a commit that exists. |
| D337 | **Closes finding M7. Supersedes the per-slide figures D205 and D219 recorded.** Compress all eight content slides and the closing slide of `blitzy-deck/executive-summary.html` under Rule 4's caps, and make the caps a **test** rather than a claim: `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` re-measures every slide and fails when one drifts over. | (a) Compress by hand again and record the new counts in this log, which is what D205 and D219 did. (b) Split the over-long slides across more sections, up to Rule 4's eighteen-section ceiling. (c) Move the surplus into `<aside class="notes">` speaker notes. (d) Read the cap as excluding table cells, KPI labels and diagram labels, and change nothing. | The delivered file measured 47, 102, 39, 132, 40, 151, 121 and 65 body words while D205's own risk cell claimed 28, 32, 32, 37, 29, 37, 35 and 35: the log had drifted from the file and nothing re-derived either figure, so a stale count read exactly like a current one. (a) reproduces that failure mode. (b) is arithmetically closed - sixteen of the eighteen sections are already used, three slides needed splitting, and splitting to satisfy a word cap is padding. (c) hides the reading burden instead of removing it, because a note is text in the same file. (d) is contradicted by the review, which counted them: four candidate tokenisations bracket its published figures and every one of them counts the raw diagram source, so the enforced measure takes the **maximum** of that family - heading counted, hyphenated words split, Mermaid source counted - and a slide inside the cap there is inside it on every looser reading. | Compression is real and the deck now carries less: the three-column risk table became two columns, the reuse-and-addition sentence and the residual-risk sentence are gone, and the five documented limits are five two-word diagram nodes. Each of those facts is stated in full in this log and in `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`, and the deck names this log. Counts on the strictest reading are 29, 36, 29, 36, 28, 31, 38, 33 against a cap of 40, so the smallest margin is two words and a future sentence added anywhere fails the test until this row is re-measured with it - which is the intent, and will still surprise. Two defects were corrected in passing and are not separate rows: the architecture flowchart's edges pointed at five node ids (`L1`-`L5`) that the diagram never defined, so Mermaid was inventing five empty boxes, and the enforcement table still described the frontend readiness check as "not yet a pipeline step" after D333 made it one. |

## §38 Comment and docstring remediation — what a comment in this tree is allowed to say

Scope: the fifteen decisions taken while closing the thirty-six findings of the final comment/docstring
review. The review's verdict was that rationale, history, rejected alternatives, per-test catalogues,
negative inventories and unretained run results had accumulated inside the code itself, in twenty-nine of
the eighty-five scoped files, and that several of those comments had gone on to contradict the code they sit
beside. Rule 1 names this document as the only home for "why", so the remediation moves the reasoning here
and leaves each call site stating what its code guarantees. Three findings were security findings and one was
a real defect rather than a wording defect; both are recorded below rather than folded into the comment work.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D338 | Adopt one explicit comment doctrine for the whole tree and apply it to all twenty-nine failing files: a comment may state a **contract** (what a caller may rely on), an **invariant** (including an ordering that is load-bearing), a **non-obvious production fact an oracle rests on**, or an **API signature obligation** (`@param`, `@returns`, `@throws`). It may not state why an alternative was rejected, what an earlier revision did, what a test below asserts, what a file does *not* do, or what a run once measured. | (a) Reduce every flagged file to a target comment percentage. (b) Delete every comment the review named and stop there. (c) Rewrite only the comments that are provably false and treat density as style. | A percentage is not the property the review was judging: the fifty-six files it passed reach comparable and higher density — `backend/tests/integration/conftest.py` is 72.8% comment lines, `e2e/tests/harness-fixtures.ts` 57.9%, `backend/tests/factories.py` 57.7% — so a ratio target would have deleted load-bearing invariants in the passing files' own idiom while leaving the flagged content classes intact. (b) throws away contracts that happen to sit in a flagged range, which the review's own resolutions explicitly ask be kept. (c) leaves the Rule 1 violation, which is the finding with twenty-plus instances. Naming the admissible categories makes the next review's question answerable per comment rather than per file. | A category judgement is still a judgement, so two readers could disagree about a given sentence. Bounded by the categories being stated here and by the flagged content classes being enumerated as exclusions rather than described. |
| D339 | **Superseded by D292, which raised the pin to 3.5.0 before this row was written, so the exception has no subject in the delivered manifest. The doctrine below still binds: a manifest comment may not present a vulnerable pin as safe.** State the `python-jose[cryptography]` pin in `backend/requirements-dev.txt` as a four-line **frozen-pin security exception** that says the version is known-vulnerable, that it is not treated as safe, and where the advisories and the owner are recorded — and nowhere claim the vulnerabilities are unreachable. | (a) Keep the reachability analysis, which is accurate. (b) Delete the comment entirely and leave the bare pin. (c) Raise the pin to 3.4.0 and delete the exception. | "Not reachable from this suite" is a true statement about the *test* suite and worthless as a *production* control, and a reader scanning a manifest reads it as an assurance that the dependency is safe here. That is the reading the security finding objected to, and the fix is to remove the assurance rather than to qualify it. (b) leaves a vulnerable pin with nothing marking it, so a dependency audit has no thread to pull. (c) edits a frozen specification this programme does not own — the same reason D227, D247 and D270 each declined it. | What shipped is D292's line instead: the pin is 3.5.0, above CVE-2024-33663 and CVE-2024-33664, and the one-line `# SECURITY:` marker states that positively. So the four-line block this row specifies is withdrawn for want of a vulnerability to disclose, not because the reasoning was wrong -- and the reachability claim this row objected to is gone either way. `backend/tests/test_dependency_closure.py` gates the pin at 3.5.0. |
| D340 | Reduce the `backend/pytest.ini` `addopts` comment to the contract — no coverage option here, scope and threshold supplied by the runner command, precision declared in `backend/.coveragerc`, both asserted by `backend/tests/test_coverage_gate.py` — and cite D195, D226, D252 and D291 for the reasoning. | (a) Keep the rounding counterexample, which is what makes the precision matter. (b) Delete the comment and let the gate test be the only record. | The counterexample is the *argument* for D291's decision and D291 already carries it; repeating it in the file is the duplication Rule 1 forbids, and the file only needs to tell a reader where the two halves of the gate live. (b) removes the pointer that makes the split discoverable at all — `addopts` naming no coverage option is exactly the kind of absence a reader cannot infer. | A reader of `pytest.ini` no longer sees why precision is set to 2. Bounded by `backend/.coveragerc` carrying that explanation at the point of declaration, and by the gate test failing if either half moves. |
| D341 | Correct `backend/tests/conftest.py`'s settings comments to the behaviour the prologue actually has — the twelve defaulted fields are **pinned** through `DEFAULTED_SETTINGS_ENV`, and only `NOTION_API_KEY` and the two undeclared consumer fields are removed — and delete the module docstring's contents catalogue, the sensitive-field risk argument, the disclosure history, the endpoint-authorization argument and the two records of what an earlier guard revision permitted. | (a) Correct the false comments and leave the rest, since the rest is accurate. (b) Delete the false comments without replacing them. (c) Change the code to match the comments. | The false pair was the worst finding in the file: `DEFAULTED_SETTINGS_FIELDS` was documented as removed from the environment while the prologue five hundred lines below assigns every one of them and says "Pinning, not removal" in its own comment, so the file contradicted itself and a maintainer reading the constant would have drawn the opposite conclusion about the oracle. (a) leaves a 52%-comment file whose history and arguments the review named specifically. (b) leaves the tuple undocumented although its role — feeding `MANAGED_ENVIRONMENT_NAMES`, and being pinned rather than popped — is not visible from its name. (c) is the tail wagging the dog: pinning is correct, because pydantic v1 reads `os.environ` before the `.env` file and removal would leave a `.env` on disk free to decide the value. | The file no longer records that the child-process guard was once narrowed to "while a test is executing", so the reason the allow-list exists is only here. Bounded by the comment retaining the invariant that the guard is unconditional in time, which is the property that history was there to protect. |
| D342 | Delete the exhaustive per-test catalogues from the module docstrings of `backend/tests/unit/test_db_firestore.py` and `backend/tests/integration/test_http_tweets.py`, and replace the firestore suite's "Every statement of the module is executed" with a pointer to the generated coverage reports. | (a) Keep the catalogues as a table of contents for a long file. (b) Keep the coverage sentence and add the measured figure. (c) Generate the catalogues from the test names so they cannot drift. | A catalogue of what the tests below assert is the tests restated: it drifts the moment one is added, it was already the largest single block in both files, and neither file's contracts live in it — they live in the isolation and divergence sections, which are kept in full. The coverage sentence is worse than redundant: it is a runtime result stated as a property, with no retained artifact, and the same measurement is regenerated into `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` from the run itself. (b) puts a figure in a source comment that a later test will falsify. (c) is machinery for a document nobody needs. | A reader wanting the inventory of asserted behaviours must read the tests. Bounded by both files keeping the divergence sections that state *why* each disposition is the oracle, which is the part the test names cannot carry. |
| D343 | In `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.ts`, replace "Every value in the table was measured against the assembled application" with a reference to the two server-side suites that assert the same values, and describe `ALLOWED_REQUEST_ORIGINS` as the **explicitly handled loopback origins** rather than as "the origins jsdom serves the suite from". | (a) Keep the measurement claim and cite the commit it was measured at. (b) Retain the jsdom wording, since both origins are loopback and the distinction is pedantic. | A jsdom document has exactly one origin, so the old wording asserts something false about the environment and invites a reader to conclude that either entry would be served — when in fact the second entry exists so a suite that addresses `127.0.0.1` explicitly is handled rather than silently unhandled. The measurement claim has the same defect as D342's coverage sentence, and the fix is available for free because `test_route_surface.py` and `test_http_tweets.py` already assert every value in the table from the server side. | The table's provenance is now indirect: a reader must open the backend suites to confirm a cell. Bounded by both suites being named at the table, and by them failing if a value changes. |
| D344 | Reduce `frontend/src/test-utils/setup-jest.ts` to three stated invariants — the base-URL variable is deleted ahead of the imports, msw interception starts synchronously during module evaluation, and the global `afterEach` asserts the isolation ledger before it resets — and delete the "four things and nothing else" framing, the `onUnhandledRequest` callback rationale, and the "Not registered here" inventory. | (a) Keep the inventory, which stops a reader looking for a shim that is not there. (b) Keep the file as it stood; at 80% comment lines it is short in absolute terms. | The three invariants are the whole reason the file cannot be reordered, and they are the only part a caller can be broken by. The inventory is a list of absences, which grows without bound and is false the moment a shim is added; the callback rationale is a decision, and D108 and D238 hold it. (b) is the finding. | A suite hunting for a global shim no longer finds a comment confirming there is none. Bounded by `frontend/src/test-utils/setup-jest.test.ts`, which asserts the registered contract positively, and by `frontend/TESTING.md` documenting what a suite must install for itself. |
| D345 | Restate the jsdom-versus-browser split in `frontend/src/components/Analytics.test.tsx` from the code as it now stands: the real-browser chart path is reachable, and it is asserted by `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts`, which opts into the harness stub's forwarding header. Remove the claims that the harness stub rejects every route, that the browser path is out of reach, and that it is asserted nowhere. | (a) Delete the fourth table row rather than rewriting it. (b) Leave the text and add a note that the E2E layer has since changed. | Those three sentences were true when the stub rejected unconditionally and became false when D231 added the opt-in; a comment that sends a maintainer looking for an unasserted ceiling, when a spec asserts it, costs more than no comment at all. The row itself is worth keeping because the jsdom/browser boundary is exactly what stops this suite from simulating the path — (a) would lose that. (b) documents the drift instead of removing it. | Two files now describe one boundary, so a later change to the E2E chart test must update this docstring too. Bounded by the docstring naming the spec by path rather than restating what it asserts. |
| D346 | Document `getTrendData` in `e2e/harness/stubs/analyticsService.ts` by its actual conditional disposition — resolves when the response carries the forwarding header, rejects with one of three distinguishable types otherwise — in the module summary, the function summary and the `@returns`/`@throws` tags alike; and move the response-header channel argument out of the file. | (a) Keep "then rejects" as the summary, since rejection is the default. (b) Document the conditional only on `@returns`, leaving the summaries as they were. | The summary is what a reader of the public surface sees first, and "requests the trend series, then rejects" is a promise the function breaks under the one condition a spec cares about. A stub whose doc comment contradicts its own return statement is the failure mode the accuracy finding names. (b) leaves two of the three places wrong. The channel argument is a decision and D231 records it. | The summary is longer than a one-line summary wants to be. Bounded by the three rejection types keeping their own `@throws` tags, so the detail is where a caller handling failures will look. |
| D347 | In `e2e/playwright.config.ts`, name `e2e/tests/harness-fixtures.ts` as the owner of `test`, `expect` and `noEgress` at both places that had claimed a "Spec fixtures" section of this file, delete the reference to that section, and delete the worker-concurrency benchmark. | (a) Keep the benchmark and cite the host it was observed on. (b) Recreate a "Spec fixtures" section here so the comments become true. | D286 kept the fixture layer in `harness-fixtures.ts` and left two comments pointing at a section this file no longer has, which sends a maintainer to the wrong module — the same class of defect as D345, from a supersession rather than from a code change. The benchmark — a 64-core host, thirty-two simultaneous launches, a twelve-second serial pass — has no retained report, trace or run artifact, so it cannot be checked and must not be stated as fact; the cap it justifies is D214's and stays. (b) undoes D286 to make a comment accurate. | The reason the local worker cap is a quarter of the core count is no longer at the setting. Bounded by D214 carrying it and by the cap's own comment stating the constraint — one whole browser process per worker — that the arithmetic follows from. |
| D348 | Harden the credential evidence in `e2e/tests/configuration.spec.ts` rather than describing it as safe: key the fingerprint with a **per-run random key**, refuse to run unless every credential fixture matches the synthetic-literal contract, redact the one attachment that still serialised raw request bodies, and delete the comment endorsing full credential values in assertion failure diffs. | (a) Fix only the comment, since every value in the file is a test literal today. (b) Drop the fingerprint and attach presence and length only. (c) Attach nothing at all. | The comment was accurate about today and wrong about the property it claimed: an unkeyed FNV-1a digest of a short secret is recoverable by enumeration, so "length plus a stable digest" is not a redaction, and a run whose reports CI retains for thirty days is exactly where that matters. One attachment was in fact writing whole credential-shaped bodies, so this was a defect and not only a wording defect. The synthetic-only guard is what makes the remaining assertion diffs safe by construction, which is the half of the finding a comment could never satisfy. (b) loses the ability to tell which field differs between two runs, which is the reason the fingerprint exists. (c) leaves a failure undiagnosable. | A fingerprint is now incomparable across runs, so a flake that alternates between two values reads as two unrelated digests. Bounded by the guard failing the run before any credential-shaped value that is not a declared synthetic literal can reach an attachment. |
| D349 | **[The deletion half is superseded by D357 — the block is restored, because `ci.yml` is a pre-existing file and AAP §0.11.4 registers its placeholder as noted-but-not-fixed. The comment-reduction half below stands.]** Delete the `HUMAN ASSISTANCE NEEDED` block from `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and reduce the workflow's remaining comments to operational and security constraints with decision-log pointers. | (a) Keep the block and correct `requirements.txt` to `backend/requirements-dev.txt`. (b) Keep the block as a record that the workflow was reviewed. | The block asks a reviewer to verify a file that does not exist, in a workflow that has since been rewritten to install `backend/requirements-dev.txt` — so it is both stale and unactionable. The AAP's preservation exception covers the original route comments in `app/api/routes/tweets.py` and nothing else, so a placeholder in a file this programme authored has no standing. (a) preserves a request that the delivered workflow has already satisfied. | Nothing records that the workflow's install step was once unverified. Bounded by this row. |
| D350 | Reduce `.gitignore` to its section headings, its twenty-three active patterns and a two-line note that lockfiles are deliberately not ignored; move the absence history, the rejected ignore categories and the manifest-pinning arithmetic here. | (a) Keep the lockfile essay, because the absence of a rule is the thing a reader cannot see. (b) Delete the lockfile note too and rely on the contract test. | A `.gitignore` is read by people looking for a pattern, and sixty-eight per cent of its lines were argument. The note stays because a deliberate absence genuinely is invisible; the arithmetic behind it — three exact pins in `e2e`, eleven added to `frontend`, seventeen pre-existing caret ranges — is a measurement that will drift, and `frontend/src/test-utils/dependency-closure.test.ts` enforces the split it describes. (b) leaves a future contributor free to add the rule the test then fails on, with no comment explaining why. | The file no longer explains why `dist/` and editor noise are absent. Bounded by D287, which fixes the pattern set, and by this row naming the categories that were considered and declined. |
| D351 | Make every targeted-run example in `frontend/TESTING.md` and `e2e/README.md` fail-closed — `npm test -- …`, `npm run test:ci -- …`, `npm --prefix e2e test -- …` — and replace the `// … drive the subject and assert` placeholder with a complete assertion. The `--` is load-bearing in the prefixed spelling: without it npm consumes the flag itself and the whole suite runs, which `e2e/README.md` section 3 states beside the form. | (a) Keep `npx` and add a warning that dependencies must be installed. (b) Name the local binary path directly in each example. | `npx` resolves from the registry when the local executable is missing, so an onboarding document that shows it teaches a contributor to fetch an unpinned tool while believing they ran the project's own — and both guides already tell them the opposite in their install sections. A package script cannot do that: npm runs the binary from the package's own `node_modules/.bin`. (b) is correct but unreadable, and it hard-codes a path that a Windows contributor spells differently. An example ending in a pseudo-comment is not an example. | The examples are marginally more verbose, and `--` before a Jest or Playwright argument is a step a reader can forget. Bounded by every example in both documents now using the same form, so the pattern is learnable from any one of them. |
| D352 | **Applied except for the two content-security-policy residuals and the advisory-affected Mermaid pin, which stay at the point of use as well as here: D325 requires a reader of the policy to be told that `frame-ancestors` cannot be delivered from a `<meta>` element and that `script-src 'unsafe-inline'` is unavoidable under Rule 4, and a pointer alone does not carry that to someone editing the directive list.** Reduce the HTML comments in `blitzy-deck/executive-summary.html` to mechanics and structure — slide numbering, CSS section boundaries, rendering order and the concurrency note — and move the content-security-policy, font-integrity, prior-failure and residual-risk arguments here. | (a) Keep the security arguments, since a reader editing the head needs them. (b) Delete every comment, including the slide numbers. | The deck is a Rule 4 deliverable and its comments were arguing Rule 1 decisions inside the artifact, which is the violation regardless of how few lines it is. The structural comments are kept because they are how a slide is located in a 1,500-line single file — the review named them as keepers. (b) would make the file unnavigable. | The head's security posture is no longer explained where it is configured. Bounded by the mechanics notes remaining, so what each directive does is still stated, and by the arguments for why being one document away. |


## §39 The suite-size figures the documents publish, re-measured on the delivered tree

Scope: two decisions about numbers rather than about code. Nothing in this section changes behaviour;
both rows exist because a figure a document publishes is a claim, and a stale claim is a defect whether
or not any test notices it.

| Row | Decision | Alternatives | Why | Risks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D353 | **Re-measure and restate every published suite-size figure against the delivered tree, and take each one from a retained artifact rather than from a run log.** The figures are: backend `1015` collected / `1012` passed / `3` skipped at `93.33%` (182/195); frontend `24` suites, `371` cases, `347` passed / `24` skipped; end to end `31` passed in `5` spec files; `1,390` passing across the three streams, which is the deck’s headline KPI. | (a) Leave each document at the figure it was written with, and note somewhere that the totals are approximate. (b) Restate only the totals a test asserts, leaving the prose figures alone. (c) Delete the figures and publish only the commands that produce them. (d) Publish a range rather than a number. | Each figure was correct for the tree its author measured, and every one of those trees was a subset of this one, so every published figure was **low** — the four backend readings alone spanned 774, 805, 847 and 911 collected against an actual 1015. A reader who runs the documented command and gets a larger number than the document promises cannot tell a healthy suite from a broken document, which is exactly the failure D307 was written to prevent, one checkpoint later. (a) keeps that ambiguity and adds an excuse for it. (b) leaves the onboarding documents — the ones a new developer reads first — as the least accurate artifacts in the tree. (c) removes information that is genuinely useful as an expectation to check a local run against. (d) is a range nobody can falsify. Every figure written here was read from `backend/reports/junit.xml`, `backend/coverage.json`, `backend/reports/coverage-gate.txt`, `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml`, `frontend/coverage/coverage-summary.json` and `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml`, and the deck’s KPI was then confirmed in a real browser rather than in the source. | The figures move again the moment a test is added, and nothing re-derives the prose ones automatically — `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` gates the structural counts and the deck’s word and diagram census, not these totals. `docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py` is the standing answer: it reads all of them out of the artifacts on demand, so a reader who doubts a number here has a one-command way to settle it. |
| D354 | **Leave every figure that a decision row or a dated evidence row states as history exactly as it stands**, including the superseded totals inside D307, D309 and D333 and the perturbation readings in `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.3. | (a) Update them too, so no stale number survives anywhere. (b) Annotate each with its current equivalent. (c) Delete the historical figures. | A decision row records what was decided when it was decided; rewriting its numbers to match a later tree would make the log agree with itself at the cost of no longer being a record — and D307’s own subject is the *practice* of re-measuring, which is unintelligible if the figures it corrected are silently replaced by newer ones. The §7.3 perturbation sweep is likewise evidence of a moment: those runs happened on the tree they name, and no later run reproduces them. (a) destroys the audit trail this document exists to be. (b) doubles the length of thirty rows to no benefit, since §1.1 and D353 already publish the current figures in one place. (c) is (a) with less ceremony. | A reader scanning for a number can find an old one in a decision row and mistake it for current. Bounded by every such row being explicitly historical in its own text, by §1.1 being extractor-produced, and by this row. |


## §40 Final-acceptance remediation — the last cross-document defects, and the deviations that need an owner

Scope: the twenty-one findings the final full-project acceptance review raised. None is a functional or
security defect in delivered code; every one is either a document contradicting its own evidence, a guard
that admitted a case it should have refused, or a bounded deviation from the frozen plan that a decision row
cannot authorise on its own. The rows below record what changed, and — for the seven deviations — what a
human owner is being asked to decide.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D355 | **Close the backend evidence chain at the weakest link rather than at the stale file.** Three changes together: the readiness probe repeats `--junitxml` to `reports/collect-only-junit.xml`, so a discovery run no longer writes the canonical stream at all; `docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py` requires the stream's case count to equal the collected count the readiness artifact records, withdraws the stream when it does not, names both figures on stderr and exits 1 under `--require-all`; and the workflow's `Verify backend report artifacts` step performs the same comparison instead of grepping for a non-zero count. `backend/pytest.ini` is untouched. | (a) Remove `--junitxml` from `pytest.ini` `addopts` and pass it only on the canonical command. (b) Regenerate the stale artifact and change nothing else. (c) Refuse any stream below a plausibility floor rather than comparing it with a witness. | The defect was not that one file was stale; it was that no guard could see a **partial** run. A zero-case stub was already refused twice over, and a partial run leaves a non-zero count that reads to every consumer exactly like a full one. The collected count is an independent witness of how many cases the suite has, so an equality is available and a heuristic is not — which rules out (c), whose floor would be a number nobody can justify. (a) would edit `addopts`, which AAP §0.5.4 fixes verbatim, to solve a problem the command line already solves: the value passed on the command line wins, which was measured rather than assumed. (b) leaves the mechanism that produced the staleness in place, so the next narrow re-run reproduces it. | The comparison depends on `backend/reports/collect-only.txt` being present and current; if it is stale rather than the stream, the guard names a difference whose cause is the other file. Bounded by both artifacts being written by adjacent steps of the same job and by the message quoting both figures rather than blaming one. |
| D356 | **State the browser evidence as resolved-by-path with a recorded digest, and not as digest-bound.** `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.2 A1 now says that `browsers:require` recorded the executable's SHA-256 and that the launch was not bound to it, because binding requires `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE_SHA256` to be set and it was unset — which is what the artifact's own closing line says, alongside its two warnings that the executable and its directory are user-writable. | (a) Re-run `browsers:require` with the digest exported, so the artifact becomes digest-bound, and keep the claim. (b) Leave the wording, since the digest genuinely is recorded. | The artifact is the evidence, and it ends by telling its reader the check confirmed the path only. (a) makes the sentence true of one run on one host while `e2e/reports/` is gitignored, so nothing durable would carry it, and the next reader following the documented command with no digest exported would meet the same contradiction. (b) is the finding. | A reader may conclude the browser was unverified. It was not: the version was read from the install layout and the digest is in the artifact — what is absent is the launch-time binding, and §7.3.4 rows S8 and S9 state exactly what that binding does when it is present. |
| D357 | **Supersedes the deletion half of D349.** Restore the six-line `# HUMAN ASSISTANCE NEEDED` block to the end of `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, byte-for-byte as the pre-work tree carried it, and correct §23's placeholder census to the whole tree: 28 across 22 production modules plus 9 more across 7 non-production files. | (a) Keep D349's deletion and amend the census to record `ci.yml` as no longer carrying one. (a) is internally consistent but keeps an unauthorised edit to a pre-existing file. (b) is worse: it edits the debt rather than leaving it, which is the same violation with more words. | The restored block asks a reader to verify a `requirements.txt` that does not exist, so it is stale on its face — which is exactly the condition §0.11.4 exists to record rather than repair, and `README.md`'s suggested-next-tasks list already carries repairing the workflow's lint steps. (b) Require the superseder beside **every** citation of a superseded row, half-supersessions included. That is the intended cost. The word map deliberately still contains the stale value, so a regression to it fails on its value rather than falling out of the pattern and passing unnoticed. |
| D361 | **Move the last substantive rationale out of three configuration files, and keep only the contract each states plus its row pointer.** `backend/pytest.ini` loses the six-line `addopts` argument and the ten-line correlation-id essay; `backend/.coveragerc` loses the rounding-band argument and the override argument; `e2e/package.json`'s `description` drops from roughly 250 words of reasoning to one sentence of identity plus two pointers. The reasoning itself is preserved here, in full: (i) `addopts` names no coverage option **because** a value there would make every invocation measure and gate, including `--collect-only`, and would override the scope and threshold the CI step and the documented gated command supply — which is also why `.coveragerc` declares no `fail_under` and no `[run] source`, since pytest-cov adopts `cov_config.fail_under` when `--cov-fail-under` is absent. (ii) `.coveragerc` sets `precision = 2` **because** `coverage.results.should_fail_under` compares `round(total, precision) < fail_under`, so at the library default of 0 anything from 89.5 up is admitted; no finite precision closes the band, since two decimals still admit a total in [89.995, 90), which is why the binding half of the gate is `backend/tests/coverage_gate.py` comparing integer counts. (iii) `junit_logging = log` with `junit_log_passing_tests = false` **because** a passing test's captured log is noise in an archived stream, while a failing one's is the evidence; the `correlation_id` field is a stable digest of the running test's nodeid, and that same nodeid is the `classname`/`name` pair of the matching `<testcase>`, which is what lets a log line, a captured-log section and a result be traced to one test. (iv) `e2e`'s scripts each name a local binary rather than resolving through `npx`, which would fall back to the registry when the local one is absent; no script downloads a browser, because Playwright 1.44.1's downloader does not verify the TLS chain of the host it fetches from (CVE-2025-59288); `browsers:require` resolves `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`, then `CHROME_BIN`, then the platform's vendor locations, then the exact path `chromium.executablePath()` reports for this pin — the only correct source for that route, because Playwright loads the build matching its own pinned revision and is the only thing that knows where `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH` points, including the value `0`; `browsers:verify` is the narrower reporter over the explicit executable and the cache path only; and `test:list` overrides the reporter so listing cannot overwrite a previous run's JUnit and HTML reports with an all-skipped stub. | (a) Leave the comments, since each already cross-referenced the log. (b) Delete the comments outright, leaving no pointer. (c) Treat `e2e/package.json`'s `description` as prose rather than as a code comment and leave it. | Rule 1 states that rationale must not be embedded in code comments and that the decision log is the single source of truth for "why"; §0.10.5 C2 carves out only the terse `# TESTING:` production markers, and these three files are not that. The `pytest.ini` file's own authoring brief is more explicit still — "write no explanatory comments … not one comment states a reason, an alternative or a risk" — so this is alignment with the frozen intent, not a new standard. (a) is the finding. (b) would leave a reader of a bare `precision = 2` with no route to the argument behind it, which Rule 3 needs. (c) is a distinction the rule does not draw: a `description` field in a manifest is read in the file, not in the log. | The three files are now terse enough that a reader who does not follow the pointer will not know why `precision = 2` or why no script uses `npx`. That is the trade Rule 1 requires, and every pointer names the exact rows. |
| D362 | **Close the three accessibility findings that are closable inside this delivery, and route the one that is not.** Six `<th>` elements in the deck gain `scope="col"`; a `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` block is added to the deck and to the canonical theme at `blitzy-deck/references/blitzy-reveal-theme.css`, clearing `transition` and `animation` on the reveal root, the stage, every section and stack section, the background layer, the progress bar and both control arrows, while leaving Rule 4's mandated `transition: 'slide'` in the reveal configuration exactly as it stands; and the missing level-one heading on every routed screen is added to `README.md`'s suggested-next-tasks list beside the production accessibility items already there. | (a) Leave all three: the tables already meet WCAG AA through technique H51, and 2.3.3 is Level AAA. (b) Change the reveal configuration to `transition: 'none'`, which is the direct fix. (c) Add an `<h1>` to `e2e/harness/main.tsx` so the served outline has one. | (a) is defensible on the letter and poor on the substance: `scope="col"` is five minutes and H63 lists it as sufficient, and a viewer who has asked their operating system to reduce motion is asking a question the file can answer. (b) is unavailable — Rule 4 fixes the slide transition by name, and a CSS media query removes the movement for the viewers who asked without contradicting the rule for anyone else. (c) would put page chrome in the harness that the application does not have, so every spec would then observe a screen the product cannot serve; §0.8.2 rules out the alternative of adding the heading to `app.tsx`, so the item is documented rather than implemented. | The reduced-motion block is CSS-only, so a future reveal version that animates through a property not listed here would need the list extended. `transform` is deliberately **not** cleared: reveal carries the stage scale-and-centre on a transform of `.reveal .slides` and positions each section with a transform of its own, so a form of this block that cleared it threw the stage into the viewport's bottom-right quadrant with content overflowing — measured in a real browser at this checkpoint, then narrowed to `transition` and `animation` alone and re-measured, with the discarded form re-injected in memory as a negative control to confirm the measurement was sensitive to the difference. The `<h1>` gap remains open in the product and is now stated in the one list a new developer reads. |
| D363 | **Put the `python-jose` deviation to the owner as a ratification question, rather than deciding the same line a third time in this log.** The pin stays at `3.5.0`. What this row adds is not another reversal but the artefact the review found missing: a named owner question, carried in the register that closes this section and pointed at from `README.md`'s suggested-next-tasks list, so the one-line difference between the delivered manifest and the agreed inventory is met by whoever can actually settle it. | (a) Revert to `3.3.0`, as D227 and D270 concluded. (b) Leave D292 standing as the last word and add nothing. (c) Move to `3.4.0`, the first fixed release. (d) Declare AAP §0.6.1 non-binding and stop recording the difference at all. | This log has already decided the same line both ways twice — D163 raised it, D227 and D270 restored it, D292 raised it again — and a fourth reversal would add no information, because the disagreement was never technical. It is a question of authority: whether a delivery may print a version the agreed inventory does not. §0.1.4 freezes the endpoint shapes, the `Settings` field names, the Redux store shape, the zod contracts and the `api.ts` signatures, and names no dependency version, which is the textual support D292 relied on; but §0.6.1 does print `3.3.0` in a table, and a delivery cannot amend a plan merely by reading that table as empirical. So the code keeps the safer pin and the amendment goes to a human. (a) knowingly ships CVE-2024-33663 and CVE-2024-33664, and additionally fails `test_dependency_closure.py`, which holds the manifest to the installed distribution — measured, not predicted. (b) is exactly what the review found missing: reasoning without a decision. (c) closes the same two advisories while leaving a known-good later release unused. (d) would delete the record Rule 1 exists to keep. | Until an owner ratifies it the delivered manifest differs from the printed inventory in one line, and any future review raises it again; that recurrence is the accepted cost of not reverting to a vulnerable pin. If the owner reverses this, five sites move together — `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, `backend/tests/README.md`'s pin table, `SECURITY-GAPS.md` row 14, this section's register and `README.md`'s backlog entry — which is why they now point at one register instead of each restating the case. |
| D364 | **Adjudicate the remaining six deviations once, in a register that separates the two the plan already authorises from the four that need ratifying.** `frontend/package.json`'s two extra `test:` scripts and the twenty-one test-side files beyond §0.5.1's map are **authorised**: §0.8.1 puts that file's "devDependencies and test scripts only" in scope with no cap on the count, and every one of the twenty-one paths falls inside a directory glob the same subsection declares in scope, with no production file among them. The `grpcio` pins, the Playwright trace mode, the msw unhandled-request ledger, and the end-to-end job's browser provisioning and invocation form diverge from named plan text and go to the owner. | (a) Revert all six to the literal text. (b) Leave all six logged and silent, which is what shipped. (c) Call all six authorised by §0.8.1. (d) Call all six owner questions. | (a) costs something measured in every case: dropping the `grpcio` pins reintroduces the `grpcio-status 1.63.0rc1` pre-release this host actually resolved; `on-first-retry` loses the trace for any failure that never retries; msw's literal `'error'` raises inside the request handler, which every caller in this repository catches, so the guard would fall silent exactly where it is needed; and restoring `npx playwright install` either fetches a browser through the downloader carrying CVE-2025-59288 or fails on a runner that has none. (b) is the gap the review named. (c) overstates §0.8.1: it governs which files may change, not which values a manifest may print, so it cannot authorise a pin the inventory omits. (d) understates it: a scope clause reading "test scripts only", with no number attached, does authorise a sixth and a seventh test script, and §0.5.3's five-script list and §0.5.1's map are detail views of that same surface rather than caps on it. | The register is a document and not a gate, so an owner who never reads it leaves the four unratified indefinitely; the `README.md` pointer is the mitigation. Two of the four — the trace mode and the browser provisioning — are observable only in CI, which has never executed, so ratifying them rests on reading the workflow rather than on a run. Calling the scripts and the extra files authorised is a reading of §0.8.1 against §0.5.3 and §0.5.1; an owner who reads the narrower subsections as binding instead moves two entries from authorised to reversed, and `test:list`, `test:load` and twenty-one files come out. |
| D365 | **Re-measure and restate every published suite-size figure again, because this checkpoint's own additions moved them.** Twenty-one findings were closed by adding guards and bindings rather than by editing production code, which took the backend suite from the `1015` collected / `1012` passed D353 recorded to `1068` collected / `1065` passed / `3` skipped at an unchanged `93.33%` (182/195). Frontend and end to end are untouched at `24` suites / `371` cases / `347` passed / `24` skipped, and `31` passed in `5` spec files, so the deck's headline KPI moves from `1,390` to `1,443`. Restated at fourteen whole-suite sites across six documents, plus two per-suite tables in `backend/tests/README.md` that no earlier checkpoint had re-swept because until now every added test landed in a file neither table listed: the root-suite `Cases` column (`41`→`72`, `20`→`62`, `146`→`168`, `26`→`31`, `42`→`90`) and the expected-outcome table (`483`→`492` for `tests/unit`, `77`→`168` for the guard contract, two missing command rows added, and its closing arithmetic restated so it now closes on the collected total rather than on a partial `847`). The second of those was found by the cross-document check this row adds, not by the sweep that preceded it. | (a) Restate after each fix-and-verify group, which is what a naive reading of "keep the documents true" implies. (b) Leave the figures at D353's readings and note only that the suite grew. (c) Publish a range instead of a number. (d) Stop publishing counts and point at the artifact. | (a) would have republished the same five figures four times over, each time knowing the next group would move them again — and a stale republished figure is precisely what findings F14 and F16 were, so the honest sequencing is one measurement pass placed after the last test-adding group, which is why this is its own step rather than a line inside one. (b) leaves the defect class the review raised open in the very documents it raised it against. (c) reads as evasion where the count is exact and costs one command. (d) is worse onboarding: a reader wants the number in the sentence, and every result stream is gitignored, so a fresh clone has no artifact to consult. Every figure here was read out of a retained artifact — `backend/reports/junit.xml`, `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml`, `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml`, `backend/reports/collect-only.txt` — and the per-suite figures were re-derived from that same JUnit stream's `classname` attributes rather than counted by hand. D354's freeze still holds: no figure inside a decision row or a dated evidence row was touched, so D307, D309, D333, D353 and §7.3's perturbation readings still read as the history they are. |

What each costs to reverse is stated in its own row.

Neither has run on `ubuntu-latest`, so both rest on reading the workflow. |


## §41 QA-acceptance remediation — the frontend runner's reproducibility, and two published test-command claims

Scope: the four findings the full runtime QA acceptance raised against the delivered test system. None of
them is a defect in what the suites assert — the backend collects with zero errors, both coverage gates hold
and 25 of 26 deliberately broken oracles were caught. Two are about the *runner* being reproducible: the
exact command `.github/workflows/ci.yml` executes returned a different exit code on an unchanged tree. Two
are claims a document publishes that its own runtime contradicts. Where the finding's suggested fix was
implemented, measured and found insufficient, the row says so and states what replaced it.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D366 | **Declare `maxWorkers: 1` and `testTimeout: 30000` in `frontend/jest.config.js`**, so that no run spawns a worker child process and every entry point — `npm test`, `test:coverage`, `test:ci`, `test:load` and a bare `npx jest` — inherits the same two numbers instead of inheriting the host's core count. | (a) `--maxWorkers=<n>` on the `test:ci` script only, which is the fix the finding suggested. (b) `maxWorkers: 4` in the config, so every entry point shares one cap. (c) `maxWorkers: '50%'`. (d) `forceExit: true`. (e) The timeout alone, leaving the pool as it was. (f) `jest.setTimeout(30000)` in `src/test-utils/setup-jest.ts` rather than a config key. (g) A third argument raising the deadline on just the four slowest Configuration cases. | Two halves, both settled by measurement rather than by reading. **The deadline**: the failing run reported `Exceeded timeout of 5000 ms for a test.` at `Configuration.test.tsx:311`, and per-test timings taken on an *idle* host put that case at 2,487 ms and three of its neighbours at 3,566, 3,562 and 3,463 ms against a 5,000 ms default — under 1.5× headroom before any load. The same run measured that suite at 21.4 s idle and 117.4 s loaded, a 5.5× inflation that puts a 3.6 s case at 19.6 s, so 30,000 ms is the first round value above the compound worst case rather than a guess. Negative-validated: a probe body that sleeps 6.5 s passes under the delivered config and fails with the finding's exact message under `--testTimeout=5000`. **The pool**: (a) and (b) were both implemented and both measured, and neither closed the finding — at `maxWorkers: 4` the teardown warning still appeared on 2 of 3 `npm run test:ci` runs, and (a) is narrower still, because the warning was reproduced on a plain `npm test`, which no `test:ci` flag reaches. Neither works because the warning is not a leak: `jest-worker`'s `BaseWorkerPool.end()` gives each child a hard-coded `FORCE_EXIT_DELAY = 500` ms to exit and `jest-runner` reports any child that misses it, so on a contended host the outcome is decided by scheduling latency and no configuration key extends the window. There is nothing to close either — `--detectOpenHandles` reports no open handle, `setup-jest.ts` closes the msw server in `afterAll`, and the suite passes in reverse file order. `maxWorkers <= 1` takes Jest's own in-band path in `shouldRunInBand`, which removes the pool and with it the entire class of outcome. Measured after the change: five consecutive `npm run test:ci` runs, every one exit 0 at 371 total / 347 passed / 24 skipped, zero force-exit lines, zero timeout lines, wall time 18.3–40.6 s against the 78.6–258.6 s spread the finding recorded. (c) still leaves 32 children on the host this was measured on. (d) suppresses a symptom and reports the same warning regardless. (f) and (g) put the deadline where a reader of the configuration cannot see it, and (g) leaves every other suite on the 5 s default that failed. | In band, the 24 files run sequentially in one process, so the suite cannot use a second core: on a runner with spare cores the frontend step is slower than a pool would make it, and that cost grows with every suite added. On the hosts measured here it does not — the in-band figures above beat the pooled ones, because worker start-up and 24 separate transform caches cost more at this size than the parallelism returns — but that margin is a property of the current suite size, not a guarantee. One process also accumulates heap across all 24 files instead of recycling it per worker, and the obvious mitigation is closed off: setting `workerIdleMemoryLimit` silently re-enables the pool, because `shouldRunInBand` refuses the in-band path whenever that key is present. A 30 s deadline also means a genuinely hung test takes 30 s to report rather than 5, a cost paid only by a test that is already failing. |
| D367 | **Correct the "valid `jest -t` pattern verbatim" claim in every place it is made, and change nothing the reporter emits.** `-t` is `testNamePattern`, a regular expression, so an emitted `<testcase name>` is a usable pattern only once its regex metacharacters are escaped. The five places are `frontend/jest.config.js`'s identity comment, `frontend/TESTING.md` in both §1 "Targeted runs" and §7's identity list, the comment beside the identity assertion in `frontend/src/test-utils/junit-correlation.test.ts`, and D157 itself, which takes this log's in-place partial-correction marker naming this row. §1 also gains the three worked commands — unescaped, escaped, and a name needing neither. | (a) Emit an escaped variant alongside the name, the finding's own second suggestion. (b) Change `titleTemplate` or `ancestorSeparator` so an emitted name carries no metacharacter. (c) Rename the titles that carry parentheses. (d) Correct only the four locations the finding names. (e) Bind the corrected claim with a committed test case. | Measured on the retained result stream: of **371** emitted names, **199** carry at least one regex metacharacter and **141** do not select themselves at all when passed raw, while **all 371** do once escaped — so the claim was false for a large minority and misleading for the majority, and the example that surfaced it selected nothing while reporting all 371 skipped, which reads as a passing run rather than as a bad pattern. Correcting the wording is the whole fix because the *identity* is right and is contract-frozen: AAP §0.1.4 protects it, and `junit-correlation.test.ts` asserts the emitted name equals `currentTestName`. (a) puts a second, non-canonical identity in every `<testcase>` and invites a consumer to key on the one the request ledger does not stamp. (b) and (c) change that identity — and (c) makes every suite author responsible in perpetuity for a property no reporter can hold, which is the same objection D157 rejected its own alternative (b) with. (d) leaves the fifth location, and the fifth is the one that matters most: §1 "Targeted runs" is the instruction a developer follows, and it told the reader to copy a name from a report and pass it straight to `-t`. (e) is the durable answer and is declined on cost, not on principle: the only sensible home is `junit-correlation.test.ts`, and one more case there moves the frontend census off 371 and the deck's headline KPI off its sum, which four documents publish and `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` cross-checks — about thirty numeric edits for a wording defect, against §0.1.4's instruction to take the approach requiring the least modification. It is recorded as a suggested next task instead. | The corrected claim is prose in four files and a marker in a fifth, with nothing that fails if a future edit reverts it — precisely the invisibility D158 objected to, accepted here deliberately and named as a next task rather than left unstated. The worked examples quote one real test name, so renaming that test leaves a stale example; it is the Dashboard heading test, whose own oracle asserts the same string, so the staleness surfaces the first time anyone runs the example. And escaping is now something a reader has to do by hand: no script in this tree emits a ready-to-paste pattern. |
| D368 | **Correct the bare-root-`pytest` figure in the root `README.md`'s Run section and make the bullet self-reconciling**: the triple becomes `21 failed, 1044 passed, 3 skipped` and states `21 + 1044 + 3 = 1068` against the collected total the bullet above it already publishes, so a reader can check all three numbers without running anything. The bullet also records that `filterwarnings` is not applied on that run, without publishing a warning count, and that its figures assume the retained artifact set. | (a) Correct the number alone, which is what the finding asked for. (b) Delete the triple and describe only the failure mechanism. (c) Add the phrasing to `PASSED_PATTERNS` in `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` so this figure joins the one-figure-everywhere gate. (d) Add a contract test asserting the root-run triple closes on the collected figure. | The published triple summed to 1015 where the document's own collected figure requires 1044, and the run prints `21 failed, 1044 passed, 3 skipped` — so the value was wrong, and the reason it was wrong is that it was unbindable: none of the eight `PASSED_PATTERNS` phrasings matches `failed, N passed, 3 skipped`, which is how this one figure drifted while nine other published copies of the suite size stayed correct through two re-measurement checkpoints (D353, D365). (a) fixes the value and leaves it exactly as unbindable. (b) discards the concrete evidence that makes the trap recognisable, which is the bullet's entire purpose. (c) breaks the gate it would join: `test_every_document_publishes_the_same_backend_passing_figure` asserts one figure across the count-publishing set, and a root run legitimately passes a different number from a `cd backend` run — 1044 against 1065 — so adding the phrasing makes a correct tree fail. (d) is the durable answer and costs a backend case, which moves the collected and passing figures every count-publishing document states plus the deck KPI: the same cascade D367 declines, for the same §0.1.4 reason, and recorded as the same suggested next task. Stating the arithmetic is what is left that costs nothing and still fails visibly to a reader. | The reconciliation is arithmetic a human performs, not a gate — a future edit can still put a wrong number there and nothing will fail. The bullet now carries two figures that both move whenever the suite grows, where it carried one. And the triple is exact only while the two artifact-dependent cases are active: `tests/test_coverage_gate.py` skips without `backend/coverage.json` and `tests/test_docs_contract.py` skips without `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml`, so a clone that has produced neither reports two more skips and two fewer passes. The bullet says so in words rather than publishing a second triple, because every count in that document is stated against the artifact-present tree and a second convention in one bullet would be worse than the caveat. |



## §42 QA-findings remediation — the deck's runtime, its diagram engine, and the harness route surface

Scope: the thirteen findings a runtime QA pass raised that fall inside this plan's authorised surface —
`blitzy-deck/**` and `e2e/**` per AAP §0.8.1. Eleven concern the deck, two the Playwright harness. No
production module under `frontend/` or `backend/` is touched by any row here. The other twenty-four findings
that pass raised have their root cause in production code that AAP §0.8.2 and §0.11.2 place out of scope, so
none is fixed; twenty-three were already carried by the suggested-next-tasks register in `README.md` or by
`SECURITY-GAPS.md`, and the one that was not — that no stylesheet is loaded on any screen, so the whole UI
renders as unstyled default HTML — has been added to that register. Every row below was measured in a real
browser before and after the change; the readings are in `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.6.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D369 | **Pin the deck to reveal's slide view at every viewport width with `scrollActivationWidth: 0`, and make the diagram renderer's scope follow the mode rather than assume one.** `renderScope()` returns `document` when `Reveal.isScrollView()` is true and the current slide otherwise, so a pending diagram is reachable under either layout. | (a) Leave the default 435 px threshold and accept that a narrow viewport gets reveal's scroll view. (b) Raise the threshold to some large value rather than zero. (c) Set `view: 'slide'` explicitly. (d) Keep the threshold and teach the renderer to work inside scroll view. | Below the default threshold the measured state was not merely a different layout: `.reveal .slides > section` dropped to **0** and `.scroll-page` to 16, all six `pre.mermaid` held no `<svg>` at all with `data-processed` absent — so the raw `flowchart …` source was the visible content — and any navigation wedged the renderer with no console output. That is a silent total failure of the deliverable at phone width, which rules out (a). Zero is falsy, so reveal skips the auto-activation check entirely; (b) achieves the same only up to whatever width someone eventually exceeds. (c) is not a documented option on this version. (d) is the larger change and leaves 16 scroll pages where the deck's whole design is a fixed 1920×1080 stage — though its useful half is kept, since `renderScope()` handles scroll view correctly if reveal ever enters it. Verified at 390×844: `isScrollView()` false, stage transform `matrix(0.2, 0, 0, 0.2, -960, -540)`, 16 sections, all six diagrams drawn, and a 100 ms interval canary whose worst gap across a full traversal was 153 ms. | The deck is now legible-only-when-zoomed on a phone, because a fixed stage at 390 px scales to 0.2. That is the design's consequence, not this row's, and it is preferable to rendering nothing. A future reveal release could change what a zero means here; the canary and the diagram census in §7.6 are how that would be caught. |
| D370 | **Make the navigation controls perceivable with a token-coloured halo, not by changing their ink.** `controlsBackArrows: 'visible'` raises the back arrow from reveal's faded 0.3 to 0.9, and `.reveal .controls .controls-arrow` carries two `drop-shadow` filters in `--blitzy-surface-0`. | (a) `controlsBackArrows: 'visible'` alone. (b) Recolour the controls to `--blitzy-accent-teal`, or to white. (c) Move the controls off the gradient. (d) Accept it: a reader can use the keyboard. | Measurement showed the reported finding understated the defect. The controls sit exactly where the divider gradient reaches `#5B39F3`, which is also the controls' own colour, so the peak deviation from the background inside the arrow's box was **2.6/255** for the back arrow and **1.19/255** for the on-panel part of the forward arrow — *both* were invisible, so (a) alone would have left the forward arrow unfixed. The arrows straddle the panel edge and the white 4 % stage margin, so no single ink colour can contrast with both, which rules out (b). (c) moves furniture the theme places deliberately. (d) leaves the only visible affordance invisible on seven of sixteen slides. A halo works because it contrasts with the ink rather than with the background. After: peak deviation 150/255 and **4.35:1** on slide 4's back arrow, 179/255 and **6.40:1** on its forward arrow, and 180/255 and **9.57:1** on the closing slide against a mathematically flat field — improvements of roughly 58× and 150×. | A halo is unusual chrome and a designer may read it as an artifact. It is also invisible against a white background, where it is not needed. Bounded by being applied only to `.controls-arrow` and by being expressed in the surface token rather than a literal. |
| D371 | **Render each diagram exactly once, and treat a render whose measurements could not have been taken as a failure to discard rather than a result to keep.** Five parts: `isDrawn()` requires `data-processed="true"`, an `<svg>`, **and** a viewBox larger than 24 units in both dimensions; `isMeasurable()` requires a non-zero client rect; `pendingDiagramsOn()` resets only what it will actually render; a deferred-scope queue drains on settle so a slide passed over at machine speed is swept; and `discardFailedRenders()` removes any svg that failed `isDrawn` so the next visit retries. | (a) Keep re-rendering on every `slidechanged`, which is what produced a new svg id on each return. (b) Add the render-once guard and the queue only. (c) Gate on `data-processed` alone. (d) Sweep all six diagrams on `ready` regardless of visibility. | (a) is the reported defect: the same diagram was rebuilt on every revisit, which is wasted work and makes the DOM non-identical across visits for no reason. (b) is what was tried first, and it introduced a **worse** defect than the one it fixed: draining the queue rendered diagrams whose slides were still `display: none`, Mermaid sizes from live DOM measurements, so it *succeeded* having measured nothing and emitted `viewBox="-8 -8 16 16"` — a 102-element graph collapsed into an invisible 16×16 box — which the render-once guard then latched permanently. Five of six diagrams degenerated after a 61 ms burst, deterministically, with a **completely silent console**, because nothing had thrown. (c) cannot see that failure at all, since `data-processed` is `"true"` on a degenerate render; nor can a has-an-svg check. That is why the viewBox floor exists, and why 24 is the threshold: the failure signature is 16 units and the smallest genuine extent in the deck is 76. (d) is (b)'s defect by another route. Verified over three 15-key bursts: 0 degenerate in 12 settled classifications, with a MutationObserver catching the bad svg created at t=131.8 ms and discarded at t=134.6 ms — a 2.8 ms window inside one settle turn — after which nothing mutated for 4.1 s. | The floor is a magic number. It is 3.2× below the smallest real extent, so a genuine diagram would have to shrink dramatically to trip it, and the §7.6 census records every extent so the margin is checkable. The queue adds one branch to the settle path; nothing else in the deck depends on it. |
| D372 | **Declare `htmlLabels: false` at the top level as well as under `flowchart`, and stop overriding Mermaid's own intrinsic width cap.** The `!important` is removed from `.mermaid svg { max-width: 100% }`, so a diagram is drawn at its authored size or reduced to fit, never enlarged. | (a) The flowchart-scoped key alone. (b) Keep HTML labels and give the label `<div>` a wider `max-width`. (c) Keep HTML labels and remove `overflow: hidden` from the svg so nothing is clipped. (d) Reduce the font size until the longest label fits at the narrowest supported viewport. | Mermaid measures an HTML label with `getBoundingClientRect`, which reports this deck's CSS-transformed stage in **screen** pixels, so a node box was sized by the stage scale while the label inside it was not. The contamination was exact and measurable: slide 5's viewBox width fitted `76 + 200 × Reveal.getScale()` across six viewports at **R² = 1.000000**, the 200 being Mermaid's inline `max-width: 200px` on the label div. Labels overran their boxes at 1440 and were destroyed by the svg's `overflow: hidden` below it — "Three foundations" read "Three found" at 1024. (a) was tried and is **inert for node labels**: `getConfig().flowchart.htmlLabels` was correctly `false` while all 31 labels remained `foreignObject`, because 11.4.0's unified renderer reads the top-level key when it builds a node label, honouring the scoped one only for cluster and edge labels. (b) and (c) treat the symptom and leave the geometry scale-dependent. (d) makes the deck's smallest text smaller still. An SVG label is measured with `getBBox` in user units, which no ancestor transform can distort. After: 0 `foreignObject` deck-wide, the scale coefficient collapsed from +200.000000 to **−0.819171** at R² 0.362, total viewBox spread **0.38 %** across a 4.8× scale range, line breaking **byte-identical** at all six viewports, no truncation anywhere, and all 31 labels inside both their node box and the svg on all four sides — slide 3's worst case moving from **+67.59 px of overflow to −24.29 px of clearance**. | Removing the `!important` means a diagram can only shrink to fit, so the one width-bound diagram — slide 3, pinned at 99.99 % of the 1560 px column — renders its labels at 20.27 px against 24.00 px elsewhere. That is the deck's smallest text, level with its table headers, and the band is 1.18× where it was 2.44×. Narrowing it further needs the diagram's content changed, not its configuration. |
| D373 | **Align every diagram's left edge with the content column by removing horizontal centring at both levels, including the modifier that survived the first pass.** `.mermaid` is `margin: 24px 0 0`, `.mermaid svg` is `margin: 0`, `.mermaid > *` is `text-align: left`, and `.mermaid.deck-diagram-sm` is `margin: 20px 0 0`. | (a) Keep the diagrams centred and accept that they do not share the column. (b) Centre the other content to match the diagrams. (c) Widen `.mermaid` to the full column so centring becomes a no-op. | Every heading, table and paragraph in the deck begins at x = 145.92; the diagrams began at +65 to +451 px, which reads as accidental rather than as a choice. Two causes compounded: `.mermaid { margin: … auto … }` centred the container inside the 1696 px column and `.mermaid svg { margin: 0 auto }` centred the svg inside the container. (a) is the finding. (b) rebuilds the deck's layout to accommodate one element type. (c) changes how large every diagram draws in order to move it, and would undo D372's sizing.
