The row exists in this form because the first pass fixed the base rules and **missed** `.deck-diagram-sm`, whose `margin: 20px auto 0` overrode them: the pre resolves to 1560 px inside 1696 px, so `auto` split the 136 px of slack into 68 px per side, and 68 × 0.96 was exactly the 65.28 px still measured on slides 9 and 15. After: all six diagrams at Δ 0.00, and every direct child of both those sections reporting one x value. | Mermaid begins painting at viewBox x ≈ 8, so the first *visible* node border still sits 6–8 px right of the heading even at Δ 0.00. Left as-is: correcting it would mean cropping the viewBox the renderer emits. |
| D374 | **Give a paragraph that follows a table the same 28 px the table already carries above itself**, with `.reveal .slides section table + p { margin-top: 28px }`. | (a) Give `table` a `margin-bottom`. (b) Add a class to the one paragraph. (c) Change `section p`'s bottom margin to a top margin. | The table's `margin: 28px 0 0` and the paragraph's `margin: 0 0 24px` left a computed gap of exactly **zero** on slide 7 — the paragraph's cap height met the table's bottom border. (a) would add space after every table including those that end a slide. (b) puts layout in the markup for a condition the selector can state structurally, and the next such paragraph would silently miss it. (c) rewrites the deck's vertical rhythm to fix one adjacency. The adjacent-sibling selector states the actual condition — a paragraph *after* a table — and 28 px reuses the value already in the file rather than introducing a new one. After: 26.88 screen px, which is 28.00 CSS px exactly. | It fires wherever that adjacency occurs; today that is slide 7 only. A future slide inheriting the gap is the intended behaviour. |
| D375 | **Keep both bullet-marker treatments, and record the contrast that makes them different rather than unifying them.** Content slides keep the `--gradient-accent-bar` marker; the dark closing slide keeps the solid `--blitzy-accent-teal`. | (a) Use the gradient everywhere. (b) Use solid teal everywhere. (c) Introduce a third, mid-tone marker for both. | The difference was reported as an inconsistency, and measured it is a requirement: the gradient's violet terminal `#5B39F3` reaches only **2.60:1** against the closing slide's `#1A105F`, below the 3:1 minimum for non-text content, while `#94FAD5` reaches **13.17:1**. So (a) makes three bullets on the deck's final slide effectively invisible. (b) discards the brand gradient on eight content slides to solve a problem that exists only on one dark slide. (c) invents a token. Geometry is already identical — both are 16 × 16 px with a 5 px radius — so the only difference is the fill, and the fill is the thing that has to change with the background. | A reader comparing the two slides side by side still sees two treatments. Bounded by the reason being recorded here and by a comment in both stylesheets pointing at this row. |
| D376 | **Leave `flowchart.wrappingWidth` at its 200-user-unit default, accepting that the architecture diagram's four authored lines render as seven.** | (a) Raise `wrappingWidth` to ≥ 312 so each authored segment fits on one line. (b) Lower the diagram font to ≤ 15.4 px, which fits the segments at the current width. (c) Set the width per-diagram with an `%%{init}%%` directive. (d) Shorten the label text. | Under D372's SVG labels, `wrappingWidth` is measured in user units and no longer tracks the font, so Mermaid wraps three of the four `<br/>`-separated segments — their widths at 24 px are 210.4, 312.1, 290.1 and 160.9 — giving 2+2+2+1 = 7 lines. Nothing is lost: the rendered lines joined reproduce the authored string character for character, every authored segment still *begins* a fresh line, all seven are legible, and the label sits 24.29 px inside its box where it used to overflow by 67.59 px and be occluded by a neighbouring node. (a) un-wraps that label and two others, adding roughly 310 user units to a diagram that is already pinned at 99.99 % of its column, which drops its effective label size to about **17.3 px** — below the deck's smallest other text at 20 px — and to 18.85 px even if the column were widened to its full 1696 px. (b) shrinks every diagram in the deck to fix one label. (c) is unavailable: the word-count contract counts raw Mermaid source, so a directive inside a diagram changes the measured slide counts. (d) changes the deck's content to satisfy its renderer. Legibility and the size floor outrank the authored line count, and no finding specifies a line count. | The rendered shape of one diagram differs from the shape its author wrote, and a future editor may re-wrap it by hand without knowing why. That is what this row is for. Raising the font also raises the wrap risk elsewhere — slide 13's "Screens blocked" is 189.2 units and wraps above 25.37 px — so `wrappingWidth` would have to be scaled with any font change. |
| D377 | **Give the harness a last-position catch-all route that names the path it could not match**, rendering a `role="status"` element carrying `data-harness-error="harness-route-not-defined"`, the requested pathname, the four routes that do exist, and the file to edit. | (a) Leave it: an unmatched path is a developer error and React Router logs a warning. (b) Redirect an unmatched path to `/`. (c) Answer 404 from the dev server instead of rendering anything. (d) Add a heading or navigation to every route so any page is identifiable. | An unmatched path rendered an empty `<main>`, and `/tweets` legitimately renders an empty container, so the two produced **byte-identical** screenshots — same 22,610 bytes, same SHA-256, 0 of 1,296,000 pixels differing. A mistyped URL was therefore indistinguishable from a working route, and the only trace was a development-mode console warning. (a) is that state. (b) hides the mistake by silently showing a different, working page. (c) breaks the SPA contract the four routes depend on and contradicts D378. (d) is the alternative D362 already rejected for the same reason: it would alter what every spec sees of the components under test. This adds nothing to any of the four workspaces, because `<Routes>` renders exactly one match and this entry is last — verified by all four post-change screenshots being byte-identical to their pre-change captures, with every `<main>` still holding exactly one element child. It follows the harness's existing idiom, naming a condition and its remedy the way `harness-api-not-intercepted` does. | React Router's `No routes matched` warning no longer fires, because the catch-all matches. `e2e/tests/tweets.spec.ts` asserts that absence deliberately, as the proof that a route matched, so a future release that stopped emitting the warning for some other reason would not be noticed by that assertion alone - the diagnostic's own presence, asserted beside it, is what carries the claim. `browserDiagnostics` otherwise fails only on a `console.error` or `pageerror`. A spec that one day counts `main`'s children on a path it mistypes will now see one element rather than none — which is the point. |
| D378 | **Normalise the `Accept` header on a client-route request the SPA history fallback would refuse, rather than leaving the route surface client-dependent.** A fourth `enforce: 'pre'` plugin, ordered after both guards, substitutes `text/html` on a GET or HEAD request for an extension-less path that is not a module URL, a control path, `/favicon.ico`, or one of the three `HARNESS_API_SURFACE` keys. | (a) Document the sensitivity and require callers to send `Accept: text/html`, which is what the README did. (b) Rewrite `req.url` to `/index.html` in the middleware, replicating the fallback. (c) Pass `htmlAcceptHeaders` through to `connect-history-api-fallback`. (d) Serve the harness with `appType: 'custom'` and own the fallback outright. | `appType: 'spa'` routes through `connect-history-api-fallback`, which refuses unless the header is present, does not lead with `application/json`, and names `text/html` or a bare wildcard — read out of the bundled copy in Vite 4.5.14 rather than from documentation. So `/tweets` answered 404 to a client asking for JSON, asking for a script, or **sending no `Accept` at all**, and the harness entry to a browser. Measured: 30 of 60 method/header combinations failed, and all 10 no-header combinations. (a) was in place and was **itself wrong** — it named `Accept: */*` as the failing value when a wildcard is accepted and always was, so the documented workaround pointed at the wrong cause. (b) duplicates logic Vite already owns. (c) is not reachable through `appType`. (d) replaces a working fallback to change one header. The route table is a property of the harness, not of the requesting client. Ordering is load-bearing and was proved rather than assumed: `/@id/__x00__extless:C:/not/a/harness/module` is extension-less and this predicate would match it, yet it still answers 403 because the filesystem guard runs first. After: 60/60 combinations and 10/10 no-header requests answer 200 `text/html`, and a 28-cell raw-socket probe confirms every 503, 403 and 204 unchanged under both a present and an absent header. | A path that reaches the fallback is answered with the harness entry instead of a 404, so a genuinely wrong URL now looks served at the HTTP layer — which is exactly what D377 makes visible in the page. The middleware rewrites one header and never responds, so it cannot answer a path either guard refused. |
| D379 | **Add `'self'` to the deck's CSP `connect-src` and correct the head comment that described the directive.** | (a) Leave it: nothing in the deck issues a same-origin request today. (b) Drop `connect-src` and let it fall back to `default-src 'none'`. (c) Widen it to `*`. | The policy allowed `https://cdn.jsdelivr.net` and nothing else, so a same-origin `fetch` of the deck's own sibling stylesheet was refused in 1 ms with a `securitypolicyviolation` naming `connect-src` — a same-origin read of a file already served from the same directory. (a) leaves a policy whose stated intent and actual effect differ, which is the kind of drift the head comment exists to prevent. (b) removes the directive that constrains egress, which is the one worth keeping. (c) discards the restriction entirely. `'self'` is the narrowest value that admits the deck's own origin and nothing new. After: the same fetch returns 200 with 23,295 bytes and zero violation events, while a control fetch of an off-origin URL still throws with `violatedDirective connect-src` — so the policy is demonstrably still enforcing. | A future same-origin endpoint on this host becomes reachable from the deck without a further policy change. Bounded by the deck being a static file with no same-origin API. |


## §43 QA-execution remediation — the hygiene, closure, evidence-integrity and published-figure defects a full runtime execution of the delivery found

Scope: the thirteen findings a QA agent raised by *executing* the whole configuration and observability
chain from a clean state — three installs, every runner, every gate, all twenty artifacts, both Codecov
inputs and every workflow `run:` block — rather than by reading it. None is a functional failure of the
delivered suite: every delivered test case passed in that execution and every artifact was produced and
numerically aligned. Seven are repository-hygiene, dependency-closure, evidence-integrity and
published-figure defects in files this work owns. The other six were raised as informational, and they
did not all resolve the same way: three turned out to be configuration this delivery owns and could fix
outright - an unreachable upload condition, a missing partial-run guard and two inherited retention
values - one was a document stating a measurable falsehood, and two are true statements about the
platform or the authorized boundary, recorded rather than changed. The rows below record what changed
and, where a boundary made a root cause unreachable, which clause put it there.

| Row | Decision | Alternatives | Why | Risks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D380 | **Ignore the two generated npm lockfiles, at their anchored paths, rather than committing them or leaving them untracked.** `.gitignore` gains `frontend/package-lock.json` and `e2e/package-lock.json`; the rationale block that previously explained why no such rule existed now explains the rule; and the Jest case that asserted the absence (`frontend/src/test-utils/dependency-closure.test.ts`) now asserts the delivered shape — exactly those two anchored rules and no bare one — with a mirror case in `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` so the property is still asserted on a host that never installed the frontend tree. | (a) Commit both lockfiles and switch the three `npm install` steps of `ci.yml` to `npm ci`, which would also freeze the transitive graph `D381` measures. (b) Leave both files untracked and unignored, as delivered. (c) Add one bare `package-lock.json` rule instead of two anchored ones. (d) Delete the lockfiles in a CI step after each install. | (a) is the better engineering answer and is **not available**: AAP §0.6.2 states the decision — "`frontend/package-lock.json` — deliberately not committed. The authorized frontend configuration surface is `package.json` only, so `npm ci` becomes `npm install`" — and a frozen plan clause outranks a preference. It stays on the backlog as an owner ask. (b) was the delivered state, and it means the documented install pollutes a clean tree: measured, `npm install` in both trees left `?? frontend/package-lock.json` and `?? e2e/package-lock.json`, neither tracked nor ignored, which defeats a clean `git status` as a review signal. (c) would suppress a lockfile at *any* depth, including one someone later commits at another path, which is the silent case worth refusing. (d) hides the file from review instead of deciding about it. | The rules make `git add` alone insufficient if the decision is reversed: `git add -f` is required, and someone who does not know that could conclude the lockfile "cannot" be committed. Mitigated by saying so in the `.gitignore` block itself, in `frontend/TESTING.md` §1 and in the backlog entry. The reproducibility residual `D381` describes is unchanged by this row — it neither widens nor closes it. |
| D381 | **Publish the measured `engines.node` state of the installed closure and route the fix to an owner, rather than papering over it or asserting it in a test.** `frontend/TESTING.md` §1 replaces its single-package `EBADENGINE` note with the audited table, `e2e/README.md` §2 replaces "anything from 16 upward works locally" with the measurement, and the root `README.md` backlog entry states the three ways out. | (a) Add an `engines` field to `frontend/package.json`. (b) Raise `node-version` in `ci.yml` to a runtime the closure supports. (c) Commit lockfiles to freeze the graph. (d) Add a test asserting the exact set of excluded packages. (e) Add a test asserting every installed package admits the declared floor. (f) Say nothing, since no *direct* pin has drifted. | Measured by walking every installed `package.json` and evaluating `semver.satisfies(target, engines.node)`: on Node 16.20.2, **four** packages in `frontend/node_modules` are excluded — `@testing-library/dom@10.4.1` (`>=18`, under the frozen `@testing-library/user-event ^14.4.3`), `@inquirer/external-editor@1.0.3` (`>=18`, under `msw@1.3.5` then `inquirer@8.2.7`), `postcss-load-config@6.0.1` (`>= 18`, under the floating `tailwindcss ^3.3.2` resolving 3.4.19) and `node-releases@2.0.53` (`>=18`) — plus **one** in `e2e/node_modules`, `node-releases@2.0.53`; on Node 22.23.1, **zero** in either tree. (a) is excluded by AAP §0.8.1, which scopes that file to devDependencies and test scripts. (b) would contradict the basis of every pin in the suite: AAP §0.6.1 selected `msw 1.3.5`, `jest-junit 16.0.0`, `@playwright/test 1.44.1` and the Jest 29.x line *because* of the 16.x ceiling, so moving the ceiling is a re-selection of the toolchain rather than a one-line edit. (c) is excluded by AAP §0.6.2 (`D380`). (d) would be flaky by construction — with the graph floating, a later install legitimately resolves different transitive versions, so the assertion would fail on a change it is not measuring. (e) would fail on the runtime CI declares, turning an accurate documentation defect into a red suite. (f) is the defect: the documents claimed the closure supported the declared ceiling, and one of them additionally told the reader to investigate any `EBADENGINE` naming a test package — which `msw`'s own transitive tree now produces. | The measurement is true of the tree that produced it and goes stale on the next install, because the graph is floating; that is the residual, and it is the same one the lockfile ask exists to close. The `msw` chain is the sharpest form of it: the pin still does what it was chosen for while the closure no longer does. Nothing here has been executed on Node 16 in this environment — no Node 16 is installed — so the exclusions are proven from package metadata rather than from a run, and the documents now say so instead of implying a run. |
| D382 | **Pin `python-dotenv==0.21.1` and ignore `.env`, so that a documented configuration step neither breaks the suite nor risks committing a credential.** The manifest gains the pin under a one-line `# CONFIG:` marker; `.gitignore` gains `.env`; `backend/tests/README.md` §7 gains Pitfall 5. | (a) Document in `backend/tests/README.md` that a `.env` must not exist, and pin nothing. (b) Change the pin to `pydantic[dotenv]==1.10.13`. (c) Remove `env_file = ".env"` from `Settings.Config`. (d) Ignore `.env` only, leaving the import failure in place. | `app/core/config.py` declares `env_file = ".env"`, and pydantic v1 attempts the dotenv read **whenever the file exists**, so with no `python-dotenv` installed the entire backend suite became non-collectable: `conftest.py`'s `pytest_configure` cannot import `app.core.config` and raises `UsageError … ImportError: python-dotenv is not installed`. The root `README.md` Configuration section instructs `cp .env.example .env`, so the failure is reachable through a documented action, and that section is not editable here (AAP §0.10.5 C3 keeps its pre-existing text byte-for-byte). Two facts settled it against (a): the conftest docstring already anticipates this exact ImportError, and the readiness gate already emits a diagnostic naming "in backend/.env" for a field a `.env` supplied — a message only reachable when dotenv is installed, so the delivery's own diagnostics presuppose the dependency. (b) is the same distribution arriving through an extra, which hides it from the exact-pin census the closure gate walks. (c) is production code outside the two authorized touches. (d) closes the credential hazard and leaves the break. The suite stays hermetic because pydantic v1 resolves `os.environ` **before** the `.env` source and the prologue assigns every managed name unconditionally — verified by running the gated suite with a `.env` in place. | One more distribution in the manifest, and a `.env` on disk is now *read* rather than rejected, so a stray entry for an unmanaged field reaches `Settings`. That case is not silent: the readiness gate names the field and refuses the run. `.env` being ignored also means a developer's file no longer shows in `git status`, which is the intent for a credential file and a change of visibility worth stating. |
| D383 | **Assert the `case_sensitive` declaration rather than its effect, and document why, rather than adding a rejection test.** `backend/tests/README.md` §7 gains Pitfall 6; `tests/unit/test_core_config.py` is unchanged. | (a) Add a test asserting a lowercase `secret_key` does **not** satisfy `SECRET_KEY`. (b) Skip such a test on `win32`. (c) Record nothing, since the declaration is already asserted. | `Settings.Config.case_sensitive = True` is inert on Windows because `os.environ` is itself case-insensitive there — measured: setting `qa_LoWeR_Probe` makes `QA_LOWER_PROBE` readable, so `popularity_threshold=999` also sets `POPULARITY_THRESHOLD`. On the `ubuntu-latest` runner `ci.yml` declares, the declaration is effective. (a) would therefore pass on CI and fail on a Windows developer machine: a test whose result depends on the host rather than on the code, which is exactly the class this suite exists to avoid. (b) encodes the platform split in a skip that no longer proves anything on the platform most likely to run it locally. (c) leaves the next maintainer to write (a) and discover the divergence the hard way. The `Config` block is pre-existing production code outside the two authorized touches, so nothing changes it. | The behaviour remains untested on both platforms — only the declaration is asserted. That is a real coverage residual, and it is the price of a portable assertion; the pitfall entry states it so the choice is visible rather than looking like an omission. |
| D384 | **Guard the frontend result stream on the reason its skips carry, not on a case count or an expected skip total.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`'s frontend verify step and `docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py` both require every `<skipped>` case in `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml` to be one whose name carries the `BLOCKED:` marker; the extractor withdraws the stream when one does not, so `--require-all` exits non-zero and no panel, KPI or trend figure is built from it. | (a) Compare the stream's case count against a readiness witness, as the backend does (`D355`). (b) Assert the skipped count equals the 24 reasoned skips the documents publish. (c) Assert a floor on the passed count. (d) Capture the canonical run's own summary line to a new artifact and compare against it. (e) Leave the presence check as delivered, since CI always runs the unfiltered script. | (a) **cannot work here**, and that is the finding's substance: a Jest name filter does not drop the tests it excludes, it registers every identity and re-classifies the excluded ones as skipped. Measured, `npx jest --ci --watchAll=false -t "formatNumber"` replaces the canonical stream with one that still declares `tests="371" failures="0"` while reporting 300 skipped and 71 passed - so it satisfies a presence check, a non-zero check, and any count equality, including the extractor's existing identity comparison. (b) and (c) work but need a figure maintained by hand in two places, and each goes stale the moment a blocker is resolved or a suite is added. (d) adds an artifact, a producer command, a contract row and a fill slot to the dashboard for something the stream already carries. (e) leaves the exposure exactly where it is: CI is unaffected, but a locally produced stream that is then published is the case this delivery's own dashboard is filled from. The marker check needs no expected count, is derived from a convention the three page suites already follow, and stays correct as reasoned skips are added or resolved. | The check is only as good as the convention: a deliberate skip added without a `BLOCKED:` reason in its title would be reported as a filtered run. That is a false positive with an obvious message and an obvious fix - state the reason - and it is the direction of error worth having, since the alternative is a partial run published as a full one. It also does not detect a filtered run in which *no* test was excluded, which is a filtered run that produced the whole suite and is therefore not the defect. |
| D385 | **Give the end-to-end verify step the same case-count equality the backend has, against the discovery output.** `ci.yml`'s e2e verify step now reads the `Total: N tests in M files` line out of `reports/list-tests.txt` and requires the stream's root `tests` attribute to equal it, naming both figures on a mismatch, and additionally requires that discovery output to exist and be non-empty. | (a) Leave the presence check, since `dashboard-extract.py` already compares discovered against declared. (b) Assert an expected count of 31 in the workflow. (c) Use the marker approach `D384` takes for the frontend. | Playwright, unlike Jest, genuinely drops the tests a filter excludes, so the stream's own count *is* discriminating and the discovery step the job already runs is an independent witness of the full figure - the same shape as the backend's check, which is what makes the three consistent rather than three different ideas. Measured: a filtered run replaced the stream with a 3-case one and satisfied every check in that step before this comparison existed. (a) leaves the workflow unable to detect what its own extractor can, which is the asymmetry the finding raises; the extractor catching it after the fact does not stop the upload from publishing it. (b) hard-codes a figure that changes whenever a spec is added. (c) does not apply: Playwright skips carry a reason in the stream and there are none in this suite. | Both figures come from the same job, so a change that broke discovery and the run in the same way would satisfy the equality. That is the same residual the backend's check carries and is bounded by the readiness step running first and being retained. The `reports/list-tests.txt` existence check makes the discovery step a hard prerequisite of the verify step, so removing it now fails the job rather than silently disabling the comparison. |
| D386 | **State `if-no-files-found` on all three single-file result-stream uploads, with the value each one's guard justifies, and give the two that inherited it an explicit 30-day retention.** `backend-junit` and `frontend-junit` gain `retention-days: 30` and `if-no-files-found: error`; `e2e-junit` gains `if-no-files-found: warn`. | (a) Add `error` to all three, uniformly. (b) Add `retention-days` only, leaving `if-no-files-found` inherited. (c) Leave both inherited, since the directory artifacts already retain the same files. | An omitted `retention-days` takes the repository or organisation default - 90 days unless someone changed it, decided somewhere the workflow cannot see - which contradicts the principle the `e2e-junit` upload states in its own comment; these two streams carry no attachment body, so 30 days matches the other result artifacts. (a) is wrong on one of the three, and the reason is worth stating: the e2e job's browser gate is fail-closed, so when no launchable browser resolves, `Run end-to-end tests` is skipped and no stream is written at all - `error` there would convert a correct refusal into a second, misleading failure. The other two are written by steps carrying `if: ${{ !cancelled() }}`, and pytest and jest-junit both write their stream even when tests fail, so absence means removal or a runner that never reached the suite. (b) leaves a value decided by a default rather than by the file, which is the finding's actual complaint. (c) leaves both. | These three uploads are guarded by `if: always()` rather than `if: ${{ !cancelled() }}`, deliberately, so a stream survives a cancellation; a cancelled run that never wrote the stream will now fail these two upload steps rather than skip them. The job's conclusion is already `cancelled` in that case, so the cost is an extra annotation on an already-terminated run. |
| D387 | **Upload the three end-to-end evidence globs rather than the whole `test-results` directory, so that `if-no-files-found: warn` means what the comment beside it claims.** `ci.yml`'s `e2e-failure-artifacts` upload names `**/*.png`, `**/*.webm` and `**/trace.zip` under `e2e/test-results/`. | (a) Keep the directory path and amend the comment to say the condition never fires. (b) Keep the directory path and switch to `if-no-files-found: ignore`. (c) Delete `.last-run.json` in a step before the upload. | `e2e/test-results/` is never empty: Playwright writes `.last-run.json` on every run and leaves one empty directory per test, so a whole-directory path always matched at least one file and the `warn` branch was unreachable - the comment described an intent the configuration could not express. Naming the evidence makes the condition mean what it says: on a green run the globs match nothing and the step warns, which is the intended signal that there was no failure to record. Measured both ways - a passing run matched none of the three, a controlled failure matched all three. (a) documents a dead condition rather than fixing it. (b) silences the signal instead of making it accurate. (c) deletes evidence to satisfy a check, and `.last-run.json` is the only record of which tests a run considered failed. | The artifact no longer carries `.last-run.json` or the empty per-test directories, so a reader who used it to see *which* tests failed loses that index; the Playwright HTML report and the JUnit stream, both uploaded separately, carry the same information. The globs are also a list that has to grow if Playwright is ever configured to retain another attachment type. |
| D388 | **Publish the first-pass figure beside the warm one and bind the pair arithmetically, rather than replacing the headline or removing the two conditional skips.** `README.md` and `backend/tests/README.md` now state both figures and name the artifact each conditional skip reads; `test_docs_contract.py` asserts that the difference between them is exactly the number of artifact-conditional skips, that the first-pass skip count is the reasoned skips plus those, and that the first-pass pair sums to the collected total. | (a) Publish the first-pass figure as the canonical one. (b) Make both tests fail rather than skip when their artifact is absent. (c) Produce the two artifacts as a fixture so the figures converge. (d) Commit the two artifacts. (e) State the precondition in prose and bind nothing. | The published `1078 passed / 3 skipped` was not reproducible on a first run from a fresh clone, which returns `1076 passed and 5 skipped`: `tests/test_coverage_gate.py` reads `backend/coverage.json` and `tests/test_docs_contract.py` reads `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml`, both gitignored, so neither exists until a previous run produced it, and each skips with a reason naming the artifact. Both figures are correct readings of the same 1081-case collection; the defect was publishing one without the condition. (a) makes the figure a reader sees after any real session wrong instead, and understates a suite that is not skipping anything by choice. (b) turns a fresh clone red on two cases whose subject genuinely has not been produced - the skip with a stated reason is the honest disposition. (c) would have a unit test run the gated coverage command or the Playwright suite to satisfy itself, which is a test that produces its own subject. (d) commits generated artifacts and puts a coverage reading into review, which `D380` and the `.gitignore` scope exist to prevent. (e) is what every earlier restatement did, and prose alone is what went stale - the arithmetic binding is the part that cannot. | The binding is arithmetic rather than measured: it proves the two published figures are consistent with each other and with the collected total, not that either was observed. A future third conditional skip would have to be added to the tuple the test reads, and the failure message says so. |
| D389 | **Correct the documented root-misuse figure to the measured one and bind the triple to the collected total.** `README.md`'s bare-`pytest`-from-the-root paragraph now reads `21 failed, 1057 passed, 3 skipped`, and a new case asserts the three numbers sum to the collected figure published two lines above. | (a) Restate the passed count and bind nothing. (b) Replace the triple with "1081 collected, 21 errored". (c) Delete the figure, since it describes a misuse. | The published `991` was stale and arithmetically impossible: `21 + 991 + 3 = 1015` against a collection the same paragraph states as 1081. Measured on the delivered tree, a bare root `pytest` produces `21 failed, 1057 passed, 3 skipped`, and `21 + 1057 + 3 = 1081`. Everything else the paragraph said was already correct - the failure count, the skip count and the mechanism (no `configfile` is loaded, so `asyncio_mode = auto` is off, pytest reports `asyncio: mode=strict`, and every `async def` test errors for want of a decorator). (a) leaves the figure free to go stale again on the next added test, and this figure has now gone stale twice. (b) loses the shape a reader matches against their own terminal, which is the whole purpose of publishing it. (c) removes the one paragraph that makes an easily-misread failure recognisable; the failure looks like broken code rather than a wrong working directory. | The bound sum does not prove the failure count: a change that made 22 tests error would satisfy the arithmetic only if the passed count were restated too, which is the intended coupling, but nothing asserts that 21 is the number of async tests. The figure is also platform-independent only as far as the async-decorator mechanism is, which is a property of `pytest-asyncio`'s strict mode rather than of the host. |
| D390 | **Re-measure and restate every published suite-size figure again, in one pass placed after the last test-adding change.** Nine documents and the deck were swept to the measured tree: collected `1081`, warm `1078 passed / 3 skipped`, first pass `1076 / 5`, the per-command table (`test_dependency_closure.py` 73, `test_dashboard_extract.py` 36, `test_docs_contract.py` 97) and its closing arithmetic `492 + 3 + 150 + 73 + 62 + 168 + 36 + 97 = 1081`, the root-misuse triple, and the deck KPI from `1,443` to `1,456`. | (a) Restate after each fix-and-verify group. (b) Leave the figures at the previous checkpoint's readings and note only that the suite grew. (c) Add a test binding the published collected figure to the retained readiness artifact so the sweep is enforced rather than remembered. | This is the same protocol `D365` set, applied for the same reason: eight of the changes in this checkpoint add test cases, so restating after each group would have republished the same figures three times over, each time knowing the next group would move them again. (b) leaves the defect class two of this checkpoint's own findings are about. (c) is genuinely attractive and was rejected on a specific ground: the collected count can only be read from `backend/reports/collect-only.txt`, which is gitignored, so the binding would add a **third** artifact-conditional skip - and the divergence between a warm and a first-pass reading is exactly the defect `D388` was closing. Every figure here was read out of a run rather than arithmetic: `pytest --collect-only -q` for 1081, the gated producer for 1078/3, the same command with `backend/coverage.json` and `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml` moved aside for 1076/5, seven per-file `--collect-only` invocations for the table, and a bare root `pytest` for the misuse triple. The sweep script refused to write anything unless every one of its 23 replacements matched its expected occurrence count exactly, so a stale figure could not be half-replaced. | These figures are true of this tree and go stale the moment a test is added; the arithmetic bindings `D388` and `D389` add are the durable half and the restatement is the perishable half, exactly as `D365` said. `D354`'s freeze still holds: no figure inside a decision row or a dated evidence row was touched, so `D307`, `D309`, `D333`, `D353` and `D365` still read as the history they are. |
| D391 | **Correct the harness `Accept`-sensitivity note to the measured boundary, and state which client produces the failing case.** `e2e/README.md` §3 and §6 previously said a request carrying `Accept: */*` returns 404; both now carry the measured four-row table and name PowerShell 5.1's `Invoke-WebRequest -UseBasicParsing` as the client that sends no `Accept` at all. | (a) Leave the note, since its practical advice - send `Accept: text/html` - was already right. (b) Configure `appType: 'custom'` and write an explicit fallback that ignores `Accept`. (c) Add a spec asserting the 404. | The note was wrong in a testable way, which is worse than vague: measured against the running harness on `/tweets`, `Accept: text/html` → 200, `Accept: */*` → **200**, `Accept: application/json` → 404, and **no `Accept` header** → 404. Vite 4.5.14 delegates the fallback to `connect-history-api-fallback`, which requires `text/html` or `*/*`, so the boundary is a *missing* header rather than a broad one - and a reader who trusted the old note would conclude their `curl` probe could not work, when curl's default is exactly the value that does. (b) replaces a working, standard fallback to accommodate a probe, and `appType: 'spa'` is what supplies the history fallback the repository otherwise lacks. (c) would assert a property of a third-party connect middleware rather than of anything this delivery owns, and the suite is unaffected: real browsers and `fetch()` always send an `Accept`, and Playwright's `webServer.url` readiness treats even a 404 as "server up". | The measurement is of Vite 4.5.14 and its bundled fallback middleware; a Vite upgrade could move the boundary, and nothing asserts it. That is accepted because the alternative is a test of a vendor's middleware, and because the consequence of being wrong is a confusing manual probe rather than a failing suite. |
| D392 | **Record the action-pinning asymmetry as an owner task with the audit attached, rather than pinning the five floating references.** The root `README.md` backlog gains an entry stating the measured split and naming the five. | (a) Pin all five now. (b) Pin only the two Codecov steps, since they sit beside test steps. (c) Record nothing, since the asymmetry follows from the authorized boundary. | Measured from the workflow at runtime: **9 of 14 `uses:` references are SHA-pinned, 5 are floating** - `actions/checkout@v2`, `actions/setup-python@v2`, `actions/setup-node@v2`, and the two `codecov/codecov-action@v1` steps, all in the `build` job. Every reference this delivery added is pinned; every floating one is in a step it may not touch. (a) and (b) edit non-test steps: `checkout` and the two `setup-*` steps are not test steps under AAP §0.8.1, and AAP §0.10.2 records the two Codecov steps as **reused unchanged**, which is precisely why they still carry `@v1`. Pinning them would be a small, safe change and the boundary still forbids it, which is what makes this an owner ask rather than a task. (c) leaves a real supply-chain residual undocumented in a delivery whose own `e2e` job pins everything - a reader would reasonably read the mixed file as carelessness rather than as a boundary. | A floating major tag is mutable, so each of those five resolves to whatever the tag points at on the day the workflow runs; `actions/checkout@v2` and `setup-python@v2`/`setup-node@v2` are also on Node 16 runtimes GitHub has deprecated, so they will warn and eventually stop working regardless of this decision. Nothing in this repository detects a change to what those tags resolve to. |
| D393 | **Add the missing frontend discovery command to both producer blocks of `docs/testing/DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`, correct the text-artifact count beside them, and bind both blocks to the extractor's `REQUIRED` contract.** Section 1 listed three commands and created three of the four text artifacts `dashboard-extract.py --require-all` demands: `frontend/reports/list-tests.txt` was absent from the POSIX block and from the PowerShell block, so a reader who followed the document exactly got `artifact not produced: frontend/reports/list-tests.txt` and exit 1. Both blocks now carry `npm run --silent test:list \| tee reports/list-tests.txt` in `frontend`, placed ahead of `test:load` as `ci.yml` orders them; the header reads four text artifacts — three readiness outputs and the frontend discovery census — and the new case `test_both_producer_blocks_create_every_required_text_artifact` requires every `.txt` path in `REQUIRED` to be written by both blocks. | Drop `FRONTEND_LIST_TESTS` from `REQUIRED`, making the three-command block correct. Downgrade `--require-all` to a warning. Fix the two blocks and add no binding. Call the artifact a fourth *readiness* file. Delete the block and link to `README.md`'s. | The artifact is genuinely required and genuinely produced — `ci.yml`'s `Check frontend test discovery` step writes it, §6.4 reports the census from it, and `README.md`'s own block already listed all four commands, so the dashboard had drifted from the delivery's own instruction rather than describing something unbuilt. Repairing the document was therefore the minimal change, and deleting the block was the wrong direction: `README.md` sends the reader here for *the full local sequence*, so this is the block a reader lands on. Dropping the requirement would remove the only frontend discovery census §6.4 has. A binding was added because the existing `test_dashboard_names_every_required_artifact_the_extractor_requires` asserts only that each required path appears *somewhere* in the document, and the §2 census row satisfied it while no command created the file — which is precisely how the omission survived. It compares full paths, because the frontend and end-to-end discovery outputs share the basename `list-tests.txt` and a basename check passes in the broken state. Calling the file a fourth readiness output was rejected as contradicting §5's `Three, one per layer` and §3.1 G5's reason for using `test:load` there. | The binding parses two fenced shell blocks and simulates `cd` — subshell-scoped for POSIX, stateful for PowerShell — so it asserts the *shape* of the instruction and not only its effect: rewriting section 1 as a loop, a script file or with `pushd` would fail it while the document stayed correct. Mitigated by selecting the blocks on content rather than position and by an assertion message that prints every path each block does write, and by the cross-shell equality that catches a command added to one shell only — both directions were negative-validated by deleting each line in turn. The added case moved every published suite-size figure once more: collected 1081 → **1082**, warm **1079 passed / 3 skipped**, first pass **1077 / 5**, bare-root misuse **21 failed, 1058 passed, 3 skipped**, `test_docs_contract.py` 97 → **98** and the deck KPI `1,456` → **`1,457`**, so the figures quoted by D388, D389 and D390 are the readings of their own checkpoints and D354 keeps them as history rather than as current state. |

## §44 QA-findings remediation — the harness's own diagnostics, and the fifty-two runtime findings raised against production screens

Scope: the fifty-two findings a runtime QA pass raised against the four harness routes, driving the real
production components in a real browser. The pass recorded **AAP compliance FULL** for every documented
behavioural claim, and its own scope statement declares implementing the missing `TweetCard`, `getTweets`,
`Chart.register`, store hooks, pages and services out of scope — so the findings are a census of the
condition of the code under test, not deviations from the plan. Three of them root-cause inside `e2e/`,
which §0.8.1 places wholly in scope, and are defects this delivery owns. Three more are cases where the
pass measured a blast radius **larger** than any document here stated, which is an accuracy defect in this
delivery's own prose. The remaining forty-six are production frontend defects the plan forbids repairing,
and D400 is the single adjudication that covers all of them. Two further rows record where that declined set
was written up rather than fixed: D401 appends three rows to the security register for the credential-handling
subset, and D402 states why none of the forty-six became a §D row in `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md`.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D394 | **Refines D143. Carry the body that D143 already drains into the message both harness `fetch` stubs throw, and redact submitted credentials out of the config stub's copy.** `getTrendData` and `updateTwitterAPIConfig` each gain a module-scope `excerptResponseBody`, which collapses whitespace to one line, caps the result at 300 characters while stating the full length when it truncates, and returns `(no body)` or `(body could not be read)` rather than throwing. The config stub's variant additionally replaces every non-empty submitted value with `[redacted credential]` **before** truncating. The status is still named, the body is still never parsed and never returned, and the error is still thrown on every path. | (a) Leave the drain as D143 wrote it: it exists for timeline visibility, and the reason is available in DevTools' network panel. (b) Surface the body only for a specific status range. (c) Surface the parsed `error` member alone, rather than an excerpt of the body. (d) Surface the body on the config stub too, without redaction. | The drain was already paying the cost of reading the body and then discarding what it read, and what it discarded was the only machine-readable statement of *why* a write or a read was refused — including this harness's own `harness-api-not-intercepted` remedy, which names the exact `page.route` call a spec is missing. (a) is defensible only for a human sitting in DevTools at the moment of failure; it is worth nothing to a CI log, a Playwright trace or a `browserDiagnostics` ledger, all three of which see the console and not the network panel. (b) adds a rule with no case behind it — every non-ok status has a reason worth reading. (c) presumes a shape: the responder is a spec's `route.fulfill`, which may answer anything, and an excerpt degrades gracefully where a member read does not. (d) was rejected on measurement rather than on principle: an endpoint that validates credentials and echoes what it received is unremarkable, and the QA pass had independently verified that no credential reaches the console, so shipping (d) would have traded a diagnostic gain for a regression in a property already proven. Redaction runs before truncation specifically so that no credential can survive as the tail of a cut-off excerpt. | The excerpt is attacker-controlled text in a developer's console, and a responder echoing a *fragment* of a credential would place that fragment there — whole occurrences are what is replaced. Bounded by the values a spec types being synthetic, asserted by `configuration.spec.ts`'s own synthetic-value gate, and by the two harness responders echoing the request line rather than the body. The cap means a genuinely long reason is cut; the stated character count is what tells a reader that happened. Two near-identical excerpt helpers now exist, one per stub, because only one of the two carries a redaction obligation and a shared module would have been a new file — see D399. |
| D395 | **Require `labels` and `values` to carry the same number of entries in the harness trend-series contract, checked ahead of the forwarding opt-in.** A mismatch now yields `TrendSeriesContractError` naming both counts. | (a) Leave it: both members were already checked for presence, array-ness and member type, which is what the file's brief asked for. (b) Check the lengths but only warn. (c) Truncate the longer array to the shorter one and forward the result. | The caller reads the two arrays in parallel, so a payload pairing 6 labels with 3 values describes no series and no conclusion about the component can be drawn from a run that supplies one. (a) is the finding, and its cost was measured: the payload passed the shape gate, was forwarded, and reached an unregistered `Chart` that throws before it touches either array — so the two defects hid each other, and the mismatch left no trace anywhere. Checking ahead of the opt-in is what makes that unbypassable; verified in a real browser, where a 200 carrying the forwarding header and a 6/3 body now names the mismatch and leaves the component mounted. (b) leaves a test able to pass over an incoherent fixture. (c) invents data on the responder's behalf, which is the one thing a stand-in must never do. | A spec that deliberately wants an unequal-length payload forwarded can no longer have one; nothing in the suite wants that, and the rejection names both counts so the reason is unambiguous. `e2e/fixtures/trends.json` carries 6 and 6 and is unaffected. |
| D396 | **Add no error boundary to `e2e/harness/main.tsx`, and assert the whole-root teardown instead.** `dashboard.spec.ts` and `analytics.spec.ts` each now assert that the `<main>` landmark the harness renders above `<Routes>` is gone and that `#root` is empty; `analytics.spec.ts` additionally drives a client-side history change afterwards and asserts the URL moves while nothing renders. | (a) Wrap `<Routes>` in a boundary so one failing route cannot take the page down. (b) Wrap it in a boundary only for the two ceiling tests. (c) Assert nothing beyond the route element's absence, as before. | The absence of a boundary is a property of the application, and the harness exists to let a spec observe the components unaltered. (a) would put page chrome in the harness that `frontend/src/app.tsx` does not have, and would hide from every spec the exact consequence two of them exist to measure; §0.8.2 rules out the alternative of adding one to `app.tsx`. (b) makes the harness behave differently under different specs, which defeats the one route table the layer is built on. (c) understates the outcome by exactly the amount the QA pass measured: a reader told "the route came down" expects the rest of the page to have survived, and none of it does. | The four routes now share a fate: a render fault in any one of them ends the page for all of them, and a future spec asserting cross-route behaviour after a deliberate crash will find nothing to assert. That is the application's behaviour, and G14 records it as a ceiling rather than as a harness limitation. |
| D397 | **[The harness half is superseded by D377 — `e2e/harness/main.tsx` does declare a last-position catch-all, because an unrouted path and `/tweets` were measured as byte-identical screenshots and the only trace of the difference was a development-mode console warning; alternative (b) below was taken after that measurement, and the mirror argument gives way to it because a mirror that cannot be told apart from a working route cannot be diagnosed. Everything below about `app.tsx` itself stands: no catch-all is added to the product, and the first test still measures both sides rather than assuming either.] Add no catch-all route to `e2e/harness/main.tsx`, and assert what an unmatched URL renders instead.** `tweets.spec.ts`'s first test now measures the working route's `document.title`, visible text and recovery-control count, then navigates to an unrouted path and asserts all three agree, that the tweet-list container is absent, that the landmark above `<Routes>` is attached and empty, and that React Router's console warning is the only signal anywhere. | (a) Add a `<Route path="*">` rendering a not-found page with a link home. (b) Add one rendering a harness-only diagnostic banner. (c) Record the gap in the register and assert nothing. | §0.4.6 fixes this route table as a mirror of `app.tsx`, and neither declares a catch-all; (a) is a missing product feature, which §0.1.4 forbids implementing "even where existing tests assert them", and the QA pass's own scope statement independently rules out manufacturing UX the product lacks. (b) is the same edit with a different label and it makes the harness stop mirroring the table it exists to exercise. (c) leaves the property unpinned, and the property is unusually worth pinning: the working `/tweets` route and two unrouted URLs render byte-identical blank pages, so nothing a user or a pixel diff can see distinguishes them, and `<main>` being present-and-empty versus absent is the only discriminator. Asserting it means supplying a not-found route later turns a passing test red on purpose. | The assertion is keyed on a route that renders no visible text, so a future change giving `/tweets` visible content will fail this test — correctly, because it would also end the indistinguishability the test records. The path `/no-such-route` is arbitrary; any unrouted path behaves identically, which the QA pass confirmed on `/tweets/999` as well. |
| D398 | **Restate the blast radius of both render ceilings everywhere this delivery understated it, and bind each restatement to an assertion rather than to prose.** Seven files said the throw "unmounts the route" or "unmounts the component"; it unmounts the whole React root. Corrected in `e2e/harness/main.tsx`, `e2e/harness/stubs/analyticsService.ts` (including the text of `UnrenderableTrendSeriesError`), `e2e/vite.harness.config.ts`, `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts`, `e2e/README.md`, `frontend/TESTING.md` and the `Chart.register` entry of `README.md`'s suggested-next-tasks list, and in §23 and rows G3 and G4 of `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md`. The feed's 30-second poll is additionally recorded as cleared and never reinstalled. | (a) Correct the prose and leave the assertions where they were. (b) Correct only the two documents a reader is most likely to open. (c) Leave it: "the route came down" is true as far as it goes. | Prose that no test holds is what drifted in the first place, so each corrected claim is now the text of an assertion: `main` count 0 and `#root.innerHTML === ''` in both ceiling tests, a post-teardown history change in `analytics.spec.ts`, and an in-page `setInterval`/`clearInterval` ledger in `dashboard.spec.ts`. (a) repeats the failure mode one checkpoint later. (b) leaves the same sentence wrong in four places, and one of them is an error message a developer reads at the moment of failure. (c) is the finding: it is true and it is misleading, because it invites the reader to assume containment that does not exist. The jsdom wording in `frontend/TESTING.md` was deliberately **not** given the root claim — under jsdom there is no 2D context, so `chart.js` returns before any registry lookup and no teardown happens at all. | The poll assertion reads a timer ledger rather than waiting out a 30 000 ms boundary, so it proves "the interval was cleared and not replaced" rather than "no request has arrived yet"; a future change that reinstalled the poll from outside the component would satisfy the ledger while still not refetching. Bounded by the same test asserting the request count. The ledger wraps `setInterval` and `clearInterval` from an init script and delegates to the originals, so it observes without altering timing. |
| D399 | **Close this checkpoint without adding a file or a test case to any layer, strengthening existing cases instead.** Every new assertion above lives inside a test that already existed; the end-to-end census stays at 31 cases in 5 spec files, the frontend at 371, the backend at 1068, and the deck's headline KPI at 1,443. | (a) Add a spec for the unmatched-route behaviour and a spec for the two stub diagnostics, then re-sweep every published figure. (b) Add one shared `e2e/harness/stubs/responseBody.ts` for the two excerpt helpers. (c) Add the assertions but skip the documentation the new counts would require. | §0.11.1 directs that where several approaches exist, the one requiring least modification wins — and here the difference is not stylistic. `test_docs_contract.py` binds the E2E census to a constant, to the retained JUnit stream's own root count and to roughly a dozen published claims across six documents plus a per-spec breakdown and the deck's three-stream KPI; and `test_every_delivered_file_is_named_by_a_section_f_row` binds any new file to a §F row plus five figures in the completeness table. So (a) and (b) each convert a ten-line change into a cross-document arithmetic exercise whose only product is a number, and every one of the new assertions has a natural home in a test whose subject is already the same behaviour. (b) additionally has a design cost: the two helpers differ, because only one of them carries a redaction obligation. (c) is not available — the contract tests would fail, which is what they are for. | Two near-identical 20-line helpers now exist in `e2e/harness/stubs/`, and a change to excerpting has to be made twice. Bounded by both being private to their module, by there being exactly two `fetch` call sites in `e2e/`, and by each stating its own contract. One retitled test — `tweets.spec.ts`'s first — deliberately still contains its old title as a substring, so `README.md`'s `npm test -- -g "renders an empty tweet-list container"` example keeps matching. |
| D400 | **Decline all forty-six QA findings whose only repair is production frontend code, and carry every one into §23 and `README.md`'s suggested-next-tasks list rather than into a fix.** The set spans all four screens: the undefined `TweetCard` and the missing `getTweets` export; the absent `Chart.register`; the absence of any error boundary, loading state, empty state, error state, pending state or outcome feedback; axios's `silentJSONParsing` turning a malformed body into a render-time `TypeError`; a non-array 200 doing the same; an unbounded unhandled-rejection loop, one per poll; an HTTP-cacheable response silently defeating the 30-second poll; an unguarded `setInterval`; `key={tweet.id}` on a shape with no `id`; a strict `===` scroll predicate guarding dead pagination; a canvas fixed at 300×150; a `ResizeObserver` left on a detached canvas; two credential fields in clear text and all four mirrored into the `value` attribute; a form never cleared; no double-submit protection and no `AbortController`; whitespace-only and unbounded-length credentials accepted; 21-pixel controls at 0-pixel separation and a 1.14:1 button background; and the absent catch-all route. | (a) Fix the highest-severity subset — the four criticals — since a white screen is worse than a style defect. (b) Fix them inside the harness, where §0.8.1 permits edits. (c) Fix them and record the deviation. (d) Decline them and record nothing beyond this row. | The plan does not merely omit this work, it forbids it by name: §0.8.2 lists "No `TweetCard` component, no `Chart.register` call, no `getTweets` export, no `setupInterceptors` export" and places every other `frontend/src/**` module out of scope; §0.1.4 forbids implementing a missing product feature "even where existing tests assert them" and directs that each divergence be recorded instead; §0.11.3 authorises exactly two production touches and both are in `backend/`; and §0.7.3 states that these ceilings "must be asserted as current behaviour and excluded from gap-closure planning", because chasing them "would drive exactly the scope expansion the requirements prohibit". The QA pass reached the same conclusion independently, listing the same repairs in its own out-of-scope statement and grading AAP compliance FULL. So (a) and (c) are the prohibited expansion, differing only in whether it is admitted; severity does not change who owns the file. (b) is worse than either: a fix in the harness would repair the *test double* and leave the product defective while making every spec observe a screen the product cannot serve. (d) discards the one deliverable that is in scope — §0.1.4's record of each divergence, and Rule 3's requirement that discovered improvements reach the suggested-next-tasks list. | Forty-six known defects ship, four of them able to blank the whole page, and this log is the only place that says so beside the code. Mitigated three ways: §23 now names each one against the file and lines that produce it; `README.md`'s list is written to be schedulable by someone who never reads this log; and the credential-handling subset is additionally written up in `SECURITY-GAPS.md`, where the exposure rather than the UX is the subject. The severity ordering is not preserved anywhere in those lists, so a reader who wants to triage must read the QA pass's own report for that. |
| D401 | **Extend `SECURITY-GAPS.md` with three appended rows — 26, 27 and 28 — rather than absorbing the new measurements into row 9, and restate the two places that publish the register's size.** Row 26 is the DOM-level credential exposure: all four values mirrored into the `value` attribute from first render and never cleared. Row 27 is the unguarded, uncancellable, un-de-duplicated write. Row 28 is the absent normalisation and length bound. The register's own preamble now states that ids are append-only, `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` row F100 states 28 rather than 25, and `README.md`'s security bullet states twenty-six in the three code-and-infrastructure categories out of twenty-eight in total, naming rows 24 and 25 as the deck pair rather than calling them the last two. | (a) Extend row 9, which already covers the credential screen's masking and pending-state gaps, and change no count. (b) Insert the three rows next to row 9 and renumber everything after them. (c) Open a sixth group for runtime-measured credential handling, keeping ids monotonic in document order. (d) Leave them in §23 only, since §23 already names each one. | Row 9's subject is which *fields* are masked and whether the control has pending state; the new subjects are different — the DOM holds every value in clear text whatever the field type says, the write cannot be revoked or de-duplicated, and a blank credential is accepted as a success. (a) would produce one row asserting five unrelated things, and a reader triaging by exposure could not schedule any of them separately. (b) is the one option that is actually harmful: `SECURITY-GAPS.md` rows are cited by number from this log, from `README.md` and from within the register itself — "rows 2, 10", "Row 17 above", "row 14" — so renumbering silently retargets every one of those citations. (c) keeps the numbering tidy at the cost of splitting one screen's credential handling across two groups that share an AAP clause, and the grouping is by reason for decline rather than by discovery date. (d) loses the distinction the register exists to draw: §23 is organised by discovery and mixes typos with exposures, which is exactly what D324 rejected. | The group's ids no longer run in document order, so a reader scanning for "row 26" finds it after row 13 rather than after row 25; the preamble says so, and the alternative was retargeting live citations. Two published totals now have to move together with any further row, which is the intended cost — a register whose stated size disagrees with its own table is the defect this pairing catches. |
| D402 | **Add no §D row to `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` for the runtime findings, and say in §D why they are not there.** §D's subject is a difference between what the frontend emits or expects and what the backend does, and every one of the forty-six is a property of the frontend alone — an absent boundary, an absent state, a strict equality, a target size — or a harness-stub defect this checkpoint fixed. The three that touch a contract were already rows: the undeclared `page` parameter, the one-argument `fetchTweets`, and `twitterService`'s own `{id, text}` interface against the `{tweet_id, content, …}` the backend and the schema use. | (a) Add a row per finding, so §D becomes the single index of everything divergent. (b) Add one summary row covering the set. (c) Add rows only for the findings that touch a request or a response. (d) Add nothing and explain nothing. | §D's rows carry an obligation column naming the suite that must assert the divergence, and that obligation is what makes the section useful rather than descriptive. A row whose obligation is "no suite may assert this, because the repair is out of scope" is a §G ceiling, and §G is where the three new ones went (G13, G14). (a) would double every ceiling into two sections with different meanings and no test to keep them agreeing. (b) is a row that names nothing precisely enough to act on. (c) sounds principled but selects on the wrong axis: the strict-equality scroll predicate and the `undefined` React key touch no request, and the cache-defeated poll touches only headers nobody sets — all three are already stated where they belong. (d) leaves the next reader to re-derive the boundary, and §D is exactly where someone looking for these findings would search first. | §D stays at sixteen rows while §23 grows, so the two sections' sizes no longer hint at each other; §D's preamble now states the split explicitly. If a later change makes one of these findings observable at the boundary — a fix to `frontend/src/services/api.ts`'s base URL would make several requests routable — the row it needs does not exist yet and has to be written then. |


## §45 Integration-final QA remediation — one hermeticity defect, one evidence-chain gap, one runner default and one double's fidelity

Scope: the four findings the integration-final QA run raised against the delivered suite. None is a defect in
production code, and no production file is touched by any of them: one is a backend test whose verdict depended
on a partial artifact produced by another layer, one is a protection that had been applied to one result stream
of three, one is a runner default that printed a misleading teardown warning, and one is a test double diverging
from the backend it is explicitly modelled on. The fifth row restates the published suite-size figures, because
closing the first two added cases.

| Row | Decision | Alternatives | Why | Risks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D403 | **Make the backend suite hermetic against a partial end-to-end result stream, on both sides of the artifact and in CI.** Three changes. (i) `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py::test_the_published_e2e_census_is_the_retained_streams_own_count` now reads `e2e/reports/list-tests.txt` as an independent witness of how many cases the layer has, and binds the published census to the retained stream only when the two agree; when the listing is absent, or declares a different total, the case withdraws itself with a named reason instead of failing. (ii) `e2e/package.json` gains `test:spec` (`playwright test --reporter=line`) and pins the same override on `test:headed` and `test:debug`, so only the unqualified `test` script writes the canonical stream; `e2e/README.md`'s targeted-run table documents `test:spec` in place of `npm test -- <path>`, and a new parametrised case holds all three scripts to the override and to being documented. (iii) the workflow's e2e verify step compares declared-against-discovered and errors by name, as the backend verify step has since `D355`. | (a) Consumer side only: delete the assertion, or skip whenever the artifact exists. (b) Writer side only: pin the reporter and leave the assertion asserting. (c) Detect a filtered run inside `playwright.config.ts` by inspecting `process.argv` and redirect the JUnit `outputFile`. (d) Commit the e2e stream so it is never absent or stale. | The finding is a **verdict flip**: a documented single-spec run left `tests="3"` where the suite's 31 belong, and the backend suite then exited 1 on `assert 3 == 31` — a document defect that no document had. Deleting the assertion (a) gives up the only binding between the published figure and a real run, which is the property the case exists for. The writer side alone (b) cannot help: the finding's own reproduction used a bare runner invocation, which no `package.json` script governs. (c) covers even that, but it decides "is this run partial?" from a heuristic over command-line arguments inside a config file, and a wrong guess silently discards a real run's evidence; no test in this tree can execute that config, so the heuristic would be unguarded. The witness comparison is neither heuristic nor unguarded — it compares two artifacts, and the comparison is the one `D355` already established for the backend stream. (d) would put a machine-specific run result under version control. | The case now **skips** in a tree that has an e2e result stream but no discovery listing, which is the state a developer who has only ever run `npm test` is in; the skip names the command that supplies the witness. A tree with both artifacts still asserts, so the binding is not vacuous — verified by running it both ways. `test:headed` and `test:debug` cannot be executed non-interactively, so their override rests on the mechanism `test:spec` proves rather than on its own run. |
| D404 | **Extend the partial-stream refusal from the backend stream to all three, each against its own witness.** `docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py` applies the existing `partial_stream_reason` to the frontend stream against the test identities `test:load` registered, and to the end-to-end stream against the case count `test:list` discovered, withdrawing each exactly as the backend's is withdrawn and exposing `frontend_partial_stream` / `e2e_partial_stream`; §6.4's two corresponding readiness rows state the reason and read FAIL rather than degrading to "not produced". The function gains `witness` and `remedy` parameters whose defaults reproduce the backend message byte-for-byte. | (a) Leave it as `D355` delivered it: the backend stream refused, the other two trusted. (b) Reuse the backend wording for all three, so the frontend message reads "while collection found 372". (c) Add a second, differently-shaped check per layer. | `D355` closed this defect class by name — the old guards "admitted any non-zero count" — but closed it for one stream of three, and a partial frontend or e2e stream was still published: `K4` read `6 / 6 / 0 / 0` and `K6` read `3 / 3 / 0 / 0` while `--require-all` exited 0. Every runner here declares its reporter in configuration, so one Jest file and one Playwright spec overwrite their layer's stream exactly as a filtered pytest run overwrites the backend's; the asymmetry was an oversight rather than a judgement. (b) would put a pytest word ("collection") on a Jest and a Playwright artifact and name the wrong remedy; two parameters with backend-preserving defaults cost less than the confusion. (c) would add a mechanism where one already fits. | The frontend witness is the readiness probe's identity count, so a tree whose `load-tests.txt` predates a test being added reads the new stream as partial and refuses it. That is the intended direction — it names both figures and the command to re-run — but it does mean the readiness probe has to be re-run after adding a test, which the artifact order in `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §1 already requires. |
| D405 | **[The cap half is superseded by D366 — `maxWorkers` is `1`, not a ceiling expression, because a cap of four was measured again on a loaded host and still left the advisory on two runs in three; the pool is removed rather than bounded, which subsumes any ceiling. Everything below about there being no leak to find, and about surplus workers being overhead rather than throughput, stands and is why the in-band value costs nothing here.] Cap Jest's worker count rather than fixing it.** `frontend/jest.config.js` declares `maxWorkers` as `Math.max(1, Math.min(4, os.cpus().length - 1))`. | (a) A bare `maxWorkers: 4`. (b) `maxWorkers: '50%'`. (c) `--runInBand`. (d) Leave the default and treat the warning as noise. (e) Chase a real leak with `--detectOpenHandles`. | The delivered command printed "a worker process has failed to exit gracefully… tests leaking due to improper teardown" on an otherwise green run, which sends a reader after a leak that does not exist: `--detectOpenHandles --runInBand` reports no open handle, and the warning does not reproduce below default parallelism. There are 24 suites, and Jest's default of one worker per core less one asks a 64-core host for roughly 63 of them, each booting a jsdom environment and an msw `setupServer`. (a) reads simpler but **raises** parallelism on a 2-core runner from 1 worker to 4, which is the opposite of a cap; the expression takes the lower of Jest's own choice and the ceiling, so a 2-core runner still resolves to 1 and a 4-core runner to 3. (b) still yields 32 workers here. (c) serialises CI for a defect that only appears at high worker counts. (d) leaves the misdirection in the delivered command. (e) was done first, and is what established there is nothing to fix in the suite. | A host with more than five cores now runs fewer workers than it could, so a much larger suite would eventually want a higher ceiling. Measured on this 64-core host the cap made the run faster rather than slower — 65 s with the warning became 22–27 s without it across three runs, then 20 s on a fourth — because the surplus workers were overhead rather than throughput. |
| D406 | **[The spelling half of `D12` is superseded by this row.] Serve the timestamp in the exact form the backend emits, and pin it.** `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.ts` gains `serializeTimestamp()`, which drops the millisecond field and the `Z`, plus the exported literal `SERIALIZED_FIXED_TWEET_TIMESTAMP`; `serializeTweet` becomes exported and every success handler answers with its output rather than with a fixture, because the value on the wire was never `serializeTweet`'s — it was `JSON.stringify` calling `Date.prototype.toJSON` on the fixture's own `Date`. One case in `handlers.test.ts` pins the served values, the shape, the unchanged instant and the single producer. `e2e/fixtures/tweets.json` loses the `Z` its two timestamps carried, so the Playwright double agrees with the msw double and with the backend. | (a) Keep the UTC-marked instant and record why the divergence is safe. (b) Change `serializeTweet` only, which is where the finding located the defect. (c) Emit a value `tweetSchema` accepts. | `handlers.ts` is modelled on backend behaviour throughout — `BACKEND_SERVER_ERROR_BODY`, `BACKEND_NOT_FOUND_BODY`, `BACKEND_UNPROCESSABLE_STATUS` and `BACKEND_TWEETS_INT_PARAMETERS` are all named for it — and `app/schema/tweet.py` types `timestamp` as a naive `datetime`, which pydantic v1 serialises through `isoformat()`: no offset, no milliseconds, pinned server-side as `2024-01-01T00:00:00`. A double that emits a UTC-marked instant tests a future consumer against a shape production never sends, which is how a fidelity gap becomes a real bug later; (a) documents the gap instead of closing it, for no saving. (b) was tried first and is what exposed the real producer — it made 18 assertions disagree with the wire while the wire itself did not move, which is the strongest available argument that fixing only the model would have been a fix in name. (c) is `D12`'s own rejected alternative and would hide that `tweetSchema.timestamp` is `z.date()`. | Any future suite asserting a raw timestamp string must use the exported literal rather than the factories' `FIXED_TWEET_TIMESTAMP`, which remains the `Date`'s own ISO form; the two now differ by design, and the pin fails by name if the transformation drifts. `tweetSchema` rejects both spellings equally, so no schema assertion changes. |
| D408 | **Read the machine-produced witnesses by byte-order mark, not as UTF-8.** `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` gains `_read_artifact()` — the extractor's `decode_by_bom` sniff, in the one test module that reads a retained readiness artifact — and the E2E discovery listing and result stream are read through it. Three parametrised cases exercise the real helper against the encodings the two documented captures produce (`utf-8`, `utf-8-sig`, `utf-16`). `_read()` is left alone for repository documents. | (a) Read every artifact with `_read()` and accept the crash. (b) Change `_read()` itself to sniff. (c) Re-document the Windows capture as `Out-File -Encoding utf8` so the artifact is UTF-8 in the first place. (d) Catch `UnicodeDecodeError` in the census helper and withdraw. | Found by running the sweep's own documented PowerShell commands rather than the bash ones: `Tee-Object` writes UTF-16LE with a mark, and D403's new witness read raised `UnicodeDecodeError: byte 0xff in position 0`, taking the whole backend suite red on a documented path — the same verdict-flip class D403 had just closed, reintroduced by its own fix. A UTF-8 reader cannot take that file either way: strictly it raises, and leniently it yields NUL-separated characters the pattern misses, which would withdraw the census case on every Windows run and leave the binding vacuous without saying so — strictly worse than the crash. (b) would apply a byte-level sniff to committed documents that are UTF-8 by definition and are also line-ending normalised, widening a fix beyond its defect. (c) rewrites a documented operator command to suit a test, and cannot reach an artifact a reader produced yesterday. (d) turns a readable witness into an absent one. The extractor already solved exactly this, with a docstring naming this exact hazard; mirroring it keeps one idiom rather than inventing a second. | The sniff and `_read()` can drift apart, since only one of them normalises line endings — both return `\n`-normalised text today, and the three encoding cases pin the sniff. UTF-32 marks are handled because the extractor's list handles them, not because a capture here produces one. |
| D407 | **Re-measure and restate every published suite-size figure, for the third time, because closing D403, D404 and D408 added cases.** Backend `1082` collected / `1079` passed / `3` skipped at an unchanged `93.33%` (182/195); frontend `24` suites, `372` cases, `348` passed / `24` skipped; end to end `31` in `5` files. The deck's headline KPI becomes `1,458`, the sum of the three streams. Restated in `README.md`, `backend/tests/README.md`, `frontend/TESTING.md`, `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`, `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` and the deck, with the per-suite split closing as 492 + 3 + 150 + 72 + 62 + 168 + 39 + 96 = 1082. | (a) Leave the figures and let `test_docs_contract.py` fail. (b) Add no cases, so no figure moves. (c) Publish a range rather than a number. | `D353` and `D365` established both the procedure and the reason: a figure a document publishes is a claim, and a stale claim is a defect whether or not a test notices. Here a test does notice — the document contract requires every publishing document to state one collected figure, one passing figure, a difference of exactly the three reasoned skips, and a deck KPI equal to the three streams summed. (b) would mean closing two findings without a guard against their return, which is the pattern `D355` exists to prevent. (c) makes the claim unfalsifiable. | Every figure is a reading from a retained artifact on one host, and durations differ per run — which is why `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7 states the counts as properties of the suite and the durations only as what that run took. `D354` still governs: figures inside earlier decision rows and dated evidence rows are history and stay as they stand. |

## §46 Security-QA remediation — the harness's two closable findings, the install path, and the register that understated what was measured

Scope: the fourteen findings a dedicated runtime security assessment raised against the delivered tree. Eleven
of them can only be repaired in production code, in a pinned dependency version, or in an infrastructure file
that AAP §0.8.2 excludes by name, and they are declined here exactly as this log's earlier security rows are —
each one written up in `SECURITY-GAPS.md` as a fact about the code with the clause that puts it out of reach.
Three are inside the authorized write surface and are fixed: two in the end-to-end harness, one in the
workflow's install steps. The last two rows are the documentation half — the register understated three
exposures, named a package this delivery does not carry, and pointed one cross-reference at the wrong row.

No test case was added or removed by any row in this section. Every new assertion lives inside a test that
