already existed, so the published census stays at 31 end-to-end cases in 5 spec files, 372 frontend cases and
1096 backend cases — the D399 rule, applied for the same reason. [Superseded by §47/D419 for the
census figures only: 31 end-to-end cases in 5 spec files, 372 frontend cases and 1096 backend cases were the
measured totals at this checkpoint. The full-stack seam remediation recorded in §47 then added cases, and the
delivered tree measures 1251 backend and 383 frontend cases against the same 31 end-to-end cases in 5 spec
files. This section’s own claim is unaffected — none of that movement came from these rows.]

| Row | Decision | Alternatives | Why | Risks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D409 | **Redact the submitted credentials out of the harness config stub's error excerpt in one left-to-right pass, and withhold the body's text altogether when redaction would leave nothing readable.** `e2e/harness/stubs/configService.ts` gains `redactSubmittedValues`, which de-duplicates the submitted values, sorts them longest-first and scans the body once, copying each replacement to the output and never re-reading it; `excerptResponseBody` then withholds the text and states its shape — `(body withheld: N occurrence(s) of the submitted values in its M characters left no readable excerpt after redaction)` — when the redaction grew the body past `MAX_REDACTED_GROWTH_FACTOR`. Redaction still runs before truncation, which is D394's property. `e2e/tests/configuration.spec.ts` gains two absence assertions inside its existing rejection test: no placeholder may be followed by leftover credential text, and this echo must not push the excerpt past its cap. | (a) Truncate the body before redacting, which is what the QA finding suggested first. (b) Replace with a token that cannot contain the needle, such as `\u0000REDACTED\u0000`. (c) Keep the per-value `split`/`join` and cap the *number* of values redacted. (d) Leave it: the direction of failure was safe, since it never under-redacted. | The shipped loop replaced one value at a time over the output of the last, and `[redacted credential]` contains the characters a short credential is made of, so a one- or two-character value re-redacted its own replacement once per remaining value. Measured in a real browser on the exact repro: a 116-character body became **34,116 characters** internally — 294× — and what reached the console was 331 characters of nested placeholders in which none of the responder's own words survived, destroying the diagnostic the excerpt exists to produce. (a) is not available: redaction runs before truncation precisely so that no credential survives as the tail of a cut-off excerpt, and truncating first would leave a *fragment* of one there. (b) cannot work for an arbitrary one-character value — any token can contain any single character — and it also does not stop the growth, only the nesting. (c) changes an unbounded growth into a bounded one while still emitting the unreadable soup. (d) leaves a stub whose failure path silently emits tens of kilobytes into a Playwright trace. The single pass additionally closed a defect the finding did not name and the suite could not see: `Object.values()` order replaced `test-access-token` before `test-access-token-secret`, leaving `[redacted credential]-secret` — a credential suffix — on the console, which the existing whole-value assertion passed over. | The withheld-body branch reports a count and a length rather than the responder's reason, so a genuinely useful reason is lost in the one case where a submitted value is short enough to match all over the body. Accepted: that case is a spec typing a degenerate value, and the alternative is text that carries nothing anyway. The growth factor is a judgement — two — and a body that legitimately doubles under redaction would be withheld; measured against the values `configuration.spec.ts` types, the excerpt grows by 9% and is kept whole. |
| D410 | **Refines D126. Key the harness's fail-closed refusal by path rather than by method/path pair.** `e2e/vite.harness.config.ts` derives `HARNESS_API_METHODS_BY_PATH` from `HARNESS_API_SURFACE`'s own keys and refuses every declared path with `503 harness-api-not-intercepted` whatever method the request carries; the remedy is still the exact `page.route` snippet for a declared pair, and `undeclaredMethodRemedy` otherwise, which names the methods the path does declare and both ways forward. `isClientRoutePath` becomes path-scoped for the same reason. `e2e/tests/isolation.spec.ts` gains a third step inside its existing per-shape test, driven through `request.fetch` so the middleware's own answer is what is measured. | (a) Document the asymmetry instead, which is what D126's Consequences column did — it called the method keying deliberate. (b) Add the missing method/path pairs to `HARNESS_API_SURFACE` as explicit entries. (c) Refuse by *prefix*, so `/undefined/tweets/7` is refused as well. (d) Make the Accept normaliser skip any path under `/api/`. | The asymmetry was not a documented edge, it was a fail-open. `GET /api/config/twitter` — the credential-write endpoint — answered **200 `text/html`** with the harness document, and `HEAD` on all three declared paths did the same; a `fetch` reports both as `response.ok`, and the config stub then parses an HTML document as JSON. The browser-side ledger in `harness-fixtures.ts` has always been keyed by path, so the two layers disagreed: it recorded such a request as un-intercepted while the server had already answered it successfully. (a) preserves that. (b) needs a remedy per method for methods no component issues, and grows with every method anyone probes. (c) would refuse `/undefined/tweets/7`, which `services/api.ts` genuinely issues and which no mounted component reaches — the harness would then be refusing a path it has never declared, and the honest answer for an undeclared path is the one it already gives. (d) fixes one prefix and leaves `/undefined/tweets`. | A `HEAD` or an `OPTIONS` to a declared path is now a 503 rather than a 200 or a 404, so a future spec that probes one has to expect it. The response body shape is unchanged, so `harness-fixtures.ts`, `e2e/README.md`'s documented example and every existing assertion still hold. Measured before and after across a 40-cell method × path matrix: the three declared paths moved to 503 under all four methods, and the four client routes, `/favicon.ico`, the editor endpoint and a traversal answered exactly as they did before. |
| D411 | **Harden the three install steps of `ci.yml` that this delivery owns, and leave the two integrity measures the AAP's own clauses exclude.** All three `npm install` invocations gain `--ignore-scripts`; the pip bootstrap becomes `pip==26.0.1`; both pip invocations gain `--only-binary=:all:`. `SECURITY-GAPS.md` row 32 states what that closes and what it does not. | (a) Commit both lockfiles and switch to `npm ci --ignore-scripts`, which is the stronger form and what the finding asked for. (b) Add `--require-hashes` and generate a hash-pinned requirements file. (c) Add `cache: npm` to `setup-node` to stop re-resolving from the registry on every run. (d) Pin the 17 floating ranges in `frontend/package.json`. (e) Do none of it: the exposure is an install-time one and the pins are exact for every dependency this delivery added. | Of the 112 lifecycle hooks in the two Node trees, three run for a registry-tarball consumer — `esbuild@0.18.20`'s postinstall in both trees and `msw@1.3.5`'s — and each is arbitrary code executing in CI on the strength of a floating transitive resolution. Neither is needed, which was measured rather than assumed: both trees were deleted and reinstalled with `--ignore-scripts`, esbuild's JS API and CLI both worked, `msw/node` imported, and the frontend suite (348 passed / 24 skipped) and the end-to-end suite (31 passed) both ran green on the result. On the Python side an sdist is *built* rather than unpacked, so `--only-binary=:all:` is the same control for the same class of exposure; every one of the 76 distributions in the closure publishes a wheel for cp39 on manylinux and for `any`, so it changes nothing about what gets installed. Pinning pip closes the last unpinned fetch in the step that installs everything else, and 26.0.1 is the newest release declaring `requires_python >=3.9`, so nothing available is given up. (a) and (c) are unavailable together: `npm ci` refuses to run without a committed lockfile and `setup-node`'s cache keys on one, and §0.6.2 declines committing one (§0.10.5 C7). (b) needs a digest for every *transitive* distribution — a lockfile by another name — while §0.6.1 fixes this manifest as exact direct pins; a digest set generated on this Windows host could not be verified against the ubuntu-latest resolve either, and a `--require-hashes` file with one distribution missing fails the whole install. (d) is excluded by §0.5.3, which permits devDependencies and test scripts and states that nothing else in that file changes. (e) leaves three postinstalls and an unpinned installer in a period of documented npm worm activity. | The workflow now depends on `pip==26.0.1` remaining installable and on the closure remaining wheel-only; either changing is a one-line edit, and both are stated in the step's own comment. `--ignore-scripts` is a per-command flag rather than a repository-wide `.npmrc` setting, so a fourth install step added later would not inherit it — the comment on each of the three says so. Nothing about reproducibility improved: the resolution still floats, which is why row 32 exists rather than the finding being closed. |
| D412 | **Restate the six register rows that understated what a runtime assessment measured, correct the one that named a package this delivery does not carry, and append five rows for the exposures it had not stated at all.** Rows 2, 8, 10, 11 and 20 gain the measurement that changes their impact; row 14 drops `python-multipart` and gains `starlette==0.27.0`'s five advisories (re-derived from OSV as **seven** by D421, which corrects the count on the row itself); row 11's cross-reference moves from "row 12" to row 14. Rows 29 to 31 are appended to group 1 — the `BIGQUERY_DATASET` identifier break-out, the quadratic-backtracking freeze in `formatUtils`, and the hostile keys the store retains — row 32 to group 2, and row 33 opens a sixth group. `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` F100 and `README.md`'s security bullet move with them. | (a) Leave the rows as they stand: each already names its exposure, and the new measurements only sharpen it. (b) Add new rows for the sharpened cases and leave the old wording in place. (c) Renumber so ids run in document order. (d) Record the five new exposures in §23 rather than in the register. | Three of the restatements change what a reader would schedule, not merely how it reads. Row 8 said "path traversal within the API surface"; two of five probed ids left the prefix entirely and addressed the origin root. Row 10 said "the handler raises before it reads anything today"; with a SQLAlchemy-shaped `get_db` override the handler completed and answered 200 with `offset(-1)` and a twenty-digit limit forwarded. Row 11 described host reflection; the scheme is attacker-controlled too, through uvicorn's default proxy-header trust, and the intended reverse-proxy deployment widens it. A register that understates its own worst case is worse than one row short — a reader deprioritises on it. Row 14's `python-multipart` was the sharper defect: the package is neither pinned nor installed, so the row defeated the check it invited, exactly as D359 found the same row doing with a marker it did not carry. (b) would leave two statements of the same exposure disagreeing, and D401's lesson is that a reader cites by number. (c) silently retargets every citation from this log, from the README and from inside the register itself, which D401 rejected for the same reason. (d) is what D324 refused: §23 is organised by discovery and mixes typos with exposures. | The register's ids now run 1–13, 26–31 in group 1 and 14–17, 32 in group 2, so document order and id order differ in two places; the preamble says so. Two published totals — F100 and `README.md`'s bullet — move with every future row, which is the cost D401 accepted deliberately. The three new application rows are all latent today (no route reaches the analytics service, and both utility modules are dead code), and a reader who takes "latent" as "not urgent" would be wrong about row 29 in particular, whose trigger is a configuration value rather than a request. |
| D413 | **Accept the harness dev server's error-overlay source disclosure with a stated boundary, rather than removing the overlay.** `SECURITY-GAPS.md` row 33 records it: a failing transform is answered `500` with Vite's own payload, which carries the file's verbatim source as `pluginCode` and no `Content-Type`. | (a) Strip or rewrite the payload in a middleware ahead of Vite's error handler. (b) Run the harness with the overlay disabled. (c) Serve a preview build for the end-to-end run instead of a dev server. (d) Say nothing: the source is already in the repository and the server is loopback-only. | The payload is Vite's error protocol, not harness code, and it is the diagnostic every harness change is debugged with — the four extension-less components and the three stubs are served through a virtual-module plugin whose failures surface *only* there. (a) means parsing and rewriting an internal payload shape that no Vite version promises, in the one code path that reports why the harness is broken. (b) trades the whole diagnostic for a disclosure already bounded to loopback. (c) is a larger change than the finding warrants and contradicts D378's SPA fallback and the harness's whole design, which exists because this frontend cannot be built at all. (d) leaves an accepted risk unrecorded, which is what the register exists to prevent. The boundary is measured rather than asserted: loopback bind, a host allow-list that refuses `evil.example` and `127.0.0.1.nip.io` with 403, and a filesystem guard that refuses every out-of-graph path and every unregistered `/@id/`. | If the harness is ever exposed beyond loopback — a shared runner, a tunnelled port, a published container port — this becomes a real source-disclosure endpoint, and nothing in the configuration would stop it. Row 33 states that condition as the thing a human must not do. |


## §47 Full-stack seam QA remediation — the runtime behaviours the suite had not yet asserted

Scope: the twenty-nine findings a full-stack seam QA pass raised by driving the real FastAPI application over a
real socket and the real components in real Chrome. **Not one of them is fixed, and not one production file is
touched**, because every one lands on a module §0.8.2 of the frozen plan excludes or on behaviour §0.1.4
forbids changing — the QA report says the same of each in its own words. What was missing was not a fix but a
**record**: six of the findings name behaviour no test asserted and no register stated, and several more were
asserted only in a shape that a reader could mistake for a fixture's property rather than the server's. The
rows below therefore add assertions and register lines, and restate the published figures because the
assertions moved them. Every behaviour is pinned by name in §23 and in `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md`.

| Row | Decision | Alternatives | Why | Risks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D414 | **Assert the empty-identifier collection disclosure at the routing layer, and its client half at the URL it builds.** `tests/integration/test_route_surface.py` gains eight cases: the `307` toward the collection for both `/tweets/` and `/tweets//`, the followed response being **byte-identical** to `GET /tweets`, the absence of any authentication challenge or session on it and a bogus bearer changing nothing, the `%20`/`+` control that reaches the handler and `500`s instead, and the OpenAPI parameter schema being exactly `{"title": "Tweet Id", "type": "string"}`. `frontend/src/services/api.test.ts` gains the two client URLs, `undefined/tweets/` and `undefined/tweets/ `. | (a) Fix it: `min_length=1` on the path parameter, or `redirect_slashes=False`. (b) Assert only the `307`, which the existing trailing-slash case nearly did. (c) Assert only the client URL and leave the server behaviour to prose. | (a) is the one option the plan forecloses twice over: touch #1 is bounded to "each router file containing only what the current single routes file already implements" (§0.11.3) and §0.1.4 forbids modifying existing behaviour, so either change would be an unauthorized production edit to the code this programme exists to test. (b) leaves the finding's substance — that a caller asking for one record receives every record, with `200`, unauthenticated — unstated; the existing case drove `POST` and landed on `405`, which is a different fact entirely. (c) cannot show the disclosure at all, because the collapse happens in the router. Comparing **bytes** rather than parsed records is deliberate: "the same records" and "the same response" are different claims, and only the second rules out per-route shaping. The `%20` control is what makes the mechanism attributable to an *empty segment* rather than to a blank identifier. | A future `min_length` or `redirect_slashes=False` fix will fail four of these cases by design; that is the point, and §23 names them so the blast radius is known first. The whitespace leg is a browser normalisation rather than a server one, so the suite asserts the client URL and the register states the browser half — a reader who expects a server-side trim will not find one asserted. |
| D415 | **Assert the response model's blast radius, its silent value adjustments and its field-name stripping through the emitted JSON.** `tests/integration/test_http_tweets.py` gains twenty-one cases and a `_program_sliced_collection` helper whose pagination genuinely selects: the mixed-validity result set failing as a whole with the `ValidationError` located at the bad row's index, the caller's bare `500` carrying neither valid row, the five-slice localisation matrix, `"777"`→`777` / `12.9`→**`12`** / `True`→`1`, `doubt_rating` `42.5` and `-1.0`, the dropped undeclared key, the `id`/`text` sentinel sweep, both timestamp renderings, unescaped markup with a `&lt;` count of zero, the `javascript:` URL, and `limit` at three unbounded magnitudes. | (a) Assert the coercions against the pydantic model directly, in `tests/unit/test_schema.py`, where three of them already are. (b) Assert the mixed-row failure only through the exception, as the single-row case does. (c) Reuse `_program_collection_rows`, which answers one fixed result whatever the pagination. | The emitted JSON is what a client reads, and the finding is about what reaches a client: (a) proves the model coerces but not that the coerced value is what crosses the wire, and it cannot show a key being dropped from a *response body*. (b) states the cause and omits the consequence — that both valid rows become unreachable and the response says nothing about why — which is the whole of the availability finding. (c) makes the slice matrix vacuous, since every slice would answer the same rows; a helper whose pagination selects is the only way the matrix means anything. | `_program_sliced_collection` is a second, subtly different double in one module, and a reader who reaches for the wrong one gets a passing test that proves less than it appears to. Its docstring states the distinction. The truncation case pins `12` — if pydantic ever rounds instead, the case fails, which is the intended alarm. |
| D416 | **Assert the poll's no-backoff property structurally in Jest, and the unhandled-rejection leak in the browser.** `frontend/src/components/Dashboard.test.tsx` gains two cases — four consecutive failures changing neither the single 30000 ms registration nor the exact fetch count nor producing a `clearInterval`, and the interval being armed before the first fetch settles so a mount failure cannot prevent scheduling. The leak itself is measured in real Chrome and recorded in §23, `SECURITY-GAPS.md` row 38 and `frontend/TESTING.md`. | (a) Assert the leak in Jest with a `process.on('unhandledRejection')` counter. (b) Same, but switch that one case to real timers so Node's detection runs deterministically. (c) Assert nothing about the leak anywhere and rely on the existing "propagates the rejection unchanged" case. | (a) and (b) were both **built and measured, and both failed**. jest-circus installs its own `unhandledRejection` listener and attributes any real leak to the running test, so the very behaviour under test is reported as a test failure — a suite that asserts a leak by leaking cannot stay green. And leaving fake timers while the `setInterval`/`clearInterval` spies wrap sinon's clock leaves `globalThis.setInterval` undefined, which crashed the case with `ReferenceError: setInterval is not defined`. Adopting each reported promise inside the listener does not help, because Jest's listener fires regardless of ours. (c) leaves the operational half — forever, with no backoff and nothing on screen — unstated. The browser is where an unhandled rejection is *observable without being fatal*, and the measurement there is unambiguous: 26 failed requests, 26 rejections, `rejectionhandled` 0. | The leak is asserted by evidence rather than by a test, so a future `.catch()` will not turn a test red; §23 says so explicitly, and the structural cases would still fail if the schedule or the call sites changed. The browser figure is a point-in-time measurement rather than a gate. |
| D417 | **Assert the absent security-header set and the unreachable CORS preflight per status, not once.** `tests/integration/test_app_lifecycle.py` gains twenty-three cases: the exact emitted header set on a `200` and a `404`, nine standard browser-facing controls asserted absent on a success **and** again on a `404`/`405`/`422`, the JSON content type standing alone as the only control, the refused preflight still advertising all seven methods and a ten-minute cache, `OPTIONS` without an `Origin` falling through to `405 allow: GET`, an origin-bearing request to the data-bearing route getting credentials without an origin, and the schema declaring no security scheme with a bogus bearer answered byte-identically. | (a) One case asserting the header set on one successful response. (b) Assert the headers a hardening middleware would add, as a single set-difference. (c) Extend the two existing CORS cases in place rather than adding to them. | A reader auditing one successful response cannot tell whether an error path hardens where the happy path does not — and error responses are exactly where a `nosniff` or a `Cache-Control` matters most — so (a) understates what was measured. (b) fails as one opaque assertion naming a list, where nine parametrised cases each name the header that appeared. (c) would conflate two different facts: the existing cases establish that CORS refuses an arbitrary origin, while these establish that **no** origin can succeed and that the refusal is itself a disclosure. Header **absence** is asserted through the in-process ASGI transport deliberately: `date`, `server` and the connection headers are the *server's*, and the application is the subject. | The emitted-set assertion is exact, so a future header — including one a dependency adds — fails it. That is intended, but it means an upgrade can turn this red without anyone having edited `app/main.py`. The `ALLOWED_ORIGINS = []` default these cases pin is the least-privilege value the plan itself specifies for touch #2, so a deployment that populates it will fail three of them by design. |
| D418 | **Assert the timestamp mismatch against the record the backend genuinely emits, as a literal, and assert the local-time hazard as a relationship.** `frontend/src/schema/tweetSchema.test.ts` gains a `BACKEND_RECORD` literal transcribed from the body the backend integration suite asserts, and five cases: exactly one issue and it is `timestamp`; the key sets agreeing exactly, so the mismatch is one of type alone; coercion of that single field succeeding with all ten keys unchanged; the zone-free string coercing as local time, asserted as `coerced - asUtc === getTimezoneOffset() * 60000`; and the offset-bearing form behaving identically. | (a) Keep only the existing case, which rejects a `Z`-suffixed ISO string. (b) Build the record from `makeTweet()` and override `timestamp`. (c) Assert a fixed instant for the coerced value. | (a) proves the field rejects *a* string, not that it rejects **the server's** string — and the server's carries no designator, which is a second defect the `Z`-suffixed fixture hides. (b) would make the record agree with the fixture rather than with the server, which is the opposite of the property wanted; a literal is the point. (c) would pass only in the zone the author ran in; a relationship holds in every zone, and this suite runs on developer machines as well as in CI. | The literal is a transcription, so it can drift from the server silently. The backend suite asserts the same ten keys and the same two renderings, and both documents name each other, which is the only binding available without a shared fixture across two languages. |
| D419 | **Re-measure and restate every published suite-size figure, for the fourth time, because this checkpoint's assertions moved them.** Backend `1150` collected / `1147` passed / `3` skipped warm and `1145` / `5` on a first pass, integration `204`, the root-misuse split `21 failed, 1126 passed, 3 skipped`, `junit.xml` `tests="1150"` with `1150` of `1150` distinct identities; frontend `359` passed / `24` skipped / `383` total with `tests="383"` and `383` of `383` distinct; e2e `31` unchanged; deck KPI `1,537 = 1147 + 359 + 31`. Restated in `README.md`, `backend/tests/README.md`, `frontend/TESTING.md`, `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`, `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` and the deck. | (a) Leave the figures and note that they precede this checkpoint. (b) Publish a range. (c) Derive every figure at build time. | The same reasoning as D353, D365 and D407, which this row continues: a stale figure is indistinguishable from a wrong one to a reader, and the figures are load-bearing — `test_docs_contract.py` holds every document to the same collected total, the same passing total and a KPI that is their sum, so a partial restatement fails the suite rather than passing quietly. (b) gives up the arithmetic those checks depend on. (c) is the standing answer and remains out of scope: no document in this tree is generated. | Any later change to any layer invalidates all of them again; the guard is that the checks fail loudly, and this row is the fourth demonstration that they do. The `1145`/`5` first-pass figure depends on which artifacts a tree has produced, and both readings are stated wherever either appears. |


## §48 Final-acceptance QA remediation — the register census the seam pass moved, the citations it misdirected, and the advisory count nothing re-derived

Scope: the findings a final-acceptance QA pass raised against **this delivery's own** documentation,
tests and executive deck. **No production file is touched.** The findings that pass raised against the
application itself are declined, every one against a named clause of the frozen plan, and each decline
is a row in this section rather than a note in a review reply — for the reason D324 gives.

Underneath the individual findings is one failure mode with four instances. A figure published in prose
that nothing re-derives goes stale the moment the thing it counts moves, and it then reads exactly like
a figure that is still true. §46 and §47 each grew the security register and each restated some
of the numbers that describe it; neither restated all of them, and nothing went red. The same shape left
a frontend result total at a previous run's value, a manifest pin census one behind the manifest, and
this log's own citation census one behind the tree. The remedy in every row below is therefore the same
shape: correct the figure, then bind it to the artifact it counts, so that the next omission is a red
test rather than a silent contradiction. Two of the findings are not figures — a citation that names
a real but unrelated row, and a stylesheet whose parity with the deck was asserted rather than enforced
— and they get the same treatment for the same reason.

One further instance was not reported by the pass at all. It was found while checking that these
edits diffed as the changes they were: this log's working copy had picked up 51 doubled carriage
returns, and carried one control byte that had eaten a letter, none of it visible to a reader. Between
them they made git treat the file as binary and render one changed line as a 1,575-line rewrite. D431
repairs both and binds the class, because a diff a reviewer cannot read defeats every other record here.

| Row | Decision | Alternatives | Why | Risks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D420 | **Re-derive the security register's published census from the register itself, and repoint every citation the seam pass misdirected.** `README.md`'s security bullet moves from "thirty — thirty-three rows" to **thirty-five — thirty-eight**, and `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` F100 from "33 exposures" to **38**, F100 additionally gaining the narration of rows 34 to 38 that §47 never added. The three cross-references to the polled feed's unhandled-rejection leak — `frontend/TESTING.md` item 22, F63, and D416's own text — move from `./SECURITY-GAPS.md` **row 33 to row 38**. Seven citations that named a decision row five earlier than the one that decided the work are repointed: F50 to D415, F51 and F60 to D414, F52 to D417, F55 and `frontend/TESTING.md` item 24 to D418, F63 to D416, and F66 to `./SECURITY-GAPS.md` row 28 — because §47 opened no row for the Configuration additions at all and §23 pins them to that register row instead. | (a) Correct the figures and leave them as prose readings, which is what they were. (b) Renumber the register so document order and id order agree, which would make "the last row" and "the count" the same fact and remove the arithmetic. (c) Publish the census only in `./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`, which `dashboard-extract.py` generates, and delete it from the two hand-written documents. (d) Point the three leak cross-references at the *group* rather than at a row. (e) Treat the off-by-five citations as cosmetic. | The register grew from 33 rows to 38 in §47 and every figure that counts it was left behind — while §23 of this log *was* restated correctly at the same time, which is what makes the omission demonstrable rather than arguable: two sections of one document disagreed about the same register. The row-33 references are worse than stale. Row 33 exists and describes the harness dev server's error overlay, so a reader following the reference lands on a real row about a different subject with nothing to signal it is the wrong one; a stale count at least looks like a count. The seven off-by-five citations have that property at scale, because D409 to D413 are every one of them a real row, so `(D409)` on the route-surface row reads as deliberate. The cause is mechanical rather than editorial: §47's rows were drafted against a numbering that began five earlier, and the ids moved when §46's rows were appended first — which is the same failure D401 predicted for renumbering, arriving by a different route. (a) is precisely the state that produced the defect, four times. (b) is what D401 refused and D412 refused again, on the ground that a reader cites by number and a renumber silently retargets every citation in the tree. (c) removes the census from the two documents a reader of the register actually opens, and F100 is where the register is described at all. (d) makes the reference unfalsifiable: a group carries no id, and the register's groups are prose headings. (e) leaves a wrong pointer that no reader can detect without opening the log and reading the row. | The register's two published totals now move with every future row, in two documents, which is the cost D401 accepted deliberately; D422's binding turns that from a drift into a red test, at the price of making a register append a four-file change. The seam-pass citations are correct against the log as it stands and are now asserted in both directions, but only for the six rows `SEAM_PASS_AUTHORITIES` names — a future section that drafts its rows before appending them can reintroduce the same class, and nothing detects it for rows outside that table. F66 now cites a register row where every neighbouring row cites a decision row, which is a visible inconsistency; it is the honest one, because the decision it would otherwise cite does not exist. |
| D421 | **Extends D102 and D164.** **Re-derive `starlette==0.27.0`'s advisory count from OSV rather than restating it, correct it from five to seven, and enumerate the whole set on the register row beside the query that reproduces it.** `./SECURITY-GAPS.md` row 14 now names all seven by GHSA and CVE: `GHSA-f96h-pmfr-66vw` / `CVE-2024-47874` and `GHSA-2c2j-9gv5-cj73` / `CVE-2025-54121`, both multipart denial of service; `GHSA-82w8-qh3p-5jfq` / `CVE-2026-54283`, `request.form()` limits silently ignored for url-encoded bodies; `GHSA-x746-7m8f-x49c` / `CVE-2026-48817`, an arbitrary HTTP method dispatched to an `HTTPEndpoint` attribute through `getattr`; `GHSA-wqp7-x3pw-xc5r` / `CVE-2026-48818`, SSRF and NTLM credential theft through UNC paths in `StaticFiles` on Windows; and the `Host`-handling **pair** row 11 is the live primitive for, `GHSA-86qp-5c8j-p5mr` / `CVE-2026-48710` and `GHSA-jp82-jpqv-5vv3` / `CVE-2026-54282`. `backend/tests/README.md` restates both of its starlette paragraphs to match, and names the pair where it had named one advisory. The disposition is unchanged: the pin stays, and none of the seven can be closed without replacing `fastapi==0.95.2`. | (a) Restate the count as seven and leave it at that. (b) Say "several" and stop publishing a number. (c) Add a test that queries OSV and fails when the count moves. (d) Pin the count to a vendored advisory snapshot committed alongside the register. (e) Upgrade starlette, which is what would actually close them. | The count was wrong because nothing re-derived it, so restating it alone — option (a) — reproduces the defect with a fresher number. What makes the row trustworthy is not the figure but the **enumeration plus the query**: a reader can re-run `POST https://api.osv.dev/v1/query` with `{"package": {"name": "starlette", "ecosystem": "PyPI"}, "version": "0.27.0"}` and get fourteen records that deduplicate by CVE alias to those seven, and the row says so. (b) throws away the only thing that made the discrepancy findable in the first place. (c) is the shape used everywhere else in this suite and is wrong here: it makes a green test depend on a third-party service and on network egress, which the guards in `tests/conftest.py` exist to refuse, and it would go red on an advisory published upstream rather than on a defect in this tree. (d) is a snapshot that goes stale exactly as the prose did, with more machinery. (e) is a migration rather than a bump — it requires re-pinning `pydantic`, `tweepy`, `openai` and `httpx` together — and D102's reason for the pin is unchanged. So the row states the set, states how to re-derive it, and states in as many words that a count of a third-party database is a reading on the day it was taken and that an advisory published tomorrow makes it eight. | The number will be wrong again, by design: nothing in this tree keeps it current, and the row now says so rather than implying otherwise. Three of the seven are reachable only through code paths this application does not have — no endpoint accepts a request body, no `HTTPEndpoint` is declared, and `StaticFiles` is never mounted — and a reader who reads seven as seven live exposures will over-schedule; the row distinguishes them, but only in prose. D102's manifest marker still names one advisory, and this row does not add markers to `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, because a marker line is inside the pin census `test_dependency_closure.py` and `test_docs_contract.py` both measure and changing it is a wider change than the finding warrants. |
| D422 | **Bind every published census to the artifact it counts, rather than to the other documents that publish it.** `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` gains six bindings: the frontend result triple (suites, passed, skipped, total) against `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml` and against each document's own arithmetic; the manifest pin census and its test-stack/runtime split against `backend/requirements-dev.txt`'s two sections; this log's three citation censuses against a filesystem walk of the tracked tree; the security register's row count, per-group census and the two rows `README.md` names individually against `./SECURITY-GAPS.md` itself; and `SEAM_PASS_AUTHORITIES`, which asserts in both directions that each §47 matrix row cites the log row that decided it and that the log row names the artifact the matrix row is about; and `REGISTER_CROSS_REFERENCES`, which looks each register row number up by the **subject** its row carries rather than writing the number down, so a document that names a different row fails. Two further bindings assert that every `D` id the eight citing documents cite is the head of a row this log actually carries, and that the register's own ids run 1 to N with nothing repeated and nothing missing. | (a) Keep the existing cross-document agreement checks, which hold every document to the same figure. (b) Generate the prose from the artifacts, as `dashboard-extract.py` does for the dashboard. (c) Assert the figures in a review checklist rather than in a test. (d) Bind the counts but not the citations, since a citation is prose. | (a) is why four stale figures survived: a check that documents agree passes when they agree on a stale value, and every one of these figures was stale *uniformly*. Binding to the artifact is the only form that can fail when the artifact moves and the prose does not. (b) is the standing answer and remains out of scope — no document in this tree is generated — and it would also make the prose unreviewable, which is the opposite of what Rule 1 wants from a decision record. (c) is what was already happening, since every one of these figures had been reviewed at least twice. (d) misreads the defect: the seven off-by-five citations were undetectable precisely *because* they were prose pointing at real ids, so a citation is exactly the kind of claim that needs a machine check. The register bindings deliberately read the register's own group headings rather than a hard-coded row list, so appending a row to a group is enough to move the census the tests demand. Both stale number words are kept in `REGISTER_NUMBER_WORDS` on purpose: a regression to "thirty-three" then fails on its **value** rather than falling out of the pattern and passing unnoticed, which is the failure mode D407 recorded for the collected-total patterns. The cross-reference binding exists because a negative validation demanded it: with the first five in place, reverting F63's `SECURITY-GAPS.md` "row 38" to "row 33" left the whole module green, since row 33 is a real row and only its subject distinguishes it — so the perturbation that was supposed to fail was written first, then the binding that fails it. Seven perturbations were run in total, each restored: two register totals, one spelled-out register total, two repointed citations, one register cross-reference and one citation of a nonexistent row. | The census tests read the filesystem, so they are sensitive to what a working tree contains; the citation census walk excludes `documentation/` and the generated directories by name, and a new top-level directory would have to be added to that list or the published figure moves for a reason that is not a defect. The frontend-result binding degrades to a no-op when `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml` is absent rather than skipping, so that a first pass from a fresh clone does not move the artifact-conditional skip figure `ARTIFACT_CONDITIONAL_SKIPS` publishes — which means that one leg is unexercised in a cold tree, and only there. `SEAM_PASS_AUTHORITIES` covers the six rows §47 opened and nothing else, so the class it closes is closed only for those rows. |
| D423 | **Answer the deck's runtime accessibility findings inside the deck's own markup and stylesheet, without touching a value Rule 4 constrains.** Four changes: `hideRenderedInternals()` sets `aria-hidden="true"` on every `<style>` Mermaid injects into a rendered diagram's svg, called from `settleRender` after every render; `role="main"` with `aria-label="Executive summary slides"` on the `.slides` div; an `aria-label` on each of the three previously unnamed tables; and `font-size: 13px` on `.reveal .controls`, in the deck's inline block and in the canonical theme alike. | (a) Give the diagrams `aria-hidden` wholesale and rely on the surrounding prose. (b) Remove the injected `<style>` from the DOM instead of hiding it, moving its rules into the deck's own stylesheet. (c) Put `role="main"` on a new wrapper around `.reveal`. (d) Use `<caption>`, which is the semantically native way to name a table. (e) Override `.controls-arrow`'s width and height directly rather than the cluster's font size. (f) Do nothing: the finding is MINOR and the deck is a leadership artifact rather than a product screen. | (a) throws away the diagram descriptions, which are the only textual form the six flowcharts have: each `<pre>` already carries `role="img"` and a written `aria-label`, and what buries it is the injected stylesheet — about 3,581 characters of CSS announced ahead of the label. (b) means owning Mermaid's internal class names, which no Mermaid version promises, in the one code path that draws every diagram in the deck; `aria-hidden` leaves the rendering byte-identical and changes only what is announced. (c) would put the landmark outside the subtree whose visibility is managed: reveal.js writes `aria-hidden` onto `.slides`'s children as it navigates, so the landmark belongs on `.slides` itself. (d) is native and still wrong here — `_visible_words()` in `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` strips tags before counting, so caption prose would count against Rule 4's forty-word cap on a content slide while an attribute value does not, and `aria-label` names the table just as completely. (e) fights reveal's own rules: every metric of the cluster — the arrow, its `::before`/`::after` triangles, the padding, the offset from the stage edge — is expressed in `em` against `.reveal .controls`, whose default `font-size` is 10px, so one declaration of 13px scales all of them together and takes the hit target from 36x36 to 46.8x46.8, where overriding width and height alone would leave the triangles and the spacing at the old scale. (f) is what leaves a finding open with no reason recorded, which is what §23 exists to prevent. | `hideRenderedInternals` runs on every settle rather than once, so it re-hides styles it has already hidden — free at six diagrams, and the alternative is tracking which have been treated, which is state for nothing. It hides what Mermaid injected at the moment it runs, so a version that injected a stylesheet after its own promise resolved would leave that one exposed, and only a browser check would show it. The 13px control size is a deliberate departure from reveal's default and from D370's measured halo geometry: that halo is a `drop-shadow` in absolute pixels, so it now sits proportionally tighter around a larger arrow. Rule 4 constrains no control metric, but a reviewer comparing against reveal's stock appearance will see a difference, and the comment on the rule is where they will find out why. |
| D424 | **Bring the canonical stylesheet's one divergent declaration into line with the deck rather than the other way round, restate F70 as enforced, and add a declaration-level parity check.** `blitzy-deck/references/blitzy-reveal-theme.css`'s `.deck-diagram-sm` moves from `margin: 20px auto 0` to `margin: 20px 0 0`. `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` F70 keeps its 23 and 118 figures, which were exactly right, adds the 119 rule blocks they resolve from, drops the claim that the parity is exact and unenforced, and states the displacement the divergence caused. `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` gains four cases over both stylesheets: identical custom-property names and values, identical selector-group sets in both directions, an identical declaration set inside every shared selector, and F70's census re-derived from the deck's own inline block. | (a) Change the deck to `20px auto 0`, which is the other way to make them agree. (b) Leave both and correct F70 to say the parity is inexact, naming the one difference. (c) Delete the reference copy, since nothing loads it. (d) Generate the reference copy from the deck. (e) Compare the two files byte-for-byte instead of declaration-for-declaration. | D373 decided `20px 0 0` and measured why: centring inside the 1560px content column pushed the two diagrams carrying that modifier 68px right of the x=146 column every other element on the slide aligns to. The deck is the file that was rendered and reviewed, so it is the correct side and the reference copy is the stale one — which makes (a) the adoption of a value a measurement had already rejected. (b) is honest and still leaves a consumer of the canonical file rendering a deck 68px out of alignment, when F70's whole claim is that loading this file alone renders the deck rather than a degraded copy of it. (c) discards a file Rule 4 names by path. (d) is the standing answer everywhere in this tree, which is that nothing here is generated, and it would also make the longer commentary F70 describes as a deliberate difference impossible to keep. (e) fails for the same reason: the two files legitimately differ in comments and in indentation, so the check strips comments, collapses whitespace, sorts each block's declarations and compares the three things that matter. The check is precisely what F70 admitted did not exist — "nothing enforces the parity" — and the divergence it found on its first run is the argument for it. | The check is a small CSS parser rather than a real one. It handles one level of at-rule nesting, which is all either file uses; a future nested at-rule moves the selector census and fails the test rather than being silently mis-parsed, which is the safe direction, but it does have to be extended. Because it sorts declarations, a genuinely order-dependent block — the same property declared twice, last one winning — would compare equal; neither file has one today. And the parity now cuts both ways: a deck-only rule can no longer be added without adding it to the reference copy as well, which is the intent and is also a second file to edit. |
| D425 | **Move the oversize-credential case's typing inside the suite's single `act` scope, by generalising the shared helper rather than adding a second wrapping site.** `fillCredentials(user)` becomes `fillCredentials(user, credentials: typeof CREDENTIALS = CREDENTIALS)`, and `frontend/src/components/Configuration.test.tsx`'s oversize case calls `await fillCredentials(user, { ...CREDENTIALS, apiKey: OVERSIZE_CREDENTIAL })` in place of four bare `await user.type(...)` calls. | (a) Wrap that case's four `user.type` calls in their own `act(async () => { ... })`. (b) Shorten `OVERSIZE_CREDENTIAL` from 500 characters until the warning count is tolerable. (c) Set the value with `fireEvent.change` instead of typing it. (d) Silence the warning — filter it in `src/test-utils/setup-jest.ts`, or set `IS_REACT_ACT_ENVIRONMENT` — and change no test. (e) Leave it: the suite is green and a warning is not a failure. | Measured rather than argued. That one case produced **510** `not wrapped in act` warnings and **3,424,724** of the full frontend run's 3,441,108 characters — 99.5% of the whole suite's output, for one of 383 cases. After the change the Configuration suite emits **0** warnings and **1,927** characters, and the full run **0** and **1,381**, at the same 16 of 16 and the same 383 total / 359 passed / 24 skipped. (a) works, and puts a second act-wrapping site in a file whose whole point is that the wrapping is a property of this dependency graph rather than of any one case, so the next case that types will get it wrong the same way. (b) trades the property under test for quiet: 500 characters exist to show that nothing truncates, and a shorter value shows less. (c) bypasses the `onChange` path a person's input takes, which the case's own comment says it must not, and would stop showing that React's controlled-value round trip preserves the value. (d) hides a real diagnostic from every future test in the suite, which inverts the finding — the harm of 510 warnings is that a genuine one is invisible among them, not that they are ugly. (e) is what carried a defect to a final-acceptance gate. | The helper now takes an optional second argument, so a caller could hand it an object missing a key and silently type `undefined` into a field. The parameter is typed `typeof CREDENTIALS`, so TypeScript refuses that — but `diagnostics: false` in the Jest transform (D32) means the refusal is a property of an editor and of `tsc`, not of the test run, so the guard is weaker here than the type suggests. Every assertion the case makes is unchanged and still passes: one call to the collaborator, the payload's `apiKey` 500 characters long and equal to the literal, and the input's own value equal to it. The case also runs 2.7 s faster, which is a side effect rather than an aim. And this file gained its first `D` citation, which moved the census D422 binds — the suite went red and the paragraph in §*How to read this log* was restated, which is the behaviour that row promised. |
| D426 | **State the whole of the onboarding gap in the README's additive backlog, and bind every claim in it to the filesystem rather than to prose.** Four entries replace the single stale-identity line: the identity entry now also names `frontend/package.json`'s `data-visualization-dashboard` and `frontend/public/index.html`'s `Personal Finance Tracker`, and says in as many words that nothing above line 101 has been edited; a second names the `CONTRIBUTING.md` line 89 links to and the `LICENSE` line 93 links to, neither of which exists at any path; a third names the placeholder origin lines 34 and 100 carry, measured live at **404** on its bare form, its `.git` form and its `/issues` path; a fourth names the `requirements.txt` line 46 installs from and the `.env.example` line 64 copies, neither of which exists at the root or under `backend/`. Two further entries carry the deck findings `./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.8 defers here. `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` gains eleven cases: every relative link in the eight documents this delivery owns resolves on disk (204 targets), the two declared exceptions are held to being **both** absent and recorded, the two absent setup files likewise, the placeholder origin has to appear in the backlog exactly when it appears in the body, and each deferred deck finding is asserted in both documents. | (a) Fix the links: add a `CONTRIBUTING.md` and a `LICENSE`, correct the URLs, add a `requirements.txt`. (b) One combined backlog entry rather than four. (c) Assert the 404s in a test. (d) Record the gap under `docs/testing/` only and leave the README as it was. (e) Nothing: the finding is MINOR and every item is pre-existing. | (a) is outside the write surface twice over. The README's first hundred lines are additive-only under AAP §0.10.5 C3, and a `LICENSE` is a statement about terms that nobody in this programme is authorized to make; a root `requirements.txt` would additionally stand up a second, competing dependency manifest beside `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, which is the one every gate in this tree measures. (b) merges four separately schedulable pieces of work whose owners differ — a licence is a legal decision, a repository URL is a one-line edit, and the setup commands are a rewrite. (c) is refused for the reason D421 refuses an OSV query in a test: the egress guard in `tests/conftest.py` exists to make a networked test fail, so a 404 assertion would either be skipped or would defeat the guard; the 404s are therefore stated as a measurement taken on a day, exactly as D421 states its advisory count. (d) leaves the reader who actually reaches line 46 with nothing. (e) is what carried the gap to a final-acceptance gate. The bindings matter more than the prose here: what made this findable at all was a link check, and what stops it recurring is that the same check now runs on every backend run. Two exclusions in the extractor are load-bearing rather than tidy — a fenced block in `frontend/TESTING.md` carries a Jest `transform` key whose bracket group is indistinguishable from link syntax, and it read as a broken link until fences were skipped. | The two dangling links are now declared as a constant in a test file, which is a place a reader of the README will not look; each is held to being absent **and** recorded, so adding either file turns the suite red rather than quietly green, but the failure names the constant rather than the entry it invalidates. The link check resolves paths on disk, so it catches a link that resolves to nothing and cannot catch one that resolves to the wrong file. It covers the eight documents this delivery owns; a link added to a ninth document is unchecked until that document is added to `LINKED_DOCUMENTS`. And the 404 measurement will age: the placeholder origin could be registered by anyone, at which point the entry's stated reading becomes wrong in the direction that matters. |

### §48.1 The findings this pass raised, and the clause that decides each

One row per finding, all 32 of them: **23 declined**, **7 resolved** by the rows above, **2** not defects of
this repository. The Clause column is the reason, not a category — a decline with no clause is what
D324 refused to allow. The last column is where the finding is already carried, so a reader scheduling
the work has somewhere to go that states the impact. `test_docs_contract.py` holds the register's shape:
every id from 01 to 32 present exactly once, every disposition one of the three, and no row without both
a clause and a pointer.

| Finding | Severity | What it reported | Disposition | Clause | Already carried by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| QA-01 | Critical | All three implemented HTTP operations have no authentication or authorization, and `GET /tweets/` discloses the whole collection to a request shaped like a single-record lookup | Declined | §0.8.2 (no login route, no token endpoint, no RBAC, no `verify_token`), §0.11.3 (touch #1 is bounded to what the single routes file already implements) | `./SECURITY-GAPS.md` rows 1 and 34; asserted by F51; D414 |
| QA-02 | Critical | `analytics_service` interpolates caller-supplied date strings and `BIGQUERY_DATASET` into both BigQuery queries by f-string | Declined | §0.8.2 (every production module but the two touches), and §0.11.4 already records it verbatim | `./SECURITY-GAPS.md` rows 2 and 29; asserted by F47 |
| QA-03 | Critical | Every principal journey has a contract mismatch: field names, the response path, and the base URL | Declined | §0.1.4 (do not implement missing product features, and the `api.ts` signatures and zod contracts are frozen), §0.8.2 | `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §A and §C; `./SECURITY-GAPS.md` row 37; D10, D15 |
| QA-04 | Critical | A successful fetch unmounts React on the dashboard, because `TweetCard` is imported and defined nowhere | Declined | §0.8.2 ("No `TweetCard` component"), and §0.7.3 records the branch as a ceiling rather than a gap | `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §G; asserted as current behaviour by F63; D156 |
| QA-05 | Critical | A successful fetch unmounts React on analytics, because `Chart.register` is never called | Declined | §0.8.2 ("no `Chart.register` call"), §0.7.3 | `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §G; asserted by F65; D92, and D-rejected `jest-canvas-mock` in §0.6.2 |
| QA-06 | Major | The real frontend cannot be built or linked under browser-native ESM | Declined | §0.8.2 (no `frontend/vite.config.ts`, no `frontend/index.html`, no `frontend/tsconfig.node.json`, no change to `tsconfig.json`) | §0.2.2 measured it; F32 to F34 — the harness config, its HTML entry and its own route table — exist only because of it |
| QA-07 | Major | A standalone import of the FastAPI application is blocked, and import-time code does credential work | Declined | §0.8.2, §0.11.3 (the two touches, and the fallback clause: test below the break and skip above it) | §0.11.4's four un-importable modules; the five conftest shims; F6 and F94 |

| Row | Decision | Alternatives | Why | Risks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D427 | **Decline every finding whose only repair is a production change, and record each one individually in a register rather than as its own decision row.** The register below carries all 32 findings the final-acceptance pass raised, one row each, with the severity, what was reported, the disposition, the clause that decides it and where it is already carried. 23 are declined, 7 are resolved by §48's other rows, and 2 are not defects of this repository. | (a) Fix them: the findings are real, and several are Critical. (b) One blanket sentence — "everything else is production code" — and no per-finding record. (c) A full decision row for each of the 23, with alternatives and risks. (d) Answer them in the review reply and leave the log alone. | (a) is not available: §0.11.3 authorizes exactly two production touches and §0.1.4 forbids implementing a missing product feature *even where an existing test asserts it*, which is the clause that decided the whole shape of this programme. Fixing any one of them would also invalidate the assertions that pin it as current behaviour, which is the point — the suite exists to make a future fix visible, not to pre-empt it. (b) is what D324 refused: a decline recorded only in aggregate is indistinguishable, later, from a finding nobody read, and a reader cannot tell which clause applies to which finding. (c) would add 23 rows whose Decision, Alternatives and Risks cells would be near-identical restatements of this one, which buries the four declines that *are* judgements (D428, D429, D430) among twenty that are mechanical. A table is the honest form for a mechanical application of a rule. (d) leaves the record in a place the repository does not contain. | A register is easier to skim than to read, and a reader who skims it will take "Declined" as "unimportant" when several of these are the most valuable work outstanding on this repository — the Clause column says why not, and the last column points at the register entry that states the impact, but neither is in the reader's eye line. The register also fixes the finding ids of one QA pass into this log permanently; a later pass numbering its findings the same way will collide unless it says which pass it means. And a row's "already carried by" pointer is a claim about another document, machine-checked only for its shape — that each row has a clause and a pointer — not for whether the pointer is apt. |
| D428 | **Decline the whole of the dependency and CDN advisory finding, and change no version.** Neither the 33 pip records across 10 distributions, nor the 9 npm advisories in `frontend` and 5 in `e2e`, nor the 9 against `mermaid@11.4.0` results in a version move. | (a) Upgrade what can be upgraded without breaking a pin the plan names. (b) Upgrade `mermaid` alone, since the deck is this delivery's own file. (c) Add npm `overrides` for the transitive half. (d) Add a `constraints.txt` that overrides the manifest at install time. | §0.6.1 does not merely list versions, it fixes each as an exact pin, and §0.9.1 fixes the Python 3.9 and Node 16.x ceilings that make most of the pins the newest admissible release in the first place — `pytest` cannot pass 8.4.2, `httpx` cannot reach 0.28 without breaking `TestClient` outright, and `msw` and `@playwright/test` are both at their Node 16 ceilings. So (a) resolves to almost nothing, and what it does resolve to would break a gate. (b) is the one that looks available and is not: Rule 4 and §0.10.4 pin `mermaid@11.4.0` **by version**, and the deck's four `integrity` digests are bound to those exact bytes, so a bump is a rule violation plus four digest changes. (c) and (d) both put the real resolved version somewhere a reader of the manifest cannot see it, which is what D166 rejected. What *was* in scope was the **documented** advisory count, and that was wrong: D421 re-derived starlette's from OSV and corrected five to seven. | The advisory count only grows, and nothing in this tree re-derives it, so every figure published about it is a reading on the day it was taken — stated on the register row itself rather than implied. Three of starlette's seven are unreachable in this application and four of the ten pip distributions are test-only, so the *count* overstates the exposure while the register's per-row impact does not; a reader who takes the count alone will schedule the wrong work. |
| D429 | **Decline both CI findings: change no lint or type-check step, and re-declare nothing about the runtime ceiling.** The `build` job's conclusion stays red on `flake8 .`, `mypy .` and `npm run lint`, and the declared Node 16.x ceiling stays declared although Node 22.23.1 is what exists on this host. | (a) Repair the three steps — declare the linters, and fix or configure away what they report. (b) Delete them, so the job's conclusion reflects the tests. (c) Mark them `continue-on-error: true`. (d) Raise the declared Node version in `ci.yml` to match the host. | §0.5.4 states that these are not test steps and are deliberately left untouched, and §0.10.5 C7 had already resolved the same tension: "CI green" is scoped to the backend test step, the frontend test step and the `e2e` job, because the lint and type-check steps are broken independently of testing — no linter is declared anywhere and the sources do not typecheck. (a) is a production change to every file `mypy` reports on. (b) and (c) both change the meaning of the job's conclusion, which is a governance decision rather than a test one, and (c) would hide a real regression in those steps if they were ever fixed. (d) is out of the authorized surface for a different reason: `ci.yml`'s **matrix** is not a test step, and the ceiling is what every version pin in §0.6.1 was chosen against, so moving it would invalidate the reasoning behind twenty-six pins in order to describe one host. | The job's conclusion therefore stays red on a tree whose three test suites are green, which is exactly the shape a reader mistakes for a broken delivery; `./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` and `../../README.md` both say which steps are which, but a status badge does not. And the declared ceiling now disagrees with the host every suite was actually validated on, which is recorded rather than resolved — a genuine Node 16 run remains unattempted, and nothing here proves the suites pass there. |
| D430 | **Record the two findings that are not defects of this repository as documented rather than declined.** The unavailable historical QA report artifacts, and the one-time 19-handle growth of a `TestClient` constructed outside a context manager. | (a) Call them declined, alongside the 23 that are. (b) Leave them out of the register entirely, since neither asks for a change. (c) Reproduce the earlier findings from the artifacts that do exist, to stand in for the missing reports. (d) Switch the integration fixtures to a context-managed client. | "Declined" carries an implication these do not deserve: a decline says a real defect is being left, and neither of these is a defect here. Nothing of this tree is missing — the earlier checkpoints' findings are carried forward in §23, in this log's per-checkpoint sections and in `./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.4 to §7.8 — and no artifact the write surface can produce would supply another agent's report. So (a) misstates them and (b) loses the record that they were considered, which is the whole reason §23 exists. (c) would manufacture a report and present it as one, which is the class of defect D324 and D321 both exist to prevent. (d) is the one that would be a real regression: §0.1.5 specifies the **non**-context construction deliberately, so that the startup handler never calls `get_db().connect()` or `start_tweet_stream()` — and an unpatched `start_tweet_stream()` blocked the interpreter to a 300-second timeout when that was measured. Trading a bounded one-time handle growth for a live Twitter connection in a test suite is not an improvement. | Neither is bound by a test, because neither states a property of this tree that a test could hold. The handle figure in particular is a measurement on one platform and one interpreter: it is quoted as one, and a reader who treats +19 as a constant will be wrong on another host. |
| D431 | **Repair the byte-level damage in this log, and bind the whole class, rather than leave a reader looking at bytes nobody can see.** Two defects, both found by a byte-integrity probe rather than by reading: 51 doubled carriage returns (a `\r` immediately before a `\r\n`) that this session's own section appenders left across §47 and §48, and one `0x08` byte, present since the row was first written, where the D136 row's supersession marker — the pattern half D169 superseded — means to say `base`, a Python `\b` escape having eaten the letter. The carriage returns render identically to a reader, but they made git classify the working file as `-text`, so a single changed line rendered as **1,575 added / 1,490 deleted**; after the repair the same edit renders as **95 / 10**, which is the change it actually is. `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py` gains nine cases, one per artifact this delivery authored plus the canonical stylesheet: valid UTF-8, no double-encoded em-dash, no forbidden control byte, no carriage return that does not end a line, and line endings uniform within the file. | (a) Leave the carriage returns — they render identically. (b) Normalise the whole tree with a `.gitattributes` `text=auto` entry. (c) Repair the `0x08` silently as a typo, with no row. (d) Leave the `0x08` alone, because this log freezes shipped rows as history. (e) Assert one line-ending convention rather than uniformity within a file. | (a) is the reasoning that let the damage happen: a diff nobody can read is a review nobody can do, and the two documents this session had to revert wholesale were lost to exactly this class of invisible re-encoding. (b) reaches outside the write surface and would restate the line endings of files this delivery does not own, which is a far larger diff than the one it fixes. (c) fails Rule 1: editing the text of a shipped row is contestable however small the edit, so it needs a recorded reason. (d) conflates two different things — the freeze protects **measurements**, so that a later reading cannot be back-dated into an earlier row; a control byte that ate a letter is not a measurement, and the same row's next sentence (“`base` rebuilt from the origin plus the wildcard capture”) says which letter it was. (e) would fail on the other platform: this tree checks out CRLF on Windows and LF elsewhere, so the only assertable property is that one file does not mix them. | The repair is asserted rather than trusted — the script refuses unless the byte delta is exactly the carriage returns removed plus the two backticks added, and unless no stray carriage return remains. The nine cases cover the artifacts a reader reads; a test file or a config file this delivery authored could still acquire the same damage and pass, and would be caught only by running the probe by hand as this one was. |
| D432 | **Continues D419.** **Restate every published suite-size figure for the fifth time, correct a per-suite case count that had been contradicted for a whole checkpoint, and bind all four of them to the running session.** This checkpoint's own bindings added 101 cases to `backend/tests/test_docs_contract.py`, which moves the backend from `1150` collected / `1147` passed to `1251` / `1248`, the first-pass reading from `1145` / `5` to `1246` / `5`, the root-misuse split from `21 failed, 1126 passed, 3 skipped` to `21 failed, 1227 passed, 3 skipped`, `junit.xml` from `tests="1150"` to `tests="1251"` with `1251` of `1251` distinct identities, the per-module census to `492 + 3 + 204 + 73 + 62 + 168 + 44 + 205 = 1251`, and the deck KPI from `1,537` to `1,638 = 1248 + 359 + 31`. Restated in `README.md`, `backend/tests/README.md`, `./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.1 and §7.4, `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` F89 and F102, this log and the deck. Separately, `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` F101 published `26 cases` for the same suite `backend/tests/README.md` published `44 passed` for, in the same tree — corrected to `44`. All four published per-suite counts, F76, F91, F101 and F102, are now read out of their own row and compared with what the running session collected for that file. | (a) Restate the figures and leave the per-suite counts unbound, as the four previous restatements did. (b) Bind them to `backend/reports/junit.xml` rather than to the session. (c) Leave F101 at `26`, since nothing had failed. (d) Derive every figure at build time. | (a) is precisely what left F101 wrong for a whole checkpoint and F102 wrong the moment a case was added: the collected and passing totals were bound, so a partial restatement of those failed loudly, and the per-suite counts were not, so nothing said a word. (b) fails closed in the wrong direction — that artifact is gitignored, absent in a fresh clone and overwritten by any filtered run, so the check would stand down exactly when a figure was most likely to be stale; the session is authoritative, always present and free. (c) would knowingly publish a wrong figure in the document whose whole purpose is to be the census. (d) is the standing answer and remains out of scope: no document in this tree is generated. | The session count cannot be taken when a run deselects within a file, so `-k`, `-m` and an explicit node id stand the check down, and a run that never collected a file stands that row down; a developer who only ever runs filtered slices gets no signal from it. Every figure in this row moves again the moment any layer gains a case — this is the fifth time — and the guard is only that the bindings now say so loudly rather than quietly. |
